#243. How long will humans be needed for translation?

Google's translation service handles one trillion words per month

"Il pleut des cordes," say the French when it's raining hard.

"It's raining ropes," is the direct translation. It's the kind of idiom which can trip up an AI translator.

"You can imagine the dilemma this throws AI into," says FranÃ§ois Yvon, a research director specialised in automatic translation with the French National Centre for Scientific Research CNRS.

He says a well-trained system will know that ropes are not literally falling from the sky and use the English phrase "it's raining cats and dogs".

"But is it going to find the equivalent when it's translating into Indonesian?" asks Yvon.

A comment on the weather is pretty low-risk, but more and more translation is being done by machines, and on topics with higher stakes than the weather.

In April, Google said people are now translating, external one trillion words a month with Google Translate.

That doesn't count the others: DeepL, Microsoft, Claude, ChatGPT and Gemini, not to mention made-to-measure corporate translation systems.

Direct sales of machine translation, external (i.e. outside general AI subscriptions) were worth an estimated $1.4bn (Â£1bn) last year.

AI translation is the "right call" in many cases says Jonathan Zimmermann

"For plenty of use cases, raw AI translation is now the right call: multiplying your costs by 100 to catch the last few percent of errors isn't rational if you're translating internal documents or product listings," says Jonathan Zimmermann, founder of OKA (Open Knowledge Association), a Swiss non-profit that translates Wikipedia articles into other languages.

"For an encyclopedia it's different. Wikipedia runs on trust. A wrong fact can sit there for years and get cited by journalists and AI systems," he says.

With more than 10,000 published pieces, mostly into Spanish, Portuguese and English, OKA is, Zimmerman says, the single largest contributor of translated articles to the collaborative online encyclopedia.

Which is something it absolutely could not have become without AI.

"Translating 10,000 articles the traditional way would cost millions. In our process, the AI produces the draft, then a human translator checks every sentence," he explains.

But that human input is required less and less.

"The machine output is now good enough that skipping the human entirely has become a serious option, and a real temptation across the industry. Two or three years ago, with tools like DeepL, our translators still rewrote a good chunk of every draft.

"Today the prose often doesn't need fixing at all. So, the question is no longer whether AI translation is good, it clearly is, but whether it's good enough to remove the human," says Zimmermann.

Ariella BaltiÃ© is a translator and legal expert specialised in translating court documents from English into French.

She was contacted recently by someone who'd already translated his documents with AI, asking her to check them.

"There were two major errors right at the beginning," she said.

The AI had translated "Le demandeur" (the plaintiff) as "the requester". "Totally awkward for legal papers," says BaltiÃ©.

When the AI saw "Madame X, Ã©pouse Y" (Ms X, wife of Y'), it came up with "Ms X marries Y".

In other words, it mistook a noun for a verb, completely losing the meaning of the phrase.

The consequences of mistranslation in the legal sphere can be serious. In the US, for example, errors in machine-translated asylum applications have led to delays in the release of detainees.

Ariella BaltiÃ© specialises in legal translation work

Accuracy is a matter of life and death in the medical field.

"The units for dosages and blood tests often include Greek letters," says Carol Bereuter, who is a specialist in medical translations.

She came across one automatically-translated text that had translated the greek letter Î¼ as milli (one thousandth) instead of micro (one millionth).

"That's a serious error. If you give someone a milligram when it's meant to be a microgram, you are giving them a thousand times the dose," points out Bereuter.

She also makes the point that, as AI translators have got better, catching errors has got more difficult.

"Whereas in the past, with things like Google Translate, the sentence would jump out at you because there'd be a grammatical error or it would sound clunky, today it sounds perfectly legitimate. It sounds fluid. It sounds natural. So the errors are a lot harder to spot."

Carol Bereuter is a specialist in medical translations

"If it's something that will bear your signature, there's a risk for your reputation. And if decisions are going to be taken on the basis of a translation concerning a medical procedure, a contract you might sign or a major purchase you might make, the risk is even greater," warns FranÃ§ois Yvon.

Even sentences that seem quite clear to a human can trip up an AI service, he says.

"When you have words with different meanings, it's usually very clear for a human which one applies because they understand the context, but when a machine translator comes across the sentence 'translate into Polish', for example, it is quite capable of deciding it must be all about shoe cream, says Yvon.

Context is often crucial. If an AI translator hasn't grasped that we're talking about a football match it is liable to mis-translate "taking a corner".

Last year OKA ran a study, funded by Wikimedia. It studied 119 articles, 10 language pairs and more than 1,000 hours of translator work.

"27% of the AI-generated text still got corrected before publication. Three quarters of those corrections were errors the AI introduced, and 5 to 6% of errors changed the meaning of the original text," says Zimmerman.

Eradicating those errors is likely to be challenging.

"As things stand, I think the fundamental way these systems function makes it quite hard to imagine how they could become 100% reliable," says FranÃ§ois Yvon.

This is because probability is a crucial part of the way AI translates each word rather than another and what is probably correct is not the same as what is correct. Especially in the realm of something as subtle and surprising as language.

He thinks a new generation of tech will be an improvement.

"We will see translation systems that are increasingly capable of evaluating what they do and correcting themselves using different AI tools simultaneously: one to do the translation, a second to criticize it and a third to produce a new version, for example."

But perhaps the big moment will be when AI can show doubt.

"The real breakthrough won't come until these systems are not merely capable of translating but also of telling us when they're not sure," Yvon says.

The critical tech staying safe by going underground

Why airlines are warning over lithium-ion batteries

#242. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."

#240. Third Point Q3 2026 Investor Letter

During the Third Quarter, Third Point returned -6.8% net in the flagship Offshore Fund.

Â¹ Net Returns through September 30, 2026.

Â² Annualized Return from inception (December 1996 for TP Offshore and quoted indices). See important disclaimers at the end of the letter.

PLEASE SEE THE NEW SERIES RETURNS AT THE END OF THIS DOCUMENT.Â²

The top five winners for the quarter were Atom Computing Inc. (a legacy private position), Warner Bros Discovery Inc (WBD)., Meta Platforms Inc (META)., Indra Sistemas SA (ISMAF), and Bausch Health Cos Inc (BHC). The top five losers for the quarter, excluding hedges, were SK Hynix Inc (SKHY)., Flex Ltd (FLEX)., MasTec Inc (MTZ)., CRH PLC (CRH), and TTM Technologies Inc (TTMI).Â³

Benign index performance masked significant turmoil in markets during the Third Quarter. The Morgan Stanley TMT Momentumâ´ factor recorded its largest drawdown since inception, falling more than 50% from its late-June peak to mid-August, while the broad momentum factor registered its highest volatility in 45 years. With two months left in the year, the Goldman Sachs High-Beta Momentumâµ basket has already recorded 39 distinct one-day moves of more than 5%. Outside of 2020, which had 41, that is more than any year since 1999.

The market's drawdown had many causes, among them renewed focus on AI capex and how it is being funded, the rise of open-source models, and concerns around growth slowing at frontier labs. Crowding and leverage fueled an explosive reaction during the depths of July and August, from the unwind at Situational Awareness to retail liquidation of leveraged memory-stock ETFs in Korea. Many of the names that drove our Q2 returnsâincluding SK Hynix, Flex, and MasTecâwere caught up in the rout.

Oil and rates also contributed. The June ceasefire with Iran collapsed in the first week of July, the Strait of Hormuz closed, and by mid-September the US blockade of Iranian ports and attacks on tankers had taken Brent from roughly $72 on July 1 to a peak of $109. Oil-fed inflation prompted higher rates: headline CPI re-accelerated to 3.7%, and new Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh raised the funds rate to 3.75â4.00%, its first hike since 2023.

Part of the move in rates is inflation, and part of it is inflation expectations: after five years of "temporary" supply shocks stacked end to end (COVID, Ukraine, tariffs, DRAM shortages, Iran), investors appear to be seeing high inflation as permanent. The less-discussed part is supply. Nation-sized companies are now competing with sovereigns for the same bond buyers to fund their AI capex plans, which is one of the reasons the 10-Year is above 5% although the economy is still growing. In Q3, anything sensitive to fuel, rates, or housing was sold with little discrimination, exchange hedging volumes hit records, and the divergence between indices sitting near their highs and the potential violence underneathâin factors, in breadth, in ratesâunderscored just how tricky this market has been to navigate.

We continue to see a tug of war play out. On one side: a strong earnings season (S&P 500 earnings tracking close to 30% growth for Q3, the third straight quarter above 25%), aggregate consumption of AI usage that is holding up, and what we still believe are the early innings of AI adoptionâwith the Muse launch and growth metrics disclosed in Anthropic's S-1 being the latest evidence that usage is compounding faster than the market expected. On the other side: the 10-Year hovering north of 5%, an unresolved situation in the Middle East, oil prices that remain high, and investors who are being a lot more circumspect about AI as a monolithic investment theme.

The AI debate has moved from whether demand is real to whether the capital can be funded and earn a return. The bear math is hard to dismiss; consensus has hyperscalers and frontier labs spending $5â7 trillion between 2025 and 2030. At a low teens hurdle and reasonable assumptions around asset depreciation, spenders need to generate $2-3 trillion of incremental annual AI revenue, a considerable sum in the context of $6 trillion in global IT spend today. The strain is now showing up in credit, not just equities: several data-center financings in the high yield and leveraged loan markets carry double-digit yields, top-rated data-center ABS that priced below 5% in January yields closer to 7% today, and CDS on Oracle (ORCL), Meta, and Nvidia (NVDA) hit record levels in late October. Some banks and insurers are approaching their exposure limits to the sector, and the financing the neoclouds still need increasingly depends on backstops from the same hyperscalers and chipmakers that already carry the largest capital needs of all, so credit investors are starting to price AI risk back onto balance sheets the market had assumed were insulated.

While the quantum of capital being spent seems hard to justify, there are several reasons to believe that total projected spending is overstated and buildout costs will be significant but more rational and stretched out over more years. First, there are tangible signs that AI is addressing markets far larger than just global IT spend, potentially as large as the $60 trillion knowledge worker TAM. Second, the capacity being built today is quickly being consumed: the revenues at the largest public cloud platforms are accelerating to rates not seen in many years and their backlogs are growing at triple-digit rates. The two leading frontier labs are, in aggregate, generating well over $100 billion in run-rate revenue and still growing at rates unprecedented at this scale. Lastly, economics on infrastructure also appear sound: according to the hyperscalers who are financing the majority of the spend, AI servers and racks are bought months ahead of contracted demand and pay back in under three years. We still think the world is structurally short the building blocks of compute and power, but with open-source models now within six months of the frontier and capital markets signaling that the next several trillion dollars of buildout will not be financed on the terms the first several hundred billion were, our view is that who captures the economics matters a lot more than it did in June. Investors are no longer underwriting AI revenue; they are underwriting who pays for it.

Our base case on the Middle East is a muddle-through stalemate in which oil stays elevated and the economy slows without breaking. The tail risk is real damage to Gulf infrastructure, which appears to be something neither side has an incentive to cause, while the underappreciated path is a deal after November 3. We believe Iran's leverage is eroding, the administration wants the war over, and China wants cheaper oil. More broadly, the midterms look like a clearing event for political uncertainty. In the meantime, anxious investors are selling the small, the cyclical, and the rate-sensitive, while strategic buyers keep paying takeover premiums for the same assets.

In light of these concerns, in a market that depends on the successful IPOs and continued growth of two primary frontier model companies, we can see a wide range of scenarios playing out from here from optimistic to apocalyptic. While we don't believe that the current data center build out shares the economic deficiencies of the infrastructure buildout during the dotcom bubble, we see signs of excess all around us, particularly the frenzy in private markets like so-called "seed" rounds for companies that are pre-revenue and pre-product at $1 billion plus "unicorn" valuations. Similarly, frenzies for allocation to anointed so-called "growth-stage" companies and a proliferation of amiable but financially unsophisticated individuals who we've seen selling access to private deals via SPVs that charge outlandish fees.

This does not take away from the fact that some extraordinary companies are being built and that AI technology will forever change the way we conduct business, even our own. We believe that this is a good time to pause and take account of who we expect the winners and losers will be. We are also of the view that such uncertainty will inevitably lead to lower multiples given the fragility of markets, as evidenced last week where companies from Service Now (NOW), Intel (INTC) and Reddit (RDDT) could lose a third of their market capitalization in a single day.

All of this calls for significantly lower gross and net exposures and a more selective approach, and that is where we have taken the portfolio. We have cut our semiconductor and data-center-adjacent positions, and as well as the hyperscalers, which we had earlier added to based on our assessment of their strong business models backed by recurring revenue, and reasonable valuations. We were broadening the portfolio already mid-year, seeking more balance, and that continued through the quarter with positions in financials, live events, industrials, commercial aerospace, and consumer-facing companies that we feel are equipped to navigate an air pocket or are actively using it to gain market share.

On the short side, our gross equity short exposure (~21% of NAV) is entirely single-name, and we think dispersion will continue to present opportunities here. Current themes include GLP-1 losers, cable, P&C insurance, and housing, to name a few. We reduced short exposure to AI disrupteesâsoftware, information services, IT servicesâbecause those shorts were amplifying our AI longs and creating basis risk rather than hedging it. We had some success shorting "AI overearners, " but it is difficult to press those given the dramatic recent de-rating.

With interest rates where they are and a roughly 20x forward multiple underpinned by 30% earnings growth, the index is not where we want our risk. We expect that the opportunity lies underneath it: in the dispersion between the companies that will actually earn a return on the AI buildout and those that are merely levered to it, and in the parts of the market that were sold for reasons that had nothing to do with their fundamentals. Having expressed such caution before just prior to market rallies, we are being equally cautious on the short side as we are on the longs. We are willing to miss the early stages of a market rally or a rotation back into tech in order to preserve capital, exercise patience, and wait for investments that better fit our event-driven framework. Our suspicion is that we could be getting close to an environment like we had in March 2020, or towards the end of the GFC, where the next wave of opportunities will come from credit markets.

Atom Computing was our top winner during the Third Quarter. We first invested in late 2021, when we led the Series B financing and joined Atom's Board of Directors. Based on Atom's technical progress and commercial traction, we subsequently led the Series C which closed in early 2026. Atom Computing is led by its CEO Benjamin Bloom who founded the company in 2018 to pursue the Neutral Atom Modality for Quantum Computing. Ben holds a bachelor's degree from MIT and PhD in physics from the University of Colorado-Boulder where he helped develop one of the most accurate atomic clocks ever constructed. Of the seven primary approaches being pursued to build practical, scalable, fault tolerant Quantum Computers, Atom employs the Neutral Atom modality. Our research led us to Atom Computing as we determined that the Neutral Atom approach's combination of practical scalable design, high quality qubits, noise immunity, and qubit longevity made it one of the most likely to achieve the goals desired by governments, enterprises, and research facilities. Since our initial investment in 2021, we have partnered very closely with the company, been supportive of their organizational development, and have been pleased with their achievements of milestones (from 100's of qubits in 2021, to 1200 qubits, to their expected announcement of the 10,000 qubit system shortly).

Atom Computing raised a Series D-1 financing (at a stepped-up valuation) in July 2026 based on their technical and commercial progress as well as the announcement (May 2026) of Atom being selected to receive a $100 million investment by the US Department of Commerce under the CHIPS Act. Last month, Atom Computing was selected as one of four companies announced for DARPA's QBI (Quantum Business Initiative) Stage C awards. The four were Atom (private), Diraq (private), IBM (IBM) (public), and IonQ (IONQ) (public). Note that while Atom was one of four to receive DARPA-C, it is one of the two that are private companies, and the only company based on the Neutral Atom Modality thus far awarded. DARPA-C awarded Atom Computing potential non-dilutive funding up to $300 million (contingent on hitting specific development milestones over the next 36 months). The QBI program is designed to rigorously verify and validate whether any quantum computing approach can achieve utility-scale operation by the year 2033 (with near-term deliverables sooner). The recent markup was primarily driven by news of a major sector investor for its new Series D-2 financing, which will close in the coming weeks at a meaningfully higher valuation than the Series D-1 round (and over 28x the valuation where we originally entered the investment in 2021).

APi Group provides fire protection, electronic security, elevator, and specialty infrastructure services. The business is built around inspecting and maintaining systems that customers need to keep buildings safe and operating. More than half of revenue comes from recurring inspection, service, and monitoring, much of it driven by regulatory requirements. We believe APi has the ingredients of an attractive long-term compounder: resilient demand, opportunities to improve margins, and a fragmented market in which to reinvest its cash flow.

Most of the value sits within Safety Services, which accounts for roughly three-quarters of segment profits. Fire protection is the largest business, and APi holds the leading position in North America. The work is local, relatively small-ticket, and recurring. APi can inspect and service equipment from any manufacturer, allowing it to manage a customer's needs across different systems and locations. Its scale supports purchasing and service capabilities, while local branches retain the customer relationships.

APi's inspection-first strategy is what makes the economics interesting. Every dollar of inspection revenue generates an additional $3â4 of annual service work and gives APi a repeatable way to deepen customer relationships and grow without relying solely on new construction. North American inspection revenue has grown at a high single digit rate or better for 25 consecutive quarters.

Elevator maintenance is a recently added business platform with similar characteristics: required inspections, recurring service, a fragmented provider base, and aging equipment that needs ongoing attention. It also creates opportunities to sell additional services to existing building customers. Specialty Services adds exposure to infrastructure and industrial investment, including data centers through fiber, HVAC, and fabrication work. These projects provide additional growth and can establish relationships that lead to future service revenue.

Acquisitions give APi another way to expand this model. Management estimates an addressable market of approximately $160 billion, much of it served by smaller operators. APi has completed nearly 150 bolt-on acquisitions over two decades, historically paying approximately 6-8x EBITDA for these businesses. We think today's opportunity is to improve purchasing, expand service offerings, and bring greater operating discipline to an established local franchise. Chubb Fire & Security demonstrates the potential on a larger scale. Its EBITDA margins increased from below 10% at acquisition in 2022 to approximately 15% today, bringing the effective acquisition multiple down to high single digits.

APi's margins and recurring revenue mix have steadily marched upward. Management's 2028 objectives include an adjusted EBITDA margin of at least 16% and more than 60% of revenue from inspection, service, and monitoring. The company's cash generation provides capacity to keep investing: pro forma net leverage stood at 2.4x as of 3Q, below management's 2.5-3x target range despite substantial first-half acquisition spending. We see a multi-year runway for double-digit earnings per share growth, and the stock trades ~18x our FY28 EPS.

The corporate credit book gave back its strong second-quarter performance in the Third Quarter. A handful of positions contributed positively, with shorts in telecom, building products, and healthcare driving gains. On the long side, Bausch Health performed strongly on continued solid operating results and a favorable patent ruling. Our largest individual detractor was the GSE preferreds. At current levels, the market appears to be pricing in only a 15% probability of an IPO, which strikes us as too low.

AI infrastructure positions were the largest detractors from performance. We continue to see rapid AI adoption and seemingly insatiable demand for compute, but sentiment toward the sector has deteriorated sharply for several reasons.

First, concerns about eventual overbuilding are mounting. Metaâs announcement late in the Second Quarter that it might sell excess compute capacityâif it ultimately had anyâwas interpreted by the market as a signal that a large, well-capitalized competitor could resell significant compute supply. In July, SpaceX guided year-end 2027 compute capacity âcloser to 10 GW than 5, â which was initially met with skepticism. However, Elon continues to reiterate this target and recently provided more details on the build-out during the 3Q earnings call.

Second, opposition to data centers has intensified. Demand for compute is strong, but local communities increasingly resist hosting the infrastructure required to support it. This issue has found traction on both the left and right, and, as elections approach, political rhetoric has grown more hostile. The market is increasingly concerned that new state and local leadership will seek to impede data center development. Ironically, restrictions on new construction would increase the value of the completed powered shells, a distinction the market has so far largely overlooked.

Third, an unparalleled wave of investment-grade and high-yield issuance to finance AI infrastructure is creating significant technical pressure on credit markets, particularly against a backdrop of rising interest rates. Goldman Sachs expects hyperscaler debt issuance will increase ~60% YoY in 2027 and eclipse $400 billion. More impactful will be the ~$300 billion of expected global project finance issuance, up 2-2.5x YoY, to fund powered shell construction and chip purchases. Many of these deals will require guarantees or credit support from IG counterparties.

AI infrastructure spending as a percentage of GDP is now approaching levels last seen during the railroad boom. While some of this capital is funding model training, a substantial portion is going toward the construction of compute infrastructure. The resulting investment boom has driven an extraordinary increase in debt issuance, weighing on credit valuations. We are already seeing the issuance tsunami pressure spreads with hyperscaler 10-year spreads +50bps YTD and HY Project finance bonds ~250bps wider since the tights in June.

Basic economics suggests that this demand for capital will ultimately be constrained by higher financing costs or declining returns on investment as capacity catches up with demand. Paradoxically, the exceptionally attractive returns currently available on some of these investmentsâincluding leases with investment-grade counterparties that generate ROIC in the teensâmay be accompanied by further declines in the prices of the securities financing them. As long as the economics of new investment remain compelling, we consider it likely that issuers will continue to access the capital markets. The marginal buyer, not the average economics of the underlying investment, ultimately sets the price of the securities being issued.

Over time, in a frictionless commodity market, the cost of capital would converge with the return on invested capital. We believe, however, that meaningful differences in execution and jurisdiction will ultimately drive greater dispersion in performance across these issuers. For now, the market largely treats them as interchangeable. This is creating mispricing in individual securities, but we believe the near-term path of least resistance remains lower. Investors are effectively treating some of these issuers as though their securities were âdeath spiral preferreds, â subject to perpetual dilution as additional capital is raised.

The irony is that the underlying assets may ultimately prove enormously valuable. When capital markets eventually close to these issuers or the cost of financing becomes prohibitive, we expect compelling opportunities to emerge for investors willing to distinguish between the winners and losers.

Amara's Law states that we tend to overestimate the impact of a technology in the short run and underestimate it in the long run. We believe the AI trade is transitioning from the former to the latter: investors are beginning to underestimate AI's long-term potential after initially overestimating its near-term impact.

Numerous anecdotal examples demonstrate meaningful productivity gains from AI, yet the evidence remains less apparent in aggregate economic data, lending some credence to the bubble thesis. We remain confident that demand for compute will continue to grow, although the ultimate distribution of that demand between open-source models and proprietary large language models remains uncertain. Regardless of which models prevail, the need for compute infrastructure should persist, and we believe data centers will benefit.

We believe hyperscalers (and certain related project finance structures) currently offer the most attractive way to maintain exposure to the theme. We do not expect issuance fears to materially change their cost of capital: the incremental financing needs are relatively modest compared with their balance sheets and cash flow generation. Hyperscaler access to relatively inexpensive capital provides a significant advantage as the market works through the current wave of infrastructure investment and financing.

We had hedged our credit positions in infrastructure with short equity positions and have since unwound significantly all of this exposure and continue to find attractive relative value shorts in BB-rated data center bonds. The technical as well as range of fundamental outcomes is creating more dispersion in AI infrastructure credit.

During the quarter we made a convertible loan investment in Nscale, a neocloud. Although volatility and uncertainty remain elevated in the sector as investors weigh the aforementioned concerns about overall AI demand and financing needs, it has also created what we believe are attractive investment opportunities. The Nscale loan has the downside protection of debt (at an attractive yield to maturity relative to public comps) and provides equity upside in our base case in what we see as an improving idiosyncratic outlook.

Execution, access to power, and cost of capital will separate winners from losers in this sector in our opinion. Nscale recently hired an experienced data center planning and construction team, and we expect this new group to support delivery of its pipeline as forecast. The projects largely consist of vertically integrated data centers where Nscale plans to own the powered shell and the GPUs, which should drive higher margins and extend the duration of cash flows. With respect to access to capital, in our assessment, the balance sheet is stronger than peers given limited parent-level unsecured debt and Nvidia (a significant equity owner) has committed to provide guarantees that should help Nscale finance compute projects at a reasonable rate.

We believe our instrument is structured with attractive asymmetry and the conversion into equity will be struck at a discount to the level at which Nscale goes public. As their cost of capital goes higher due to the sector massively de-rating, our potential return increases since we are buying in at a lower valuation.

Across credit markets in Q3 2026, the rate selloff and start of credit spread widening introduced volatility after a relatively benign first half of the year. Our structured credit performance was essentially flat over the quarter, with trading gains in shorter duration consumer ABS and the monetization of segments of our residential mortgage exposure counteracting the move in rates. While the rate selloff extends mortgage duration in our portfolio, our current securitizations have a cost of financing close to current Treasury rates enabling a steady current cash yield to our exposure.

As we see volatility increase across credit, we are constructive on the following sub-asset classes through year-end:

1) Residential mortgage securities: When rates first sold off with a 60bps move in September alone, 30-year fixed mortgage rates moved north of 7%. Non-bank mortgage originators had existing inventory on their balance sheets that were immediately subject to pricing pressure as the originated coupons were too low and their ability to generate mortgage volume going forward is challenged as the demand for mortgages at these rates is much lower. While we expected these originators to sell loans at a discount, many of the portfolios did not trade and we have seen the new issue supply of residential mortgage securitizations grow, pushing spreads wider on already higher base rates. We purchased BBB exposure at an 8.5% yield that was at a high 6% yield a few weeks earlier. Over the next six weeks, we believe more opportunities may emerge to add new issue and secondary exposure north of 10%.

2) Leveraged loans and CLOs: While the FOMC hike meant SOFR would be at an elevated level for longer, the cost of debt servicing for leveraged loans became more expensive to sustain for longer. Within CLO portfolios, leveraged loans first rallied despite growing concerns around LME maturity walls in 2027 and 2028. We are now seeing widening in leveraged loan spreads and opportunities for wider trading CLO tranches and potential repricings

3) Consumer ABS: Within this segment, the subprime cohort and certain vintages in specific consumer asset classes are under pressure. We have seen impairment in the student loan market where 2023-2025 vintage securitizations are likely to take actual credit losses while subprime auto loan delinquencies continue to rise. We expect the current market opportunity set to provide liquidity in the next few weeks given broader volatility, but we anticipate a more pronounced credit cycle in the coming year as higher rates and headwinds for the consumer create corrections in the credit markets.

#239. As Christmas rush looms, how do China-made toys find their way to the US?

China is the world's largest toymaker, producing the majority of toys on sale in the US

On the underside of most children's toys on sale in the US you'll find the same three words â "Made in China".

Nearly eight in 10 toys (78.3%) imported into the country in 2024 came from China, according to US government figures. , external

Last year, toys imported from China were worth $9.8bn (Â£7.4bn), trade figures show. , external

With Christmas on the horizon, the toy industry in the US and elsewhere is about to have its busiest time of the year by far.

As retailers prepare their stocks for the festive rush, be they online giants or "mom-and-pop" physical stores, the BBC World Service's Follow the Money series traces how a toy gets from a factory in China to the US. And where the money goes.

Guangdong province, which neighbours Hong Kong in southern China, is the heart of the country's toy manufacturing industry.

In the city of Dongguan workers on the floor of the Mihox factory pop plastic toys out of steel moulds, and pile them into crates ready for painting.

The company says it employs more than 800 people and makes around 55 million toys a year for the American market, which represents half of its business.

Follow the money: The journey of a $30 toy from China to America

Every toy starts with a design from a customer, such as an American toy company. The factory then makes a prototype, and builds moulds from which plastic parts and other components are formed.

Going from the first idea to finished toy takes one to two years, says Melanie Wang from the Mihox sales team. Much of that is the customer going back to the drawing board. "They modified the design again, again, and again to improve it."

Once the design is signed off, things move more quickly. A mould takes about a month to make, and the production run lasts about three months.

The Mihox factory in Dongguan employs more than 800 people

So, how much of the US retail price does the factory get? "About 20%," says Wang.

On a $30 toy, that's $6, which has to cover the price of the plastic and other materials, production costs including wages, and what profit the factory makes.

Chinese factories typically make a profit of between 3% and 10%, according to Jay Foreman, chief executive of US toy firm Basic Fun. So for a $30 toy that's between 60 and 18 cents of the $6 the factory charges to make it.

Samson Ko is president of the Toys Manufacturers' Association of Hong Kong. Many of his members moved their factories across the border into mainland China in the 1980s, attracted by cheaper land and labour.

He says the way toys are made has barely changed since then. "Automation now takes place mostly in the moulding and the packaging process. But for soft toys, or even some of the electronics nowadays, it's still very labour intensive."

And Chinese labour isn't cheap anymore, he adds, after recent rises.

Melanie Wang explains that Mihox gets 20% of a toy's retail price

Average pay in Vietnam is about half of that in China, said one report, external at the start of this year.

Yet fewer than 10% of Ko's members have moved any production outside China, and in those cases mostly only because customers asked them to do so.

The Chinese factories remain busy, he says, because a single toy can undergo more than one manufacturing process, from moulding the parts, to adding electronics, sewing materials, and final assembly. This often requires it travelling to different factories.

Ko adds that in Guangdong these separate facilities are located close to each other, and used to "working in harmony". He says this makes the whole manufacturing process run more smoothly than in other countries.

From the factory, the toy goes by truck to a port, such as Shenzhen, the biggest in Guangdong.

Ivy Tse is co-founder of FreightAmigo, a Hong Kong logistics company that runs an online platform for both Hong Kong and mainland China exporters. These businesses can book a cargo container, sea passage to an overseas destination, arrange insurance cover, and secure finance.

Tse says it takes about 15 days for a cargo ship to get from Shenzhen to the US west coast, and 25 to 30 days to the east coast. This year's Christmas toys left China in August and September.

Freight, from factory to the US, usually adds about 10% to the manufacturing cost of a toy, adds Tse. So if we stick to a toy that retails for $30 in the US, and therefore costs $6 to make in China, the cost of getting it to the states is 60 cents.

Once the toy reaches the US there is currently a tariff on Chinese toys that has to be paid to the US Customs and Border Protection (CBP).

Following talks between President Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping last month, the two governments have recommended toys for lower tariffs. But no new rate or start date has been set, and the US must first hold a public consultation. For now the basic rate is 12.5%, and some toys pay more.

This is charged on the manufacturing price of the toy, not on how much it retails for. So for a $30 toy, that basic rate is 12.5% of $6, which is 75 cents.

This tariff is paid for by the American company that has imported the toys. One such firm is Learning Resources, a family business based near Chicago that sells educational toys. Half of these it imports from China.

"We pay 100% of the tariffs, let there be no doubt about that," says the company's chief executive Rick Woldenberg.

Jay Foreman says that most of the money spent on a toy in the US doesn't leave the country

The cost of making the $30 toy, getting it to the US, and paying the import tariff, comes to $7.35. So that leaves $22.65, or 75.5% of the total.

That money never leaves the US, explains Foreman from Basic Fun. His firm, which is based in Florida, makes Tonka trucks and Care Bears cuddly toys.

"It's kept by my company, the [US] transportation company, the retailer," he says.

If we allow $2.65 for the US delivery firm, the toy company and the retailer will typically share the remaining revenues 50-50. So that would give them $10 each.

While profit margins are commercially sensitive and therefore not generally revealed, industry studies show that the toy firm and retailer would usually make a profit of up to 25% or $2.50.

Getting a toy onto the shelves in the US requires both a lot of work and a long journey.

#237. Living with a stink? Technology might help

The operators of Newby Island landfill have been ordered to take action over odours

Milpitas, an affluent city in California's Silicon Valley tech hub, has an odour problem.

For decades, residents have had to endure rank smells wafting from industrial sites, among them a 342-acre landfill.

"When I first moved here, I could not open my window for over 10 years," says Chia-Ling Kong, an engineer for a tech firm who lives around half a mile from the dump.

While things have got better in recent years, Kong says she and her neighbours must still contend with around "two or three hours of bad smells a day".

Perhaps befitting Silicon Valley, the community is putting its hopes in technology.

The operators of Newby Island landfill have been ordered by local authorities to use drones and install monitors to systematically track and measure the chemicals responsible for bad odours. They have also been told to automate the collection of landfill gases responsible for smells.

Kong is optimistic a data-driven approach will make a difference. "Hopefully with this continuous monitoring, it makes it easier to pinpoint problems at the moment it starts."

The operators of the landfill say they are committed to operating it safely and protecting air quality.

Just like in Milpitas, where Kong is part of a citizen's campaign against the odours, communities around the world are raising a stink about bad smells making day-to-day life miserable. Sometimes a rubbish dump is to blame, or it might be a sewage treatment works, or a reeking factory.

When the complaints start stacking up, resolving them is not always simple. Many countries have no regulations in place dictating how to manage smells.

In the US, for example, the Environmental Protection Agency does not regulate environmental odours as a pollutant. This leaves states and local governments to take charge of the issue.

Urban sprawl is often part of the problem, bringing communities closer to stinky facilities that may historically have been kept at a distance.

This is creating more demand for technologies to monitor and prevent smells. Odour consultant Micheal Bull says people are increasingly concerned about neighbourhood stinks.

"You're getting developments being built nearer to odorous processesâ¦ as land has become more valuable, there is also infilling," Bull says. He is speaking about his experiences in the UK, but it's a similar story in many parts of the world.

Bad smells from waste sites are among the most difficult to resolve

The technologies used to tackle industrial pongs generally fall into three categories: they absorb odours; convert them into something less smelly through a chemical reaction; or burn them away.

Experts on fixing industrial odours say that the best technologies, when used correctly, can remove 90% or more of the target compounds responsible for smells.

But this can be expensive and some operators opt for simpler and cheaper solutions. These include masking odours from landfill or sewage processing by spraying giant quantities of deodorisers.

Odour experts contacted for this article were dubious about the effectiveness of such industrial scale air fresheners. "In my view they're not likely to change the odours," says Bull, who likens them to fabric conditioner.

Bad smells from waste sites â a common cause of complaints â are among the stinkiest and most difficult to resolve, according to Carlos Diaz, an environmental consultant based in Spain. "Landfill is difficult to treat because you have a very large surface," he says.

In some places at least, technology is making a real difference. Residents in Des Moines, Iowa, have long complained of the so-called Eastside Funk, a putrid scent that residents report drifts over parts of the city.

A study commissioned by city authorities showed that a trio of industrial meat companies were among culprits.

"Within 10 miles, there's about half a million people," says Dalton Jacobus, who works in the Department of Neighborhood Services. "If the winds are out of the wrong direction, 100,000 people can be in the middle of that odour plume."

The authorities are now paying around $240,000 (Â£182,000) a year on technology to track the stink, so they can take action. "What we wanted to do was build a ring of odour monitors around the facilities that we suspected of emitting odours," says Jacobus.

The monitors, which measure levels of chemical compounds in the air, work in combination with a weather station to provide real-time data on the concentrations that are coming from each facility based off the wind patterns.

This has put an end to finger pointing about who's to blame each time a smell sparks complaints, says Jacobus. "Now we can say with reasonable certainty that 'this odour event did originate from your facility, what was going on'?"

In Des Moines, authorities have set up "electronic noses" to pinpoint bad smells

Meanwhile, some of the sites that have been blamed for causing unpleasant smells in Des Moines have spent millions of dollars to upgrade their own odour control systems.

Smithfield Foods, which operates a meat processing in Des Moines, says it has invested $2m to install a regenerative thermal oxidiser that destroys the chemicals associated with bad smells. Even so, the company says it does not believe it is the source of odour complaints.

Before installing the monitors â which some local companies help pay for â officials in Des Moines would drive out to the source of a complaint to sniff the air.

Relying on the human nose to sniff out industrial smells is still commonplace. Claudia Barrios ObregÃ³n from Kunak, the company behind the monitors in Des Moines, says: "Right now, the way that cities control or measure odours is olfactometry: a human panel measures and gives an opinion of what the odour is like. But that's not that realistic."

The Spanish company's monitors are an example of so-called electronic noses that make it easier to detect smells.

Whatever technology is used, it can be hard to keep everyone happy, as smells are so subjective. "Unfortunately, the general rule is that the nastier we perceive the odour to be, the more sensitive towards it we are," says Johan LundstrÃ¶m, a professor in the science of smell at the Karolinska Institute in Stockholm, Sweden.

#233. The tech helping crack decades-old cold cases

Crime scene evidence is often not enough to identify suspects

Randy Gail Sperino was last seen alive on the evening of 9 November 1993, as she was getting into a pickup truck in Granite City, Illinois.

The 34-year-old's body was found hours later in a field nearby, the victim of blunt force trauma.

The identity of her killer was a mystery. DNA evidence found at the crime scene was tested against standard law enforcement databases, but no matches were found.

For three decades, investigators from the Madison County Sheriff's Office chased leads and interviewed hundreds of people as they attempted to identify the perpetrator.

The case looked like it would remain one of more than 250,000 unsolved homicides in the US.

Then, last year, detectives tried a fresh approach. They sent genetic evidence to Othram, a Houston-based company that uses DNA technology to help identify the perpetrators in cold and current crime cases.

The Madison County detectives had been inspired by investigative genetic genealogy techniques used to identify the Golden State Killer, Joseph James DeAngelo, back in 2018. In that case, an investigative team constructed a family tree to help identify the killer.

They were able to do so thanks to DNA data that relatives had provided to commercial databases, similar to consumer ancestry platforms.

Unlike traditional forensic DNA testing, which generally relies on a direct comparison between evidence and a known person, investigative genetic genealogy can identify distant genetic relatives of an unknown individual.

Othram set about applying this technique to track down Sperino's killer. While the suspect's own DNA profile wasn't available, profiles submitted by his extended relatives allowed the company to identify a male family member.

"That lead broke the case open," said Captain Tim Lawrence of Madison County Sheriff's Office, which was responsible for investigating the crime.

It was a crucial clue that led the detectives to Albert "Buddy" Zigler, 70, who police say then confessed to the murder. He was charged in May this year.

Wes Sperino, Gail's son, said at a press conference: "I finally get closure and I finally know who did this."

Albert Zigler was charged with murder in May

The technique that led detectives to Zigler is credited with revolutionising how forensic cases are solved.

It has been highly successful in helping solve cases involving unidentified human remains and identifying suspects in longstanding investigations.

Othram claims it has helped solve thousands of cases. The company's chief executive David Mittelman likens investigative genetic genealogy to a GPS system: "The DNA profile is the GPS signal and it says 'you belong here.' But to understand where 'here' is, you need a map."

Public records, obituaries, and census data build that map so Othram's scientists can establish how different family trees connect.

However, Mittelman is quick to point out that DNA technology can only go so far before "a cascade of investigative steps" are needed.

An Othram scientist prepares a bone for DNA extraction

DNA science isn't the only technology that is helping detectives solve cold cases.

One of the challenges facing detectives on major investigations like the hunt for Sperino's attacker is the sheer amount of evidence they dig up.

Officers investigating her murder realised AI could help them wade through the mountains of evidence they had accumulated since 1993.

They used a platform called Centerline AI, from Florida software company CentralSquare, to analyse more than 2,000 pages of records and identify evidence for the DNA testing that helped to break the case.

The AI helped them reconstruct timelines and organise witness information. "It's so much easier than having a detective have to read through 2,000 pages," Lawrence says. His team could interact with the AI like a chat bot.

This also helped when it came to interviewing Zigler after they had tracked him down. Detectives were able to ask questions of the AI to help "figure out if Zigler was being truthful", says Lawrence.

But the technology is "only as good as the investigator," says the captain. There comes a point, he says, "where you leave the technology behind and you have to go track people down and talk to them."

Investigative genetic genealogy has its limitations too. Mittelman says: "It can't solve every case. If you have no DNA, you can't really use this technique."

"But this technique is a lot more powerful and a lot more likely to yield identities than having to rely on the chance that you find a match somewhere in a database," he adds.

While firms like Othram only use ancestry platforms where users have provided consent for law enforcement matching, there remain some concerns about the ethical implications of using this data.

A lack of funding is another hurdle. Mittelman says: "Every law enforcement agency wants to do it. There's just not a lot of funding for it."

Sometimes cases are funded through donations or crowdfunding. Othram operates a website listing cases that members of the public can help fund.

While investigative genetic genealogy has been adopted in countries such as Sweden, Denmark and Australia, it is not yet used in the UK. The Home Office is conducting a study to consider whether it should be adopted in the UK but did not respond to a request for comment.

The UK is already in a strong position, says Syndercombe Court. "We have such an efficient DNA database that if you can get any DNA out of a crime scene, you've got over a 65% chance of putting a name to that person, which is enormously powerful," she says.

#230. Rivian CEO says new R2 SUV, autonomy make 2027 an ‘inflection year’ as EV maker marks five years since IPO

It's been five years since Rivian Automotive celebrated its initial public offering with CEO and founder RJ Scaringe ringing the Nasdaq opening bell from the company's factory in Normal, Illinois, while the company's sleek vehicles sat outside the stock exchange in Times Square.

It was a euphoric time for all-electric vehicle companies that has since diminished. And much like the EV hype, Rivian's stock price is down more than 80% since the Amazon-backed company debuted on the Nasdaq at $78 per share on Nov. 10, 2021. That includes a nearly 30% decline this year.

A host of internal and external factors have contributed to Rivian's stock decline, but the company continues to be the most promising EV maker to emerge during the early 2020s, as most of its peer group to go public during that time no longer exists.

Rivian founder and CEO RJ Scaringe acknowledges that not everything has gone to plan amid supply chain shortages, geopolitical tensions and slower-than-expected consumer EV adoption, but he said he believes the next year will prove to be a catalyst for the company and investors.

"2027 is really an inflection year for us," Scaringe told CNBC during an exclusive interview. "I'm excited to start to really show what we believe so fully, the potential of the business."

Scaringe's bullishness comes as Rivian ramps up production of its midsize R2 SUV — meant to expand the brand to the mainstream market — and as the company continues to invest in its next generation of vehicle software and autonomy.

Scaringe said the R2, with an entry-level model at about $45,000, remains the core business to be able to showcase its newest technologies. Rivian delivered a record 19,248 vehicles in the third quarter of 2026, up 46% year over year, and said R2 was already its best-selling vehicle by volume, outselling both its R1 flagship products and its commercial van.

"That's going to define the business. The path to profitability, the path to very significant growth in revenue is all built around R2, and then some of the variants that come following R2," Scaringe said.

For the most recent period, Rivian said the automotive cost of goods sold per vehicle improved by more than $12,000 quarter over quarter, helped by a higher R2 mix and better fixed-cost leverage.

Rivian also is in the midst of building a new multibillion-dollar Georgia factory that Scaringe has said is necessary to get the company to the volumes it needs to achieve net profitability.

Its sole factory in Normal, Illinois, is capable of producing 215,000 vehicles annually. That includes up to 155,000 R2s alongside R1 models and electric vans. Rivian said the Georgia plant will be capable of producing up to 300,000 units annually once the initial phase construction is completed in late 2028.

Of course, all of that means additional capital spending and pressure on the company to improve profits.

"We've made the decision to simultaneously work towards profitability … and deeply invest in our technology, and in particular in autonomy," Scaringe said.

Rivian's operating performance is improving, but several Wall Street analysts expect the company's investment requirements to rise significantly in 2027.

Rivian generated $176 million of consolidated gross profit in the third quarter, but its automotive business posted an $80 million gross loss, while its software and services unit generated $256 million of gross profit at a 42% margin.

Wall Street analysts broadly agree Rivian's R2 launch is showing encouraging early demand and lowering manufacturing costs, but they're concerned about whether that can offset rising autonomy spending, the Georgia factory buildout and continued cash burn.

"Funding has long been a key debate for Rivian investors," Deutsche Bank analyst Edison Yu said in an Oct. 30 investor note after the company reported quarterly results that exceeded Wall Street's expectations. "The notion of a multi-year (even if credible) path to cash flow breakeven will be less attractive to investors."

Rivian said during its late October earnings call that its total available future liquidity is expected to be about $13 billion when factoring in available credit and money from partnerships with Volkswagen and Uber as well as a $4.5 billion U.S. Department of Energy loan for the Georgia plant.

Rivian said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion to end the third quarter.

The company may still need to raise more capital. Scaringe said Rivian plans to be "opportunistic" about any additional capital raises, which have previously included equity deals with Volkswagen and Uber.

"As we think about the environment looking out, we want to continue to position ourselves to be in a place to invest in the technology. And so that means we'll be opportunistic as it pertains to capital. But I want to be clear: We're like very focused on getting the profitability," Scaringe said.

The company has significantly upgraded the software capabilities and hardware on its vehicles to prepare them for autonomy technologies, or physical artificial intelligence, as Scaringe calls it.

The next step in that is releasing "point-to-point" driving capabilities, in which the vehicles can drive on their own from destination to destination with the driver acting as a monitor in case of any unexpected problems.

U.S. EV leader Tesla is the only automaker to currently offer those features on its vehicles. Scaringe said Rivian plans to not only catch up to Tesla's capabilities but exceed them as it uses multiple sensor types to allow the vehicle to "see" better, especially in inclement weather.

"That's not like the finish line. ... Think of it as like the starting point where you suddenly have a more apples to apples comparison between Rivian and Tesla," Scaringe said.

The release of point-to-point over the coming weeks and months, first to employees and then to customers, is expected to be followed by autonomous vehicles with Uber by 2028.

Scaringe said he believes such technologies will become a differentiator for the company in the coming years.

"I think it will go from a purchase consideration for maybe 10% of the buying public that's in the top five things considered in making a purchase decision to the No. 1 or No. 2 purchase consideration for the vast majority of buyers," Scaringe said.

Rivian plans to offer more details about its AI and autonomous plans during a second annual investor technology event on Dec. 9.

"We view this as a likely positive catalyst as the event could set a path for AV features driving incremental vehicle demand & higher [software] revenue in 2027, building on 2026 growth," TD Cowen analyst Itay Michaeli said in an investor note last week.

Rivian's stock is currently rated overweight with a $17.70 target price, according to analyst estimates compiled by FactSet. That would be a roughly 26% increase from its Monday closing price but still a far cry from its IPO.

#229. China confirms Xi-Trump-Putin historic summit. What will be on the agenda?

China confirmed Monday that President Xi Jinping will meet with Russian President Vladimir Putin and U.S. President Donald Trump in Shenzhen on Thursday, setting up the first-ever gathering of the three leaders.

That confirmation from a spokesperson at China's Foreign Ministry follows announcements from Moscow and the White House. The Wall Street Journal had reported last month that Putin had proposed a three-way sit-down in an attempt to negotiate an end to the four-year war in Ukraine.

While the summit among the three nations, on the sidelines of the APEC meeting in Shenzhen, is unprecedented, the leaders have held bilateral meetings over the years. Xi has met with Trump three times since October last year and more than 40 times with Putin since becoming president in 2013. Trump and Putin have met in person on several occasions, including a visit to Alaska by the Russian leader in August 2025.

"For Xi, just the ability to bring Putin and Trump to the table, a first among equals scenario, will deliver much of the political benefit even before the discussions begin," said Peter Alexander, Shanghai-based managing director at Z-Ben Advisors.

Trump has departed Washington, D.C. for a four-day trip to Asia that also includes a stop in the Philippines.

The main hurdle to the first-of-its-kind summit now appears to be "a disagreement over the agenda," said Neo Wang, chief China strategist at Evercore ISI.

The White House signaled the U.S. intention to prioritize talks over nuclear non-proliferation and the Middle East war, while downplaying the prospects of engaging in talks over Ukraine — a topic high on Moscow's agenda.

"He [Trump] will discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world, whose proliferation President Trump believes is very dangerous," Olivia Wales, a White House spokeswoman, told reporters on a briefing call. The president will raise the U.S.-Israeli war against Iran in the meeting but is not expected to bring up Russia's war in Ukraine, the official said.

China has no interest in engaging in nuclear disarmament talks until it achieves parity with the U.S. in about 10 years, said David Roche, strategist at Quantum Strategy.

Putin's position on the war appears unwavering, with the Russian president telling U.S. envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff in a meeting in Moscow last week that Europe should "figure out" whether "they want peace or war." The meeting came a day after an earlier round of talks aimed at ending the war in Ukraine wrapped up in Abu Dhabi.

Russian presidential aide Yuri Ushakov said last week the talks in Shenzhen would continue the diplomatic efforts aimed at reaching a negotiated settlement to the Ukraine war, according to state-controlled local media Russia Today.

The summit will likely yield little concrete result, but presents an "almost 100% photo op" that lifts China's status and power at the top table, said Roche, while Trump's political standing slides. Nvidia and the chip-smuggling issues involving a suspected Chinese spy will stay off the agenda, as Trump steers clear of thorny issues where he is unlikely to claim success.

China and Russia could turn more aggressive with their military ambitions over the next two years if they perceive Trump as politically weakened, Brookings Institution senior fellow Robert Kagan told CNBC earlier this month, following the dramatic defeat of Trump's Republican Party in the midterm election.

China did not specify what will be discussed at the three leaders' meeting in Monday's statement.

"Xi may be inclined to do Putin a favor by showing up, given close strategic ties," said Wang. But a joint statement on Ukraine territory, security arrangements or sanctions against Russia appears unlikely, he said, as it would leave Beijing "more exposed to political scrutiny and accountability."

A U.S.-China trade truce set to expire in January is likely to be on the table, with analysts largely expecting an extension despite signs of growing friction in the bilateral relationship.

Aside from an immediate extension, leaders may also look to assess "whether a more durable framework for a rebalancing of U.S.–China trade relations can be established," said Carlos Casanova, senior economist for Asia at UBP.

U.S. trade deficit with China remains substantial, with Beijing's exports to Washington recording double-digit growth for most of this year, from last year's low base. Narrowing that deficit will be central to Washington's call for a more balanced trade, Casanova said.

#226. The grads ditching the UK for a fresh start abroad

Charlie West, left, Rachel James and Owein Iveson have all moved overseas

Last year Rachel James quit her corporate job in London, packed a suitcase and booked a one-way ticket to Sydney, without a job lined up or a plan in place.

"I went for a fresh start," says the 26-year-old from Surrey. "To take a risk and try something completely different. With the worst case being there will always be a flight home if all fails."

More than one in 10 UK university students are now planning to move abroad for better opportunities after they have graduated, up from 7.8% two years ago, according to a survey, external of 15,000 final year university students.

Meanwhile, a study by the Institute of Student Employers (ISE) says that competition for graduate jobs in the UK was at a three-decade high.

UK employers now receive 140 applications per graduate vacancy on average, an increase of 14% from 2025, and up from just 30 applications per vacancy 20 years ago, according to the ISE's report, external.

For young British adults going abroad, where are they heading?

While there is no age-specific data for which countries people go to, Australia remains by far the most popular nation for British adults overall who move abroad, official figures show., external

In addition to Australia's weather and use of the English language, another reason is its visa-friendly policy. Under its Working Holiday Maker programme,, external British passport holders between the ages of 18 and 35 can live in Australia for up to three years.

Rachel James says that Sydney is a nicer place to live and work than London

Rachel, who graduated from Loughborough University in 2022 with a degree in English, had been working for accountancy giant PricewaterhouseCoopers in the UK. She now has a job in marketing in Sydney for gym chain Fitness First.

She says her annual salary has gone down from Â£42,000 to Â£37,000, while she now pays $AUS 2,000 (Â£1,000) a month to rent a two-bedroom apartment with a friend, similar to what she was paying in London.

Despite having less disposable income, she has no regrets. "The weather's beautiful, even in the winter, and the walks on my doorstep are unmatched.

"People want to leave work at 5pm so they can go and do their hobbies - whether it's surfing, running, enjoying the sunset, a picnic or sunset yoga. I didn't come with career progression as a focus - it's more about the different way of life."

Around two-thirds of British nationals who emigrate are aged between 16 and 34, according to the most recent figures, external from the UK's Office for National Statistics.

It said that 75,000 more people in that age bracket left the UK in 2025 than arrived.

After Australia the next most popular destination for British adults is the US.

Charlie West says that working in the US has given him better pay and more opportunities

Charlie West, 25, from Cheshire, moved to the States in 2024 for better pay and opportunities in academia after he completed a masters in medieval studies at Oxford University.

West is now two years in to a six-year funded PhD course at Yale's Department of Italian Studies, which includes him teaching.

"I really wanted to do a PhD but it's difficult to get funding in the UK, particularly in the humanities, and funded stipends are about Â£21,000. I'd have had to work [outside academia] at the same time to afford to live.

"In the US I'm paid $51,000 a year (Â£37,770), about to rise to $52,000, and it comes with a lot more opportunities to teach and travel to conferences."

The European Union also remains popular with young Britons looking to move abroad, although the visa requirements since Brexit have made it more difficult.

One way to get into the European jobs market is to have studied in the EU. International graduates can apply for a permanent residence permit after their degree ends.

Owein Iveson has no plans to return to the UK from Finland

This is what Owein Iveson, 24, from Sunderland, is doing as he is about to graduate from Finland's Aalto University with a masters in design.

Owein says he "fell in love with the country" after visiting for the first time in 2020.

"Finland's a beautiful country," he says. "The streets are very clean. It's a very quiet lifestyle. The nature is fantastic and the public transport works really efficiently."

Owein lives in Espoo, a city about 15 minutes on the metro from Helsinki, and doesn't expect to return to the UK anytime soon.

"To be honest the jobs market is not great in Finland, but there is high demand for IT workers, and if you can work across the EU there are many more opportunities."

Immigration lawyer John Kiely, a London-based partner at international law firm Howard Kennedy, says it is easy to see why more UK graduates are looking overseas.

"When you combine student debt, high housing costs, relatively weak wage growth and uncertainty about future employment, the willingness to roll the dice somewhere else becomes quite rational."

Back in Sydney, Rachel says the city simply offers a better quality of life than London.

"I don't see life in Sydney as any more expensive than if I was in London, but personally I have found the perks in Sydney better."
