#197. The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

BBC's Samira Hussain reports from Wall Street on what the fall of the tech superstar means.

Jensen Huang, known for sporting a black leather jacket to shareholder events, appeared to have it all. He was courted by leaders in the US and China alike, worshipped by investors and seen as a loyal family man. But a rockstar approach to his personal life has ultimately led to his resignation.

The 63-year-old has been married since 1985, long before the foundation of Nvidia, the company that propelled him to tech stardom.

He is known as a workaholic and a demanding manager, with high standards for those who worked for him, which included his own children, promoted within the company. But he has seen his personal reputation go into freefall this week, undone by romantic entanglements that throw doubt on his focus and his judgement.

On Thursday, he resigned following back-to-back revelations of two affairs. News of the first - a relationship with a businesswoman alleged to be a Chinese spy - ricocheted around the world, raising eyebrows among investors. It was followed on Wednesday by reports that the tech boss had also had an affair with an Nvidia colleague.

His departure creates uncertainty in the global stock market, which was already knocked-off balance in recent weeks. Nvidia is a linchpin in the US's race for technology leadership with China.

Huang may not be a household name on the same level as Elon Musk or Mark Zuckerberg.

But his role in the tech industry, especially in relation to the explosion of artificial intelligence in the last three years, may prove more consequential. Nvidia dominates the market for the microchips that are used to power the new technology.

The company, which is still valued at nearly $4n (Â£3.02tn) despite recent events, is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

Huang enjoyed access to the seats of political power on an unrivalled scale, being courted by both US President Donald Trump and China's President Xi Jinping.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession has led to him being dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" and seen him amass an estimated fortune of $145bn, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

Despite always donning his trademark all-black outfits and taking a playful approach to investor presentations, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a Master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work in senior roles at Nvidia.

The company became a key provider of the computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in a new direction. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing AI technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on par with the advent of electricity. Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company and its customers include the biggest firms on the planet, Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic, all spending big on its products.

So it is no surprise that at a recent AI summit at the White House, Huang was picked out from a group of 42 to flank the president, alongside Elon Musk, both men with whom Trump has famously close relationships.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Jensen Huang, told the BBC, referring to the late boss of Apple. He added that Huang was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

He described him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".

But while the former Nvidia CEO is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well", he said.

Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, he added, something that was "very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," Witt added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

First reports emerged on 29 October in the New York Times that Huang had conducted a relationship with Alice Huang, no relation. US officials allege the businesswoman associated with Singapore-based firm Megaspeed is a Chinese intelligence asset.

After the revelations of the first affair, Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

Trump, as well as other leaders in the AI tech industry, initially backed Huang to continue in his role at Nvidia. The president described him as "honourable".

But the reporting of a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare who has worked at Nvidia for 18 years, prompted a board meeting, followed by the announcement Huang was stepping down.

In the aftermath of that news Trump said Huang had done "a fantastic job" and proposed Tim Cook of Apple as a suitable replacement at the head of Nvidia.

#195. Measles is surging in the U.S. — and outbreaks can cost millions

Measles outbreaks aren't just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely.

The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year.

More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million.

The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs.

The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases.

"We're starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital," Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview.

Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick.

When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities.

Costs could differ heavily depending on the state.

Johns Hopkins' data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour.

The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses.

Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses.

The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states.

The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case.

"A lot of this is incurred up front, and that's because of the human labor that's involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there's an outbreak," Patenaude told CNBC. "It's a largely labor-driven cost."

That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them.

The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response.

People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery.

A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates.

The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses.

Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease.

Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone.

If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick.

That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles.

"Even if you don't get the infection, the contact costs you," he said. "And so it's just showing that there's this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens."

The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments.

"It's not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks," he said. "They're working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you're doing because there's been a huge outbreak in the community."

Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise.

"It's not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer," he said. "But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you're having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you're going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly."

#192. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

#189. Some under-25s should get fewer benefits, Milburn review to say

Alan Milburn will publish his review next week

Some young people could see reductions to the benefits they are entitled to under a radical overhaul of the welfare system to be recommended by a government-commissioned review.

As reported in the Times,, external Alan Milburn's year-long report, to be published on Tuesday, is expected to recommend moving under-25s off the main sickness and disability benefits.

Milburn, a former Labour health secretary, has been tasked by the government with helping reduce the one in eight 16 to 24 year-olds in the UK who are not in education, employment or training - otherwise known as "Neet".

He said on Wednesday night that his plans would not be "pain-free" and would affect both existing and new claimants.

His report is expected to say that under-25s should no longer be able to claim the health element of UK-wide universal credit - or personal independence payment (Pip), the main disability benefit in England and Wales.

Disabled people in Scotland can claim the devolved Adult Disability Payment.

Instead, under Milburn's plan, under-25s would receive an allowance on the same basic rate as universal credit.

Some could be eligible for a new "additional cost allowance" to cover day-to-day costs related to their conditions.

Some claimants would receive less under the plans - although the exact amounts will not be clear until early next year, when the government publishes legislation to implement the changes.

People can claim Pip to help them with the additional cost of being disabled or living with long-term physical or mental health conditions. It is available to those both in and out of work.

In his interim report,, external Milburn said survey data found that 19% of young people claiming disability or health benefits were in work.

Milburn, a Labour MP from 1992 to 2010, is also expected to recommend an investment of billions into more employment support for young people, including work placements, job subsidies and one-to-one personal advisers.

Those with more severe disabilities and unable to work would get a new benefit paid at a higher rate.

Milburn told the Times: "Candidly, it could mean that some people will get less financial support at the beginning of their benefit claim than under the current system.

"But actually, for young people, there's a different debate that we've got to resolve, which is whether the priority is their longer-term life chances or just their immediate shorter-term finances.

"I think what the current system does [is] it prioritises the latter over the former. I believe that's the wrong choice and it needs to be the other way around."

Speaking later on Thursday, Prime Minister Andy Burnham said young people would be given support "when they need it as teenagers rather than leaving them on benefits in their 20s".

He added: "The principle that we will be working on is that we will guarantee support for young people and that is the whole basis on which we will proceed."

Just under one million young people in the UK are Neets, data from the Office for National Statistics (ONS) from April to June suggests. This was a rise of 3% on the same period last year.

Milburn's recommendations are likely to be backed by Prime Minister Andy Burnham but could prove controversial among Labour MPs, who opposed welfare changes last year under his predecessor Sir Keir Starmer.

Dame Meg Hillier, who helped spearhead a backbench rebellion against Starmer's reforms, suggested she and her colleagues were broadly supportive this time around.

"What happened last year was a cut was being proposed and none of us could explain to people what would happen to them," the Labour MP told the BBC.

"Milburn has done some very good work on this so I'm giving him a fair wind," she added.

But Bell Ribeiro-Addy, one of the Labour backbenchers who revolted last year, said she was "not sure" she could vote to cut the benefit entitlement of existing claimants.

Tom Ashforth, a 19-year-old student at Sheffield Hallam University, receives both components of Pip at the higher rate.

He said the financial support helps him manage additional expenses linked to his visual impairment, including prescriptions, mobility aids and accessible learning resources.

"If I were to lose it, I would struggle to afford essential things," he said.

Student Tom said he relies on the money he gets from Pip to support him through his time at university

He said he believes decisions over disability benefits should be made on a case-by-case basis, rather than according to age.

"You cannot assume that everyone under the age of 25 is in the same position," he said.

In his Times interview, Milburn acknowledged that changing the rules for existing claimants would be "difficult politically" but stressed it was necessary.

James Taylor, from disability equality charity Scope, said Pip was a "lifeline" and restricting access to it "won't make young disabled people's extra costs disappear".

Harriet Edwards, from the charity Sense, said changes were "long overdue", but that without additional details, families were "deeply anxious".

A government spokesperson said: "We will always ensure there is a safety net for the most vulnerable and those who are unable to work."

The reforms would represent a radical overhaul of Britain's welfare system, at a time when there is heavy political pressure for the UK to find the money to spend more on defence.

Both the Conservatives and Reform UK have argued for significant cuts to welfare, including to fund a boost to defence.

Responding on Thursday, Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the government should not be spending "lots of time" on employment schemes, which she said would ultimately fail.

If Milburn's recommendations were approved, it could take almost a decade for the British state to see monetary savings.

The new system "will need significant investment" and would involve "rebuilding the system from scratch", Milburn said.

His new proposed system would involve an element of carrot and stick, wherein young people could lose some of their benefits if they did not engage with the extra work support options.

In an interview recorded on Tuesday, Skills Minister Jacqui Smith told the BBC that young people had a "responsibility" to take up employment support.

"It is a contract. People need the chance, and they need to take that chance," she added.

A separate review - co-chaired by disabilities minister Sir Stephen Timms - examining disability benefits is also expected to be published this month.

#186. Poundland gets more time to secure a rescue deal as emergency talks continue

Poundland has avoided immediate collapse after a deadline was extended to find a rescue deal.

A deadline to secure a buyer for the retail chain was set to expire at midnight, but a court has given Poundland an extra 10 days to find a buyer.

The discount chain, which has 11,000 employees, has been edging towards administration after being blocked by owners Gordon Brothers from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

A committee of MPs questioned Gordon Brothers following accusations that the investment firm hoped to profit from selling off Poundland's 600 shops and stock piecemeal.

Gordon Brothers - which bought the chain for just Â£1 last year - is accused of forcing that outcome by restricting Poundland's access to available cash needed to restock shops, and pay wages and rent, ahead of the crucial Christmas trading period.

The investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claims.

However, pressure remains on Poundland's owners after a takeover bid was rejected. The decision prompted calls for answers from politicians, including Prime Minister Andy Burnham.

About Â£50m had been frozen by Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning Poundland bosses were unable to restock shelves across its 600 shops.

The BBC understands that Gordon Brothers agreed to release a further Â£11m today from Poundland's accounts, on top of Â£10m announced earlier, as the firm seeks to find a new buyer.

Gordon Brothers said it was in talks with three parties for a sale of the whole business, and it had received "several offers from established high street retailers for significant parts of the business".

Following the meeting between MPs on the business and trade committee and representatives of Gordon Brothers, chairman Liam Byrne told the BBC that he was "deeply concerned" and described the firm's behaviour as "much like a wolf on Wall Street, on the British High Street".

"They refused to prioritise protecting jobs and stores over and above their own financial returns," he said.

"It's clear that they've taken millions out of Poundland at a time when they themselves said the business was on a knife edge."

Byrne said the committee's message to Gordon Brothers was to ensure Poundland had "access to the funds that it needs to trade at what should be one of the busiest periods in the year".

He urged Gordon Brothers to "do what is right to save jobs and stores and do that now".

"It's a good business, it's a much-loved business and, particularly in the middle of the cost of living crisis, communities up and down the country rely on access to the kind of stores that it operates," Byrne added.

Views on the chain's performance differ between those running operations and the owners.

Poundland boss Williams told staff last week that like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4%. The sales period included Halloween, but Williams said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He added that the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year when there were losses of Â£68m.

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It has argued that the best way of securing jobs may be to split up the business and sell chunks of its 600 sites to other retailers.

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up Poundland shops, which could mean continuing employment for workers at those sites.

The main issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland as a national chain.

Are you affected by issues discussed in this story? Do you work for Poundland? Share your experiences.

#183. Cantwell urges Lutnick to stop AI chip sales to China ahead of Xi meeting

Sen. Maria Cantwell, the top Democrat on the Senate commerce committee, alleged in a letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick on Sunday that the department under his leadership failed to prevent China from accessing the U.S.'s most advanced artificial intelligence chips.

In the letter, Cantwell prodded Lutnick to enforce export controls and stop the flow of AI chips to companies barred from receiving the technology. She sent the missive ahead of a planned meeting this week between U.S. officials and Chinese President Xi Jinping on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation, in China.

Her urging comes as Washington reels from revelations that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had a relationship with an alleged Chinese spy named Alice Huang. Nvidia is the leading U.S. company designing advanced AI chips.

Alice Huang was an executive at Singaporean technology company Megaspeed that allegedly smuggled restricted chips to China.

Lutnick, meanwhile, has come under fire for stalling efforts to crack down on chip sales to China and for his close personal relationship with Jensen Huang, who was a vocal opponent of export controls.

"The Department of Commerce has resisted efforts to restrict companies flagged as national security threats, weakened safeguards, and left open pathways for the PRC to train advanced AI models using sensitive American technology," Cantwell wrote to Lutnick. "These failures to carry out the law help give away America's technology advantage in AI."

The Commerce Department did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Monday.

Cantwell called on Lutnick to use his authority to enforce a 2018 law, known as the Export Control Reform Act, which gives the Commerce Department broad latitude to enforce export controls and protect sensitive American technology.

She also cites potential financial conflicts involving Lutnick's sons, who control Cantor Fitzgerald, which she said held $1.2 billion in Nvidia stock at the time of his nomination. And she notes the Trump administration's 25% fee on certain Nvidia chips sold to China, which she said could create "a perverse financial incentive to approve exports of sensitive American technology."

"America's national security and AI leadership cannot be compromised by family business interests or a policy that generates federal revenue from exporting technology critical to our competitive edge," Cantwell wrote.

Both Democratic and Republican members of Congress have ramped up pressure on Lutnick and other Commerce Department officials after a Nov. 9 New York Times report alleging the department may have slow-walked an investigation into Megaspeed.

On Thursday, Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., summoned both Huang and Lutnick to testify before his committee in December.

Lutnick has agreed to appear before the panel, Kristen Eichamer, a Commerce Department spokesperson, said in a statement last week.

And Democrats, who will take control of both the House and Senate in January, have signaled they will investigate Jensen Huang and Commerce officials.

On Wednesday, Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the top Democrat on the Senate banking panel, and Sen. Chris Coons, D-Del., the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations subpanel with jurisdiction over East Asia and the Pacific, sent a letter to Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller instructing them to preserve all documents and communications related to Nvidia's exports of "advanced AI chips to China or to entities that may divert such chips to China."

The letter gives all three officials until Nov. 17 to confirm that preservation directives have been issued.

#182. JetBlue unveils new first-class seats to bring back profits

JetBlue Airways unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country's carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. The carrier showed off the new cabin at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport on Wednesday. Retrofits will continue during 2027, CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

JetBlue will have 12 of the seats on its Airbus A320 fleet and 30 extra-legroom seats, down from 42 of those options previously. Because the airplanes don't have ovens, JetBlue said it is offering an array of cold food options like sesame noodles, pastrami sandwiches and tortellini salad, though they vary by route.

The rollout comes as the New York airline tries to stabilize after last posting a profit in 2019. Last month, JetBlue executives sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors.

"We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," Geraghty told CNBC in an interview on Oct. 27.

Geraghty told CNBC's Phil LeBeau on Wednesday that JetBlue has "met with a few of the creditors over the last couple of weeks as part of ... normal investor engagement." She said the company made it clear to bondholders that JetBlue doesn't need to reorganize.

The airline's shares are up more than 40% this month, more than other passenger carriers, as of midday Wednesday, though they are still off about 11% so far this year.

JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

Airline executives have said they expect fares to stay elevated next year as they grapple with higher costs, including but not limited to fuel, such as labor and airport fees. Geraghty said Wednesday that even if fuel prices moderate next year, "I don't think fares are going to fall as much as people expect."

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new recliner first-class seats between Kennedy airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines unveiled its new first-class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seating that has extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview Monday.

JetBlue last month reported that it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty last month said "absolutely not."

#181. UK economy grows at fastest rate for more than two years

Computer programming and consultancy services in particular contributed to overall growth (generic photo)

ï»¿The UK economy grew at its fastest rate for more than two years in the three months to September.

Between July and September, the economy expanded by 0.7%, accelerating from the previous three months, official figures, external show.

The robust growth suggests a far more resilient economy than expected over the summer, despite the impact of higher energy prices from the Iran war.

Strong growth in the services sector - the largest part of the economy, which includes non-physical goods such as consultancy services and computer programming as well as hotels and restaurants - helped boost the numbers.

Within services, computer programming registered its fastest growth for the past three years, helped by cloud computing and AI-linked spending.

The boost from computer programming and consultancy services has been the biggest driver of growth so far this year.

Grant Fitzner, chief economist at the Office for National Statistics, said it was "possible growth could have been even stronger" if there hadn't been a war in the Middle East.

But he says households may still not feel better off, and that prices might soon start to rise more quickly than wages.

However, Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the figures were "welcome news".

"Despite a year where events around the world have pushed up costs here at home, our economy is growing, bringing hope for the future," he said.

Chancellor John Healey told the BBC the figures showed a "remarkable resilience" in the face of global conflicts, and hopes they "bring back confidence in Britain".

Asked about how government decisions could be the reason for growth when AI investment has been a main driver, Healey said ministers can always "help or hinder" business confidence and investments.

Although investors have recently been concerned about slower artificial intelligence development in the US and the long term viability of AI firms' business models, businesses have continued to invest in the technologies.

Sarah Coles, from investment platform AJ Bell, said that since the start of the year, "it's the less visible back-office work that has delivered, with administrative and supportive services seeing strong growth" noting that these are the areas benefitting from the increasing use of AI.

The latest figures put the UK on course to be the second fastest growing economy across the major G7 countries which also includes Canada, Japan, Germany, Italy and France.

However, Jake Finney, senior economist at consultancy PwC, cautioned that growth was likely to slow over the coming months "unless energy markets stabilise".

A sharp rise in energy bills is forecast for January, which is likely to hit consumer spending and confidence, he said.

High energy costs have been stoked by the ongoing conflict in the Middle East and Russia's invasion of Ukraine.

US and Israeli attacks led to Iran blocking the Strait of Hormuz - a vital shipping route for the world's oil and gas.

Meanwhile, Russia and Ukraine have been attacking each other's oil refineries, leading to a squeeze on global refining capacity, hitting diesel and petrol prices.

What's happening to the UK economy and how does it affect you?

Entrepreneur Richard Benc says the UK watch market and exports have been surprisingly resilient

Richard Benc, founder of mechanical watch company Studio Underdog, says sales are better than he expected.

"We've seen more resilience than we had forecast for and expected. We are still projecting growth this year," he says.

The business exports about 80% of its watches. "We've seen a surprising amount of resilience, particularly from our US customers," he adds, although some UK customers are now taking longer to buy watches.

Do you have any views, comments or questions about this story?

#179. Trump says he's OK with Jensen Huang returning as Nvidia CEO, sees no national security risk

President Donald Trump said he would be open to former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang returning to the company he resigned from last week amid the fallout from an extramarital relationship he had with an alleged Chinese spy.

"I think he learned a big lesson," Trump said of Huang when asked by reporters on Monday whether he wants to see the company co-founder reinstated as CEO.

Trump, speaking to reporters outside the White House before departing for a trip to Asia, downplayed national security concerns stemming from the affair with a former executive of Megaspeed, a Singapore-based technology company that U.S. officials suspect of having funneled Nvidia's advanced chips to China.

"It was not put at risk, and our country is now stronger than ever before," Trump told reporters when asked about U.S. national security concerns. Trump is due to meet with Chinese President Xi Jinping at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum.

The president told reporters he is not planning to replace Howard Lutnick as commerce secretary after questions arose about the Cabinet member's handling of export controls involving Nvidia's advanced chips.

Republican and Democratic members of Congress have raised concerns about the national security implications of the episode after it was revealed that Huang, who co-founded Nvidia in the 1990s, had an extramarital affair with Chinese businesswoman and alleged spy Alice Huang. Nvidia is the leading global supplier of advanced artificial intelligence chips.

Critics argue the affair could jeopardize the U.S.' global dominance in AI and that it raises questions about influence exerted by Nvidia's Huang on regulators and lawmakers as he opposed export controls that limited the sale of the company's chips to Chinese firms.

The New York Times reported Tuesday that the U.S. believed Alice Huang was a Chinese spy and that Secretary of State Marco Rubio alerted Trump to the situation aboard Air Force One in May before Jensen Huang joined the president on a flight to China.

The Washington Post reported Friday that Rubio had issued a memo to Trump warning the president about Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy.

But Trump on Monday said "I haven't heard that" when asked about Rubio's warning.

"I don't know that she is a spy. Usually, what happens with that is that any woman coming to the country that's a beautiful woman that comes from China, they say she's a spy," Trump said, of Alice Huang. "I don't know that she was a spy at all."

Trump also attempted to defuse tension bubbling within his Cabinet between Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and Lutnick.

Axios reported last week that Lutnick has "repeatedly stood in the way" of cracking down on companies suspected of diverting Nvidia's advanced AI chips to China, prompting a Situation Room confrontation between the men. Both are traveling with Trump on Monday to Asia, as is Rubio.

And Bessent, in an appearance on CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday, seemed to express frustration at Lutnick for not putting Megaspeed, Alice Huang's former company, on a Commerce Department list of companies that pose national security concerns.

The departments of Treasury, Defense and State and the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative all "were in favor of putting Megaspeed on the 'entity list' for months," Bessent said.

Trump acknowledged on Monday that Lutnick might have delayed putting Megaspeed on the list but defended his rationale as essential for diplomacy.

"He was doing that because you could have — if you didn't delay it, If you didn't do something — you could have a blow-up with China," Trump said. "We're having a very good relationship with China right now, and I understand both sides of it, and I'll make a decision. But I understand very well both sides."

Trump, who previously had said he did not plan to raise the Jensen Huang issue with Xi when they meet this week, said he may do so. He also said he may meet with North Korean leader Kim Jong Un in China.

As Trump downplays concerns about fallout from the affair and advanced chips finding their way to China, Capitol Hill leaders are taking a different approach.

A spokesperson for House Foreign Affairs Committee Chair Brian Mast, R-Fla., told CNBC "negotiations are still going" about Jensen Huang testifying before a trio of House committees.

Mast, along with House Select China Committee Chair John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Intelligence Committee Chair Rick Crawford, R-Ark., had given Huang a deadline of 8 p.m. ET Friday to respond to their request to appear before the committees. Mast has said he would subpoena the former CEO if he doesn't accept the invitation voluntarily.

#178. Faisal Islam: Why the UK economy is surprisingly resilient

The UK economy grew at its fastest rate for more than two years in the three months to September.

"Top gear" and "strikingly strong" were among the descriptions of the 0.7% growth offered by the economists who track every minor murmur of Britain's economy.

It is an upgrade on "solid" and "resilient", and in turn a world away from "hardest hit by the Iran War".

Against the backdrop of the world's biggest disruption to energy flows, rising market borrowing costs - and the cruel summers since the pandemic - it is impressive growth.

It should reset some of the swirl of self-reinforcing negativity.

The UK's economic growth has averaged less than 0.1% in the four Q3s [ie July to September] since the pandemic.

The average prediction just months ago for this July to September was just 0.1%.

Even the UK's official forecaster the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR) effectively pencilled in 0.3% at the Budget just 3 weeks ago.

This figure has not been exceeded outside the pandemic since 2017. It puts the UK on course to be the second fastest growing economy after the US across the major G7 countries which also includes Canada, Japan, Germany, Italy and France.

It is tempting to think about what the economy would have done if the Iran War hadn't happened. If interest rates had been cut instead of their recent rise.

While the latest figure kills the doom, it is clearly not a boom either.

Spending on AI and tech have driven the surprise upside over the year. While the AI data centre boom may at any point turn to bust, these figures suggest that spending across the economy on investing in these technologies is helping to boost growth. Consumers have been resilient, at least till the autumn, perhaps helped by better economic vibes.

While there has been an understandable focus on high UK borrowing costs, another measure of the international standing of the UK economy, sterling's value, reached post Brexit referendum highs last week.

Productivity and investment is also looking stronger, especially after recent revisions. Some of the most encouraging figures are in private sector business investment.

I spoke to the chancellor at Nexeon in Oxfordshire, which is searching for a UK factory to manufacture its cutting edge electric vehicle battery - and British - technology. My colleagues visited British watchmakers in Maidenhead carving out a niche to take on the Swiss.

However, this may well not last for the final three months of the year.

Consumer confidence has already fallen as petrol and domestic energy prices start to rise again.

There is a significant challenge with everyday cost-of-living pressures in the coming months. Construction and private house building remain depressed.

The Iran conflict remains the big unknown and whether post the US midterm elections there could be a durable end to the conflict in the Gulf.

Some oil and gas production there is starting to normalise. It could bring down petrol prices, energy bills, government borrowing costs, and mortgage costs too.

Expect to see Downing Street investing heavily in "settlement of the conflict", which the chancellor told me is the "best economic policy".

#176. Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

Arm's boss Rene Haas (L), Nvidia's Colette Kress (C) and Chris Malachowsky (R) have been named as possible successors

Nvidia is now facing a scenario few would have considered until very recently - a future without Jensen Huang.

The influential co-founder of the AI chip-making giant resigned on Thursday over reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Chief financial officer Colette Kress is in charge, for now, as the tech giant faces one of its biggest challenges - finding the right person to permanently lead the business that Huang helped create more than three decades ago and turned into one of the world's most valuable companies.

We take a look at which candidates have emerged as potential successors.

Colette Kress is chief financial officer at Nvidia

Colette Kress has been an industry veteran for more than 30 years, having previously held high positions at tech firms like Cisco and Microsoft.

Kress, who also serves as Nvidia's executive vice-president, has seen the firm's transformation from making chips for video games in the early 1990s to becoming an AI semiconductor powerhouse.

She has "fronted every earnings call" alongside Huang and is a "familiar and trusted voice for the market at a time when it badly needs one," said Josh Gilbert, an analyst at investment firm eToro.

But doing the job permanently could be a different story, said Gilbert, as the firm, known for its edge in technology, could want an engineer in the chair.

Chris Malachowsky is one of Nvidia's three co-founders

Another option is Chris Malachowsky, who founded Nvidia alongside Huang and Curtis Priem, and is the only executive that shares the same level of seniority.

Malachowsky has played a key part in developing the company's computing technology.

Ian Buck, who oversees Nvidia's high-performance computing business, has also emerged as a potential candidate if the company is keen to put an engineer in the top job.

Buck was among the early pioneers who helped popularise the use of graphic processors beyond gaming applications.

"He could protect Nvidia's technological edge, but his ability to manage the wider commercial relationships and geopolitical challenges would face scrutiny," said Charu Chanana, investment strategist at investment bank Saxo.

Today, Nvidia's leading processors are widely regarded as the gold standard for AI development.

Huang's children also work at Nvidia in executive positions but are relatively young and inexperienced.

Nvidia is likely to favour an internal appointment initially but could look externally for a more permanent replacement, said Jun Bei Liu, founder and lead portfolio manager at Ten Cap.

Several names of tech entrepreneurs outside of Nvidia have been floated by experts.

US President Donald Trump suggested Apple's former chief executive, Tim Cook, as a good replacement.

"NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place - TIM COOK of Apple. A Legend replaces a Legend!" he wrote on Truth Social.

Liu said Cook could be a possible choice as someone familiar with managing global supply chains, though she questioned whether he would be willing to leave the iPhone maker, where he is the chair.

Apple's Tim Cook (R) with Jensen Huang (L) and his wife Lori Huang at the White House

Liu and Chanana also suggested Rene Haas, chief executive of Arm Holdings, the UK chip designer.

Nvidia's processors are made with the help of chipmakers around the world, including Taiwan's TSMC and South Korea's SK Hynix and Samsung.

Haas is familiar with leading a publicly-listed company and would bring semiconductor experience, though Nvidia's business model is considerably different from Arm's, said Chanana.

"The challenge is finding someone who can combine Jensen's technical understanding, strategic vision and relationships across the global technology industry," Liu said.

Ten Cap's Liu had believed it was just a matter of time before Huang would step down as Nvidia's chief executive.

"The issue is no longer simply about personal conduct," she said. "It is about corporate governance, national security concerns and maintaining confidence in the leadership of one of the world's most strategically important companies."

Several major investment firms have downgraded their forecasts for the chip giant - a rare shift given investors' longstanding optimism about the company.

An early indication came as markets opened in New York shortly after Huang's resignation was announced.

Nvidia shares opened down more than 13%, wiping more than half a trillion dollars off the firm's share market value.

Jensen Huang's departure leaves a void in the influential AI giant

Stacy Rasgon, senior analyst in US semiconductors at Bernstein Research, wrote in a note to investors that "our gut tells us we probably haven't fully probed the depths of what might be found here".

Rasgon halved Bernstein's share price target for Nvidia and said: "We've had enough and prefer to watch from the sidelines."

Ray Wang, chief executive of Constellation Research, said the crisis isn't so much an issue for the wider AI industry but rather one for Nvidia itself which has to consider if the person it appoints as its chief executive has the same appeal as Huang.

"That's really what the issue is, because a lot of these partnerships and investments have been made because of the trust with with Jensen," he said.

#174. Nvidia fallout: Commerce targets loophole for China's data center use

The Commerce Department on Monday moved to close a loophole that has let Chinese companies lease Nvidia's advanced computing power from data centers in third countries, effectively circumventing controls on sensitive chip technology being exported to China.

The interim final rule from Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security is the first U.S. policy response to the scandal that led Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang to resign last week after reports that he had an affair with an alleged Chinese spy who worked for Megaspeed, one of Nvidia's biggest Asian customers.

The rule was "written by and at the direction of" the Treasury Department and the White House National Security Council, BIS said in a press release.

The rule came after growing criticism of Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick for not doing enough to prevent possible diversion of Nvidia's most advanced chips to China in violation of American export controls.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, in an interview on Friday with CNBC's "Squawk Box," blamed the Commerce Department for not putting Singapore-based Megaspeed on its "entity list," which would restrict certain exports to it.

Share prices of Nvidia and other companies linked to the artificial intelligence trade fell Monday morning after the interim rule was announced, among them chipmakers Micron Technology, Intel and Advanced Micro Devices.

The rule is designed "to prevent certain foreign actors from leveraging advanced computing power based on U.S. technology to threaten U.S. national security and foreign policy interests," according to the text of the rule.

The new restrictions "will undermine the ability of companies headquartered in countries of concern from using" advanced artificial intelligence semiconductors produced by U.S. companies to train advanced AI models, the rule says.

The rule also says that a license will now "be required to export, reexport, or transfer (in-country) advanced semiconductors to Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, or Brazil (and any additional country designated by the Secretary of Commerce, in consultation with the Secretary of State or the Assistant to the President for National Security Affairs) – countries which present a higher-than-normal risk of diversion."

The New York Times reported on Nov. 9 that American authorities began investigating Singapore-based Megaspeed and one of its executives, Alice Huang, in 2024, "after the company started importing billions of dollars' worth of advanced Nvidia A.I. equipment into Malaysia."

"Megaspeed had been planning and constructing data centers around Southeast Asia to serve Chinese clients, including Alibaba, the Chinese tech giant," The Times reported that day. "While that behavior is legal, U.S. officials were concerned that some of the Nvidia chips the company had bought were illegally routed into China."

Jensen Huang resigned as Nvidia CEO last week after reports that he had an affair with Alice Huang, and that she was a spy working for China. The two are not related. China's government has denied the claim that Alice Huang was a spy.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio, who, as national security advisor, leads the National Security Council, warned President Donald Trump last spring that Jensen Huang was in a close relationship with Alice Huang, The Times has reported.

Rep. John Mooelnaar, the Michigan Republican who is chairman of the House Select Committee on China, in a statement on the new rule that it "sends a message to the Chinese Communist Party that America is serious about closing dangerous export control loopholes China has been exploiting for years."

"Our most advanced AI chips are simply not for sale to our foremost enemy," Moolenaar said. "This new rule is an important step in keeping chips out of ill-intentioned hands, strengthens our national security laws, and will help protect America's lead in the AI race."

Chris McGuire, a former deputy senior director for technology and national security at the National Security Council, in a post on X called the new rule "a major step forward to countering Chinese smuggling of and access to advanced AI chips."

"The work here is not done, as there are some critical loopholes that remain open and need to be closed in a follow-up rule (namely those related to activities inside the United States)," wrote McGuire, a senior fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations. "Commerce should move extremely quickly to close these. But this is the first time in over 18 months that the administration is starting to take export controls seriously, and does represent a meaningful step forward."

McGuire said that the fact that the new rule was written by and at the direction of the Treasury Department and the NSC "is remarkable and unprecedented."

"I have never seen a BIS rule that was not written by BIS," he wrote. "That implies that Commerce was dragged kicking and screaming over the finish line by the White House and Treasury, and instructed to publish this rule rather than the rumored weaker one that it preferred. Remarkable."

Commerce's new rule was published a day after Sen. Maria Cantwell of Washington state, the ranking Democrat on the Committee on Commerce, Science and Transportation, in a letter to Lutnick said that Chinese companies such as Alibaba and ByteDance have "used overseas data centers equipped with advanced Nvidia chips to train advanced AI models."

"These companies are exploiting a loophole in U.S. export controls by using advanced American chips in foreign data centers even when those chips are restricted from the [People's Republic of China]," Cantwell wrote.

"Despite warnings from the Bureau of Industry and Security that certain arrangements may violate existing restrictions, the Department of Commerce failed to close this loophole, allowing PRC companies to advance their AI capabilities using sensitive American technology," she wrote.

#173. Heat pumps: How much heat does your home really need?

Floorplans. Air duct diagrams. Heat pump specifications. Most people in the market for a new heating system probably don't want to think about this level of detail.

But, perhaps surprisingly, some do want to think about those things. In extreme depth.

Every day, roughly 150 new users sign up to an app called Cool Calc, which works out the heat requirements of properties in the US.

Just over one third of those new users are not tradespeople but private homeowners, says founder and chief executive Bill Fiore.

When choosing a heating system, people can find themselves bombarded with information â how powerful a heat pump do they need, for example? It isn't always easy to take a tradesperson at their word.

"As an overall trend, we are seeing homeowners be more informed," explains Fiore. "They want to do a calculation on their own."

Cool Calc is used by tradespeople and homeowners to work out heat loss

A heat loss calculation is a figure, usually measured in watts, that defines how much heat a home loses during cold weather. It's worked out room by room and is a key part of designing an efficient heating system.

This estimate is especially important to get right when installing a heat pump, which is sized fairly precisely, according to the projected heat loss.

Should that calculation be off, the heat pump will likely perform poorly. You could be left with an underpowered device that struggles to warm your property, for example. Given the costs involved, homeowners are often anxious to ensure installers avoid such mistakes.

Certain new technologies are not, for the moment, helping, adds Fiore. "People are going to AI and they're getting a lot of misinformation because it's not backed by anything credible," he says. Cool Calc is planning to integrate with AI chatbots to try and improve the quality of the advice they spew out.

It is probably a good idea to be cautious about your new heating system's design, says Kevin Kircher at Purdue University. He and colleagues recently conducted a study, yet to be published, examining the heat loss calculations provided by tradespeople to roughly 75 US homeowners.

"We were surprised," says Kircher. "The heat loss calculations often led to overestimating the design heat loads by a factor of two or more." That would result in oversized systems that use much more power than necessary.

Not only that, some people reported charges of more than $1,000 (Â£745) â and in one case as much as $2,500 â just for a heat loss survey, adds Kircher.

Besides using excessive amounts of energy, an oversized heating system can also feel uncomfortable, says Dan Dempsey, a former senior engineering fellow for a major heating and cooling provider in the US.

Dempsey, who took part in Kircher and his colleagues' study, lives in Indiana. He says it is his retirement hobby to monitor the performance of heating and cooling systems in the homes of friends and family members, by analysing data from their smart thermostats.

"I spent five years manually downloading everything, every week," he says. He points out that oversized systems tend to cycle a lot, meaning they switch on and off again in quick succession.

This leads to discernible swings in temperature: rooms that fail to remain at their target temperature of, say, 20C, but rather fluctuate, which feels uncomfortable, says Dempsey.

For people seeking a better understanding of their home's heat loss, various free or partially free apps, including Cool Calc and AutoHVAC, are available. There are options for UK homeowners, too, such as web app Heatpunk.

Heat loss apps help drive business to renewable energy firms says Andy Rankin

Many things can influence the heat loss figure, including the amount of insulation in your home, whether the building has double or triple glazing, and also the size of your radiators.

Heatpunk allows people to draw a floor plan of their home and input data about the property in order to estimate its performance. Thousands of people use it every month, mostly professional installers, says Andy Rankin, founder and managing director of Midsummer Energy.

The web app also suggests specific heat pump models for each property, based on the results of the calculation. Naturally, these are models available through Midsummer Energy. "It's been a very effective marketing tool," says Rankin.

Dwellow, another company that offers heat pump installations in the UK, is keen to follow suit. The firm recently launched its own heat loss estimator.

While professional heat loss calculations remain essential, getting people to do their own "self surveys" first could ultimately reduce how much time professional heating engineers must spend analysing properties says Ian Mclaren, Dwellow's co-founder and director.

"Going into a home and just being able to check the information rather than start from scratch could turn a survey into a half-hour trip â far more efficient and less costly," says Mclaren.

There's no substitute for engineers doing a good job when it is their turn, however, stresses Maurizio Sibilla, of the Centre for Advanced Built Environment Research at the University of West England, Bristol.

"The solution is better installer practice, clearer customer-facing evidence, and improved methods, not expecting homeowners to do the engineering themselves," he says, adding that providing homeowners with information about the calculation and also data on heat pump performance post-installation can reassure them that the system works as expected.

Adam Chapman questions whether homeowners should do their own heat loss survey

Doing your own heat loss calculation may end up "over-intellectualising" things, says Adam Chapman, chief geek at heat pump training firm and installer network Heat Geek.

"I don't personally see what a homeowner would gain from it," he says. "We will guarantee the efficiency with way less radiators and pipework upgrades than any other system out there."

Chapman points out that only the most careful of surveys will capture a home's heat loss well.

He says it is common for Heat Geek installers to discover retrofitted windows that haven't been foam-insulated into their openings, leaving draughty gaps, or cavity walls that a homeowner thought had been insulated â but which were actually missed by the builder.

"It's stuff you can't see," he says. "What we actually need is home surveys that go and spot for these sorts of things."

Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

#171. The rise of 'housefishing' - when does using AI go from enhancing to misleading?

The images Olivia Nicole saw of a home on Rightmove (l) were completely different to how the property looked in real life (r)

Have you ever been impressed by the pictures of a property on an estate agent's website, only to find the place looks completely different when you view it in person?

Then there's a chance that AI was used to enhance the images you saw.

From repainted walls to imaginary lawns, estate agents are increasingly using the technology to make homes seem more appealing, arguing it helps buyers visualise the potential of a place.

But experts warn it can go too far and mislead prospective buyers - a trend dubbed "housefishing".

Earlier this year Olivia Nicole, 29, was searching online for five-bedroom properties in south-west London when a house on Rightmove caught her eye.

But when she arrived to view it several days later she initially thought she had come to the wrong place.

"The rooms were much smaller than they were in the pictures and the decor was totally different," she tells the BBC. "Some structural features were also different."

For example, there was a raised section of the wall behind the sofa that didn't appear in the images. The windows were also noticeably smaller. Olivia was so surprised she took her own pictures of the property for comparison.

AI has been used to make the property look more luxurious

It later turned out the pictures she had seen on Rightmove, which also appeared on the Zoopla and Winkworth websites, were enhanced with AI.

But that wasn't clear in the listing, Olivia says, and she feels the agent wasted her time.

"It was frustrating, because I had taken several hours off work for the viewing which I had to make up for."

All three companies told the BBC they had strict guidelines on how AI could be used on their websites and have since taken the images down.

Estate agents have long used wide-angle lens cameras or Photoshop to make homes seem bigger or more appealing to buyers, but AI is making this process much easier and more accessible.

It is difficult to quantify how common the practice is, says the Guild of Property Professionals, a trade group of independent estate agents, but it has issued guidance on how to use AI enhancement responsibly.

According to a recent survey, external of 906 UK homebuyers, one in five who had who purchased a home in the past year said they had viewed a property where the listing photos had been digitally enhanced to make the home appear better than it actually was.

Jack Bushrod reviews quirky property listings he finds on Rightmove for his social media channel, Housing Horrors, and says he has seen a "surge" of AI-enhanced images over the last six months.

He thinks using AI is fine up to a point - to declutter a busy room, or add furniture to an empty one so a prospective buyer can see its potential.

But he draws the line at anything that misleads a prospective buyer or wastes their time.

"I've seen one house where they had removed a pillar and added a window. Others might use AI to remove stains on the carpet or cracks in tiles. It's little details you would only notice in person."

He says most of the enhanced images he's seen weren't clearly labelled as AI-generated.

Cara says AI was used to empty her son's room in the image on the left

Cara, 38, is currently selling her home in Lowestoft, Suffolk, and says she was surprised when she saw the AI-enhanced pictures of her property her estate agent, Howards, put on Rightmove.

In one photo, her son's room was completely emptied of toys and clutter.

In another, of her daughter's room, the agent appears to have removed a doll's house, brightened the room, and used AI to change the shape of a rug, but that image has since been taken down.

Cara has had several viewings so far and says that one person has commented on the differences. But she says she trusts Howards, which has acknowledged the use of AI in the property listing.

"If they'd changed something structurally or it was really wild and didn't look like our home at all then I would put my foot down," she says.

In one picture (l), the AI removed a doll's house and changed the shape of a rug

A spokeswoman told the BBC that Howards had now introduced a policy of labelling all AI-enhanced photos. She added that the firm never used AI "to misrepresent the fundamental features of a property".

"It's about helping people imagine the possibilities, not changing the reality of what's being sold."

Trading Standards told the BBC that estate agents can use AI to make basic enhancements to photos, or to virtually furnish an empty room or garden, as long as they indicate they have used the technology.

But it says structurally changing a room, for example by adding a window, or hiding features could mislead consumers and break the law.

Jack Bushrod thinks all estate agents should have to clearly label AI-enhanced photos and mention any use of the tech prominently in the key features slide of a property listing.

He also expects to see more housefishing as time goes on.

"My worry is the more estate agents use AI, others will see the trend and jump on the bandwagon."

#170. OpenAI inks agreements with four independent evaluators to boost company's safety work

OpenAI on Monday announced it has signed agreements with four independent evaluators to help support the artificial intelligence company's safety work.

The evaluators, Apollo Research, METR, Transluce and Goodfire, will initially conduct distinct pilot assessments around model behavior and safeguards, OpenAI said. The company will give the organizations "deep access" to its internal systems as part of the agreements, adding that it plans to use what it learns to expand its work with independent experts going forward.

"We expect this first phase of assessments to lead to longer-term, consistent embedded engagements over time that can continuously provide third party safety signals and recommended improvements on our most load bearing safety claims and safety cases," OpenAI said in a blog post.

Independent evaluators have occupied a quiet corner of the multitrillion-dollar AI industry thus far, but they've taken on outsized importance following a series of high-profile safety and security incidents this year. The nascent ecosystem is still ironing out best practices, including how its work should be funded and what the reporting structures should ultimately look like.

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman pledged to grant independent evaluators "employee-like access" to the company in September, shortly after Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said his company would do so as part of his call to slow down the pace of advanced model development. Anthropic has since announced agreements with evaluators including Accenture and METR, and it said in October that it's engaging in discussions with others.

OpenAI's safety practices have been under intense scrutiny since July, when two of its models escaped containment, accessed the open internet and hacked a company called Hugging Face. Following the incident, OpenAI said it would invest more in its safeguards and alignment work, which is the process by which the company works to ensure that its models act in accordance with human interests and values.

The company said Monday that its pilot assessment with Apollo Research will explore whether its models covertly work against their developers' intentions, and whether its safeguards detect that behavior. Meanwhile, Goodfire will embed researchers within OpenAI to focus on training run monitoring, debugging and alignment, and METR intends to carry out two assessments focused on model alignment and monitoring.

OpenAI said Transluce will conduct an initial assessment of its post-training alignment evaluations, and that the organization plans to conduct an eight-week-long follow-on assessment with broader access to additional models and information next year.

The evaluators will get access to some of the "same internal resources" that OpenAI's own researchers use, including its source code, model checkpoints, training data and other documentation, according to the release. The organizations will also have access to relevant researchers and engineers within the company.

OpenAI said Apollo Research and Goodfire "accept financial compensation for their work," but that it is not contingent on the results of their evaluations. METR and Transluce do not accept compensation for their assessments, OpenAI said, but they might accept compute or API credits to help support their work.

"We do not believe that accepting financial compensation prevents assessors from maintaining independence — but we believe it is important to disclose this," OpenAI said.

The evaluators will be able to publish their findings, but OpenAI said it reserves the right to redact confidential information.

Disputes that arise between the company and the third parties will be overseen by OpenAI's Safety Advisory Group, which is a group of senior officials from across the company that assesses model capabilities and safeguards, and other OpenAI executives. If disagreements are not resolved, they'll be escalated to the Safety and Security Committee, an independent oversight committee of the OpenAI Foundation's board.

"We are committed to expanding this work and helping build the capacity and shared standards needed to sustain it," OpenAI said.

#169. Capstone Energy+, Inc. (CEPL) Q2 2027 Earnings Call Transcript

Capstone Energy+, Inc. (CEPL) Q2 2027 Earnings Call November 16, 2026 4:45 PM EST

Alfredo Gomez - General Counsel & SecretaryVincent Canino - CEO, President & DirectorJohn Miller - Interim CFO & Director

Eric Stine - Craig-Hallum Capital Group LLC, Research Division
