#168. Burnham says 'win-win' deal can be agreed between UK and EU

Andy Burnham said he believes a "win-win" deal can be agreed with the European Union, adding it could be "more like the Brexit that people thought they were voting for" in 2016.

The prime minister is exploring closer ties with the bloc and is expected to sign new agreements on food, energy and youth mobility at a UK-EU summit on 20 November.

Speaking after meeting European leaders in Ireland on Thursday, Burnham said he hopes a deal could help lower prices and boost the UK economy. He predicted the negotiations could "go down to the wire".

The Conservatives have previously said Labour are "dragging us into the European Union via the back door with their EU reset".

While Burnham has argued that Brexit has done "more harm than good", he has ruled out major changes to the UK's relationship with the EU before the next general election.

Labour's 2024 general election manifesto pledged there would be "no return to the single market, the customs union, or freedom of movement", and Burnham has committed to this.

The manifesto also said the party wanted to "make Brexit work" and reset the UK's relationship with the EU.

Former Prime Minister Sir Keir Starmer struck a deal in May 2025 covering fishing, trade and other areas and next week's summit will see the UK government seek to agree further changes.

Burnham has also made clear he wants to examine the "pros and cons" of the options for Britain's long-term relationship with the bloc.

Burnham's push for closer EU ties causes ripples in his party and others

UK cannot 'cherry pick' EU single market deal, Barnier tells BBC

Brexit referendum 'possible' in Labour election manifesto, says Burnham

Can Burnham avoid another Labour welfare rebellion?

Speaking to reporters on Thursday, Burnham was asked to explain if his decision to reopen the debate on the UK's future with Europe had helped or hindered the negotiations linked to the summit.

Burnham replied "I think it has helped" before adding: "We've got a big week ahead of us to try and get this deal over the line.

"But let's be clear what's at stake: what we can achieve through this deal is lower prices for British consumers, lower costs for British businesses, more growth in our economy so there's really big prizes here, and I will fight now over the days to come to make sure we get this deal over the line."

Pressed on whether discounted university tuition fees for European students would be part of the deal, Burnham said "requests are coming from the European side, we are putting our requests on the table too".

He added: "I believe there is a good deal to be done that could be a win-win for Britain and Europe. I think it actually could be more like the Brexit that people thought they were voting for in 2016."

Ahead of the summit, the UK and EU agreed a draft deal on removing post-Brexit checks from food and agriculture exports.

Similar texts for deals on emissions trading and a youth travel and work scheme have also been circulated to EU nations.

As a result of the agreement, the government claims the UK economy will eventually see Â£5.1bn worth of annual benefits.

Burnham also told MPs last month that the summit presents an opportunity to make progress on "controlling illegal immigration".

Conservative Party leader Kemi Badenoch has previously strongly opposed any move to align with EU rules, saying it would "actually take us backwards".

On Thursday, Conservative MP and former minister Dame Priti Patel said: "Andy Burnham's obsession with the EU risks dragging Britain backwards, damaging our economy and leaving hardworking British taxpayers footing the bill."

The Liberal Democrats earlier this month described the draft deal as a "patch, not a fix" and said the "real solution is to re-join the single market and to establish a customs union with the EU".

Reform UK believes one of the "key benefits" of Brexit is that Britain no longer has to "adhere to ridiculous EU rules" and any form of alignment could have a negative impact on the country's trade policy.

The Green Party has called for the UK to re-join the customs union, with leader Zack Polanski previously saying "full membership of the EU remains the best option for the UK".

#167. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.

#166. Nvidia's Colette Kress confronts key questions in first earnings report since Jensen Huang’s exit

Days after Jensen Huang's abrupt departure from the chipmaker he founded 33 years ago, Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress will now face intense questions about what comes next.

Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly earnings after the close on Tuesday. While the company is again expected to show explosive growth as the key infrastructure provider to the artificial intelligence boom, Kress and her team will likely be forced to answer questions on chip sales to China, the risks tied to deals with neoclouds and what comes next under new leadership.

Huang resigned Thursday, but Nvidia's legions of customers, partners and investors still rely on the company to continue shipping large quantities of graphics processing units and AI systems. Kress, who has been Nvidia's CFO since 2013, was tapped to lead the company while the board searches for a successor.

Analysts expect Nvidia to report 91% revenue growth to $109.1 billion, according to LSEG, with $2.47 in earnings per share.

Kress, alongside Huang, offered a full-year growth forecast suggesting that the company's historic expansion is poised to continue. They said revenue expansion in fiscal 2028 would be roughly 70% in what Kress, on the last earnings call, described as a "supply constrained outlook."

"What would be good for them to do is reiterate that and confirm that is still intact, if they have the ability," said Bloomberg Intelligence analyst Kunjan Sobhani.

But Kress has to do far more than just put up numbers. The stock has dropped for six straight weeks, the longest losing streak since 2012, pushing the company's market cap down by $1.7 trillion.

Here are some key issues Kress has to address:

Huang's public downfall began in October, when The New York Times reported that American intelligence agencies were surveilling a woman named Alice Huang, who was close with the former CEO, believing she was a Chinese intelligence asset. The report alleged that Alice Huang was helping smuggle Nvidia's premium chips into China, violating U.S. export controls.

An Nvidia spokesperson pushed back on the report saying, "We're confident that if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know."

Nvidia has also insisted that it gets essentially no data center sales from China, consistent with how it reports sales in its recent Securities and Exchange Commission filings.

But Jensen Huang's romantic ties to Alice Huang, a Megaspeed International executive, raised concerns about Nvidia's actual business in China. A big part of Nvidia's sales to Southeast Asia, according to some industry experts, are intended for Chinese companies, either through smuggling the chips to the mainland or by building data centers for Chinese clients in Singapore or Malaysia.

In a statement Sunday, a Megaspeed representative said that the cloud provider is conducting a thorough internal investigation, and that Alice Huang left the company in 2025.

"Megaspeed designs and maintains its global operational and supply-chain control frameworks to comply with all applicable laws and regulatory regimes, including United States export control requirements and local jurisdiction rules in every market where we operate," Megaspeed said in the statement.

Culper Research, a short selling research firm that published a note on Nvidia's "China problem" in May, alleges that 20% of Nvidia's fiscal 2026 compute revenue was "driven by China."

Nvidia has denied any diversion, and said it has no evidence that any such activity has taken place. But that was before last week's scandal.

Investors will be listening closely to what Kress says about the market for Nvidia's AI chips. Some analysts expect the government to pursue new rules that would force Nvidia to more closely track its customers and how they plan to use the company's critical technology.

Kress might not be able to say much, as politicians in Washington, D.C., are ramping up investigations of Nvidia and calling executives to testify in front of Congress.

Sen. Ron Wyden, D-Ore., and Rep. Richard Neal. D-Mass., are probing the administration's approvals for Nvidia chip sales to Chinese companies and asked the Commerce Department to save any communications regarding Nvidia and Jensen Huang.

Other representatives are asking law enforcement agencies to look into Huang's tryst with the alleged Chinese intelligence asset.

Jensen Huang said he would testify in a closed-door meeting with the chairs of the House Foreign Affairs, China and Intelligence committees. The committees are also looking to meet with current Nvidia executives, including Tim Teter, the general counsel.

If government officials are able to prove that Nvidia knew that its Southeast Asian clients like Megaspeed were pass-throughs to Chinese companies, the scandal could grow even deeper.

"The real story here should be, did Nvidia know their client?" Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in an interview with CNBC on Friday. "And was there a robust effort at Nvidia to make sure that there was no leakage and that they were selling to what were obviously pass-through shell companies?"

Huang was singular in creating a new class of companies called neoclouds, which competed with Nvidia's hyperscaler customers — Microsoft, Amazon, Google, Oracle and SpaceX — by offering Nvidia compute to smaller AI labs and startups.

Last quarter, Huang said the company's neocloud business, which it called ACIE, would drive Nvidia's growth going forward.

But Nvidia had a unique relationship to the neoclouds, which are often new or nascent businesses that need upfront capital in order to secure land, power and Nvidia chips. Nvidia has acted as supplier, investor and financier to these companies.

Huang was integral in deciding who got chips first and publicly pushed to expand Nvidia's role in financing major data center infrastructure.

For example, Huang in August announced a program on CNBC to help these types of companies as well as frontier labs access financing from six major financial firms. Huang was central to the effort, which has yet to result in a completed deal, and it's unclear whether Kress has the same ability to rally Wall Street, or if she has the enthusiasm to keep using Nvidia's balance sheet to sell more chips.

"Jensen did all of these innovative deals when it comes to supporting, for example, neoclouds, using his relationships, using his balance sheet," said Sobhani, the Bloomberg Intelligence analyst. "Those deals could slow down."

Jensen Huang built Nvidia and has acted as its sole figurehead. But while he's been key to helping develop the AI market, the demand now is massive regardless of who's in charge. Nvidia's high-end GPUs are essential for training AI models and can be used to serve models in a process called inference.

Many investors have looked at Nvidia's earnings as a proxy for overall AI demand. Huang said in August that "demand was accelerating." Kress echoed the point, saying, "Incredibly, we are seeing demand acceleration even at our scale."

Nvidia's board has said it expects Kress to deliver on Nvidia's commitments to shareholders, customers and partners, but investors worry how long that can last. Morgan Stanley analysts cut their estimates for calendar 2027 and 2028 data center sales by about 10% on the Huang news.

"We feel certain that we are cutting to an overly conservative number," the analysts wrote, noting that it leaves a lot of room for weaker demand. "But faced with some uncertainty and multiple downgrades, we would rather err on the side of being too conservative."

Nvidia has spent or committed to spend heavily on memory, wafers and other key components for its products.

In its most recent filing, Nvidia said it committed to $92 billion in supply and capacity for the rest of this fiscal year, $87 billion in the fiscal year ending January 2028, and $88 billion in the fiscal year ending January 2029.

Kress is well known to investors and has appeared on the company's conference calls for years, but Tuesday's earnings will be different — she's running the show now.

In the past, Kress would read off prepared remarks about the quarter's results and the company's forecast, then turn it over to Huang, who would singlehandedly answer analyst questions, riffing on everything from the company's technology to the broader AI ecosystem.

Kress sent a short note to staff on the day Huang resigned, but Tuesday's earnings will be the first time Nvidia's external audience will hear from her about the CEO transition, the company's search for a successor, upcoming launch events, or how she and the board are trying to maintain Nvidia's culture and technological lead.

#165. Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has been summoned to testify next month before a Senate committee about his alleged affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Huang abruptly quit on Thursday as head of one of the world's most valuable companies after revelations of alleged extramarital affairs, in a stunning fall from grace that sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who is under scrutiny over exports of sensitive Nvidia AI chips to China, has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December.

Nvidia has lost $550bn (Â£415bn) in stock market value in just five days as the scandal dominated headlines.

Senator Tim Scott sent letters on Thursday to Huang and Lutnick requesting their testimony in front of the Senate Banking Committee.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," the South Carolina Republican posted on X.

"The American people deserve answers," he added.

Scott asked Huang to appear voluntarily on 3 December, but also warned the panel could issue a subpoena to compel his testimony.

Three House of Representatives committees - on intelligence and foreign affairs and a panel on China - have also sent a letter to Huang summoning him for a closed-door interview on 1 December.

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

Lutnick is under scrutiny because unnamed officials in President Donald Trump's administration have been briefing US media that the commerce secretary stalled efforts to tighten export restrictions on advanced AI chips to China.

A commerce department spokesperson told US media that Lutnick had "proactively" agreed to appear before the Senate Banking Committee.

Huang, who has been Nvidia's public face since 1993, admitted on Sunday to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang.

On Wednesday, he was reported to have had a second relationship, this time with an Nvidia executive.

Alice Huang (no relation) worked as an executive at the Singaporean firm Megaspeed. She has been suspected of being an intelligence asset for China, according to the New York Times. China has rejected that allegation as a smear.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December

US Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday the Department of Justice had not yet drawn any conclusions about whether Alice Huang was engaged in espionage for Beijing.

Nvidia officials have repeatedly declined to comment since Wednesday. A personal representative of Huang also declined to comment on Thursday.

According to US media, Huang's alleged second affair was with a long-time Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reported directly to him within the company.

Sources have told the BBC that Nvidia employees had noticed the pair were unusually close.

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Huang had been widely expected to weather the storm after the first affair became public, even as Nvidia announced it had launched an investigation into whether its CEO had been "the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

One current Nvidia staff member told the BBC that the initial reports of his infidelity were more or less brushed off by rank-and-file workers inside the company.

It wasn't until Huang's alleged affair with the Nvidia executive became public that workers started to feel "this is crazy", the person said.

Another Nvidia employee said they had been told not to speak with the press or otherwise comment on the situation in public.

Many staff have earned millions of dollars in compensation through their ability to cash out their Nvidia shares as the stock soared.

Nvidia has said it has "no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised", but pledged that its independent inquiry would look at all available information.

The company's meteoric rise had been led by Jensen Huang, who became a major booster of the AI industry, which relies on Nvidia for the powerful computer chips it manufactures.

Initially those chips were designed for computer gaming.

But as AI technology evolved in the last four years into chatbots and large language models, companies building AI tools suddenly required huge numbers of the Nvidia computer chips that could process large amounts of data.

Nvidia is now hunting a permanent replacement for Huang. Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress was named on Thursday as interim CEO.

Several tech insiders that the BBC spoke with this week noted that Huang's downfall has been a major topic of conversation in San Francisco Bay area circles, an area filled with tech employees, industry executives and billionaires.

Huang has been married to Lori Huang for 40 years. Both of their children are also executives at Nvidia.

Nvidia's stock fall this week is the most significant five-day drop the company has seen since going public 27 years ago.

Its market value rose by more than 470% in the past five years, peaking just a few weeks ago. It is now down by 31% against a backdrop of wider uncertainty in the tech sector.

#163. Pressure grows on Trump's Commerce Secretary Lutnick in fallout from Nvidia scandal

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has blamed Howard Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to properly oversee the export of high-spec AI chips, as the fallout from the Nvidia scandal widens to President Donald Trump's cabinet.

Pressure is building on Lutnick from across the political spectrum to explain his department's actions.

Jensen Huang was ousted as Nvidia CEO after revelations he had an affair with a businesswoman who was also allegedly a Chinese spy.

Megaspeed, the firm she worked for, had been probed by US government agencies over suspicions it was smuggling Nvidia chips into China, reports said, but was not on a list of firms US companies are prevented from working with.

Bessent told CNBC several agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

Bessent's comments come after Lutnick was called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over the Commerce Department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

Other congressional committees have asked both Lutnick and his department to retain all records relating to Nvidia and Huang.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said some parts of the administration wanted more controls on trade with Megaspeed

Bessent said he was "frustrated" by delays in putting the company on the export control list and reports of leakage of AI chips into China.

"President Trump made it very clear that China should not receive any Nvidia chips above H100s, so any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts our great companies in the AI race, because it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Last year, the Commerce Department had been investigating Megaspeed over suspicions it had been smuggling some of Nvidia's advanced AI chips into China, according to the New York Times. Megaspeed has been contacted for comment.

There was also pressure on the Commerce Department to add it to the list, but the Financial Times separately reported that the department was delaying efforts to have the cloud computing firm included.

However, Bessent also pointed the finger at Nvidia.

"The story has been thus far of Jensen Huang's personal foibles," he told CNBC, external.

"The real story here should be: did Nvidia know their client, and was there a robust effort at Nvidia to make sure that there was no leakage and that they were selling to what were obviously pass-through shell companies?

"It's important that anyone at the company there be held accountable for that."

Bessent's pointed comments are a rare instance of internal debate within the Trump administration spilling out into the public view, although tensions have reportedly simmered between Bessent and Lutnick in the past.

In 2024, the two reportedly jostled for the role of treasury secretary ahead of Trump's return to the White House, and last year the pair butted heads over the administration's tariff policy, according to US media.

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

Huang has been summoned to testify before the Senate Banking Committee about his alleged affair with the former Megaspeed executive, who is suspected of being a Chinese spy.

The commerce secretary has also been summoned to appear before the committee in December.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," committee chairman Republican Senator Tim Scott posted on X.

Democrats on key congressional committees have asked the Commerce Department and Lutnick to keep all records relating to decisions to allow Nvidia to sell its advanced AI chips to Chinese companies, including all their communications with or about Nvidia and Huang.

Ways and Means Committee Ranking Member Richard E Neal and Senate Finance Committee Ranking Member Ron Wyden are starting investigations into the way Nvidia was allowed to sell these chips.

They say Nvidia's advanced H200 chips could be sold to Chinese firms if they were first imported from their manufacturing facility in Taiwan into the US for inspection.

A 25% tariff - a levy paid by US importers to the federal government - was imposed on those chips under a section of the Trade Act that Neal and Wyden say is meant to allow the US president to take action against imports that risk national security.

"Trump's use of the statute appears to have done the opposite, undermining US national security and putting Americans at risk," they said.

#162. Why is Microsoft's outage lasting so long and will emails be lost?

Watch: What we know so far about the Microsoft outage

A major Microsoft outage has impacted schools, healthcare systems, businesses, courts and governments around the world.

Services including Teams, Outlook and AI software Copilot, are being restored almost 24 hours since issues first surfaced.

Microsoft said the outage was not a cyber-security incident and was caused by a technical issue. It has apologised for the disruption.

Here's what we know about the services affected and what went wrong.

Schools, GPs and businesses hit by global Microsoft outage as company scrambles to solve issue

Some Microsoft 365 services have been affected by "connectivity problems" with problems reported in at least 74 countries.

From late on Thursday, customers reported varying levels of access - many were unable to send or receive emails, view certain files or details on their calendar apps.

But some users were able to use Teams, messaging and online meeting functions without issues.

As of Friday afternoon, Microsoft said it had seen "encouraging recovery progress" in Europe and improvement in North America.

The company added that it was focused on "addressing the remaining areas of disruption" but warned "some users may still experience intermittent issues".

In a later statement, the company said it was continuing to see improvements "with email queues significantly reduced and approaching normal levels".

The company attributed the ongoing outage to an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade.

"We have addressed the underlying issue and continue to restore service to remaining customers and regions," it said on Friday.

Although services are beginning to resume, Microsoft has warned of continued "intermittent access issues", meaning some users will still struggle to load their inboxes and could experience "delayed email delivery".

In other words, it is not quite safe to assume every email you send is going to arrive in its intended recipient's inbox right away.

Earlier, Microsoft said it would be extending the "timeout window" to 48 hours "to help protect delayed messages as delivery catches up".

The email timeout window refers to the amount of time Outlook will wait to receive an email.

When they do show up, the timestamp will be when they were delivered rather than when they were sent.

Although email service Outlook appears to be resuming, the outage has so far lasted for more than 20 hours - in outage terms that is considered a long time.

Prof James Ferryman from the University of Reading's department of computer science said the details the company has disclosed provide some clues.

"What Microsoft has told us is that the part of its servers that sets up secure connections is getting swamped, and once that happens the server just stops answering," he said.

"The trouble is that this kind of failure snowballs.

"One server falls over, its work gets passed to the next one, and meanwhile huge numbers of phones and laptops keep trying to get back in.

"You can't just turn it all back on at once. You have to bring it back bit by bit."

The challenge is therefore not just fixing the fault, but managing the huge surge of devices trying to reconnect once services start coming back online.

Gergely Orosz, a former leading software engineer at several major tech companies including Skype and Uber, said this could be the "most disruptive" business email outage "of all time".

He said it was still difficult to know just how many people have been affected by the disruption, but added he "cannot recall" an outage over business email ever taking this long to return to normal.

Australian cyber-security expert Troy Hunt told the BBC the outage could have far-reaching consequences.

"Microsoft is a massive part of the internet backbone and reminds us how critical email still is.

"Not only does this outage affect many people's ability to work but also things like the ability to communicate about appointments, purchase things that need verification, create or access accounts and services," he said.

In a statement, Microsoft said it was "deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners.

"We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously. We are seeing broad recovery and stability across our services."

More than 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps according to Microsoft's website. The full scale of the outage has not been confirmed.

Microsoft 365 is used by companies worldwide and is expected to have a knock-on effect to other services.

Tech giants OpenAI and Anthropic both earlier reported problems as a result of the outage.

ChatGPT-maker OpenAI said it was investigating an issue affecting its connections to Microsoft's services, which had caused some tools to not work as expected.

From city governments in the US to Australian spy agencies, disruption across the globe has been widespread.

Courts from Los Angeles to Texas earlier warned that communications might be delayed.

In Canada, the public broadcaster CBC reported that a healthcare network running several hospitals in Toronto was experiencing email issues, but that patient care was not affected.

The government of Japan also said its shared IT platform had been disrupted.

Across the UK, GP surgeries, pharmacies, police and schools have been affected by the outage.

Some GP surgeries had urged patients not to submit messages via their website while police forces were also earlier asking the public to call if they want to make a report.

Have you or your business been affected by the outage?

#160. Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

#159. This Anthropic proxy play is getting traders' attention – here's where they see it going

Who says you can't tailgate an IPO?Traders are preparing for this week's blockbuster Anthropic IPO in different ways, including buying options on one publicly-traded fund that invests in private assets.The Destiny Tech100 (DXYZ) is a publicly-traded, closed-end fund that invests in equity, equity-linked instruments, or shares of private funds and venture companies to gain exposure to private companies. Currently, 14% of the fund's assets are tied to Anthropic, according to the company's website.Options volume on the fund jumped to more than 10x the 30-day average Monday, according to Cboe LiveVol data. Of the 42,300 contracts traded by midday, 41,800 were calls, with just 141 puts likely opened by buyers.

Just shy of $5.5 million in options premium on DXYZ traded on Monday, with almost all of it tied to calls, but just marginally more spent buying than selling calls, according to SpotGamma. The highest-volume contract is currently the 30-strike call expiring Friday, a 90-cent contract that needs the fund to rally 19% into the weekend to pay off.Traders may want to be wary of how the fund traded around the SpaceX IPO: DXYZ surged almost 150% in May before unwinding all the gains in the weeks ahead of SpaceX's debut. Since then, the fund has barely moved, trading around $26.There are other bullish signs surrounding Anthropic's IPO in the derivatives markets. Current prices for perpetual futures on the blockchain exchange Hyperliquid are implying an opening price of $98, a big pop from the $70-$78 price range quoted in the company's amended S1 filing. The AI giant is expected to price Wednesday evening and begin trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday under the ticker ANT.

#158. Microsoft says services fully restored after day-long global outage

Products such as Microsoft Teams and Outlook have been out of service for much of Friday

Microsoft has said its services have been fully restored after a worldwide outage affected several of its tools, causing disruption.

The tech firm said early on Saturday morning that efforts to address the underlying problem had been completed and customers using Outlook, Teams and Copilot should no longer be affected.

The outage hit more than 70 countries, disrupting everything from business operations to government services and personal users. More than 430 million people use the affected products worldwide.

Microsoft apologised for the technical issue, which it said was introduced during a "network capacity upgrade" and was not caused by a cyber-attack or an artificial intelligence agent.

Experts said the event was a "stark reminder" of how heavily businesses and vital services rely on a small group of big tech providers.

Users began reporting they were having trouble accessing Microsoft 365 products on Thursday.

By Friday, the far-reaching issue was affecting institutions from the Japanese government and the Philippines Supreme Court to the Australian spy agency, and trains, hospitals and courts in North America.

Services were beginning to recover by Friday afternoon UK time, nearly 24 hours after the issues first began to emerge.

Why is Microsoft's outage lasting so long and will emails be lost?

Courts, spies and MPs: How the Microsoft outage has been felt around the world

In the UK, some parts of the NHS were affected, as were some schools and police forces.

UK cyber minister Liz Lloyd said the incident had "limited" impact on critical frontline services such as 999 calls and that there had been no disruption to the transport network.

In Canada, however, the national rail network said passengers may not be able to receive email updates about their trips, and hospitals across the country also had issues with emails.

In the US, staff members from the House of Representatives told Politico it had set back an entire day of legislative work as everything was on Microsoft's Outlook.

Singapore's government acknowledged some of its systems had been impacted, and in India, the outage disrupted work for many on Friday.

In Turkey, advisers and officials attending the COP31 climate summit in Antalya were left frustrated by being unable to access Outlook.

The issues also affected smaller businesses and individuals.

Pete says his business has been brought to a standstill

Peter Pentz, a winemaker in South Africa, said his entire business had been effectively brought to a "standstill".

His winery's communication with exports was also brought to a standstill, he said, and "critical production work" had been halted as the Cape West Coast winery "relies on these systems for parts of our day-to-day operations".

Georgia from Staffordshire in England said she had been due to complete her first house purchase after waiting eight months to get to this point - but the outage has delayed that.

"The solicitor mentioned there may be a delay due to not being able to contact other agencies," she said.

"We're not the only one completing today - there are hundreds of houses and for them to ring everyone in time is unfeasible."

Microsoft first acknowledged users in the Americas were unable to access Exchange Online mailboxes at 17:54 GMT on Thursday, and shortly after warned the disruption was possibly affecting Microsoft 365 globally.

It launched an investigation and engineers began working to restore services, but by 04:36 on Friday Microsoft was reporting only a "partial recovery" in some parts of the world.

By 13:32, the tech giant said there had been "broader improvements" in North America, "sustained improvements" across much of Europe and "encouraging recovery signals" in the Asia Pacific region.

Microsoft said it restored services gradually due to the scale of the problem and to help ensure services remained stable once they were up and running again.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners," a Microsoft spokesperson said.

"We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously."

A full post-incident report will be released once Microsoft has completed a review.

Experts said that while workers may have been happy to not have email access for a working day, the incident showed the dependence many organisations have on these cloud-based services.

"The global Microsoft outage is a stark reminder of how heavily we rely on a handful of big tech providers," said Rob Demain, chief executive of cybersecurity firm e2e-assure.

Matt Saunders, field chief technology officer at tech consultancy Adaptavist, said it would be challenging to mitigate against a similar problem.

"Classic IT disaster planning strategies suggest using a second service for resilience, but in practice, it's almost impossible for a business to function without tools such as Microsoft," he said.

"In the wake of this outage, organisations must have a plan in place for how to work without such tools, for when incidents like this occur."

#156. How the Jensen Huang scandal created a headache for business-friendly Singapore

A major scandal involving Nvidia founder Jensen Huang and a suspected Chinese spy based in Singapore has cast a spotlight on the city-state's success as a global chip hub - and a location for alleged illicit trade.

On Thursday, Huang stepped down from the US chip giant after admitting he had an affair. He was reportedly involved with a woman named Alice Huang, who ran Singaporean data centre company Megaspeed, a major buyer of Nvidia's chips.

For decades, Singapore has painstakingly cultivated a reputation as an open, business-friendly economy offering easy access to both the US and Chinese markets.

With the AI boom, it has become a major hub for the trade of high-end semiconductor chips in recent years.

Singapore was the second-largest billing centre globally last year for Nvidia, accounting for nearly a fifth of what the US chip giant earns.

But notably, less than 1% of its earnings were for chips that actually entered Singapore, as the island acts as a hub for many Asian companies to place orders and send them elsewhere.

Since 2022, when the US restricted advanced American semiconductors from being exported to China or acquired by Chinese companies, US officials have been concerned that other countries such as Singapore could be used by Chinese companies to gain access to the chips.

It became the biggest buyer of Nvidia's chips in South East Asia and is suspected of giving backdoor access to these chips to Chinese companies via its data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Singapore has said that while it does not enforce "unilateral export controls imposed by other countries" it expects all companies in its jurisdiction to respect such regulations.

The city-state has had some success in policing this.

It has charged four companies and five individuals - including Chinese nationals - for various offences including fraud, following US concerns that Chinese AI company DeepSeek had been buying Nvidia chips from third parties in Singapore, among other places.

They also tried to probe Megaspeed, but were faced with a number of difficulties.

In a statement released on Thursday, the government said that since February 2025, they had been looking into whether Megaspeed and other firms had made false declarations about data servers which were purchased and exported through Singapore.

The companies stated the equipment would end up in neighbouring Malaysia, but Singapore authorities suspect the equipment's final destinations were elsewhere.

Singapore said it sought assistance from US authorities several times to get more information from one of the investigated companies - Supermicro, a US-based company which the BBC understands was supplying computer servers to Megaspeed.

But "despite these repeated requests, Singapore has to date received limited information or assistance", it said in its statement. The BBC has sought comment from the US State Department.

Singapore authorities said they had also investigated Alice Huang - not for espionage, but for any possible role in Megaspeed's suspected fraud.

In the end, they found "insufficient evidence" of crime and let her leave Singapore in February this year, after holding her passport for several months.

Since launching their probe nearly two years ago, Singapore has yet to bring any charges against Megaspeed or any of the companies and individuals linked to the case.

Singapore and US investigations into Megaspeed are still ongoing.

In 2024, US officials had paid a visit to a Malaysian data centre owned by Megaspeed and saw Nvidia technology still in their boxes, raising concerns that Megaspeed was shipping high-end chips out elsewhere, according to a report by the New York Times.

Last year, Megaspeed appeared to have imported 50,000 more chips than Nvidia could account for, according to a Bloomberg report. Nvidia later adjusted its own figures after visiting more warehouses.

Megaspeed has maintained it has always acted lawfully and that previous inspections by authorities found no evidence of illegal activity.

On Thursday, the company said it "welcomes appropriate government scrutiny" and that it is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government".

China has also long rebuffed accusations that its companies and institutions are illegally obtaining high-end US chips.

Jensen Huang resigned from Nvidia on Thursday following a board meeting

How Megaspeed and Alice Huang came to Singapore highlights other vulnerabilities stemming from the city-state's openness.

In recent years, the city-state has become a prime destination for Chinese companies, with tech firms such as TikTok and Manus making it their global headquarters. Besides Singapore's ease of doing business, it has a population that is mostly ethnically Chinese and largely views China positively.

For a Chinese firm, Singapore offers a base near the mainland and a "neutral address" that global customers and investors accept, said Winston Ma, adjunct professor at New York University's School of Law.

But critics say this has given rise to "Singapore-washing", where Chinese companies use the city-state's clean image to obscure their origins, reduce suspicions and even cover up dubious activities.

Washington-based security analysis group C4ADS told the BBC they had assessed Alice Huang's setup of Megaspeed to be such a case.

BBC checks of corporate records in China, Hong Kong and Singapore found that in 2019, Huang sold a gaming company and its subsidiaries to Chinese gaming firm 7Road.

In March 2023, a 7Road subsidiary in Singapore named Huang as its executive director. The next day it changed its name - to Megaspeed.

Huang was in charge for eight months before she transferred the company to a Singaporean man.

During this period, Megaspeed went through "a critical period of corporate restructuring and regional expansion", and her corporate relationships raised questions about whether "Megaspeed's Singaporean identity is genuine or effectively Chinese-controlled", according to C4ADS.

Even after she transferred Megaspeed, C4ADS assessed that she was still "practically in control" of the company, based on her links to Megaspeed via another company as well as LinkedIn posts where she continued to identify herself as Megaspeed's "chairwoman" at conferences.

Megaspeed has not answered BBC queries regarding the company's and Alice Huang's links to China and the Chinese government.

Alice Huang, seen here in Taipei with Jensen Huang last year, ran Megaspeed for several months in 2023

All this reinforces the dilemma faced by Singapore.

"For the longest time, Singapore has managed to enjoy the best of both worlds with excellent relations with US and China," Nanyang Technological University politics and international studies lecturer Dylan Loh said.

Singapore has long positioned itself as a neutral party between the two superpowers. Its prime minister Lawrence Wong has insisted that his country is neither pro-China nor pro-America, but "pro-Singapore".

But Loh says this balancing act "has now fast become very difficult to manage and sometimes quite fraught too" with the US-China dispute over chips.

With the Megaspeed case, the tightrope Singapore has walked is set to get even narrower, according to analysts.

Beijing may ask why Singapore is investigating issues pertaining to US restrictions and whether they are siding with the US' interests, says Chong Ja Ian, a Singaporean political scientist and non-resident scholar with Carnegie China.

Washington, in turn, might read Singapore's latest statement as "pushing blame" on the US, which could lead to backlash from the White House, he said.

"Singapore has made a policy position not to take sides. But by not choosing a side, they run the risk of being seen as complicit" in either side's agenda, he said.

But Benjamin Ho, an assistant professor and China expert at Singapore's S Rajaratnam School of International Studies, noted that this was "not the first or last time Singapore will be caught between the US and China".

"This is the price that an open economy has to grapple with - you get caught in the middle when things turn sour."

Additional reporting by Jack Lau of the BBC's Global China Unit.

#155. Market's Nvidia obsession obscuring an opportunity in this name

The market is unable to divert its attention from anything not related to the ongoing saga that is Nvidia and its embattled former CEO Jensen Huang. It's understandable, given both the detail of the story and Nvidia impact on the market. It remains the tech story du jour, and it's getting plenty of ink.

I think investors should remember there are other big names reporting this week outside of Nvidia that deserve time and attention. One such name is The Walt Disney Company (DIS).

After a prolonged period of strategic transitions, the company may finally be turning the corner. Driven by a renewed focus on the bottom line, management's cost discipline now supports a runway for double-digit earnings-per-share (EPS) gains through fiscal 2027, even as it continues to trade at about its 5-year average forward multiple of 14.3x, well below the broad market multiple of more than 18x. Trading back to a market multiple, justified if it establishes above-market EPS growth, suggests ~26% upside, which is also consistent with a sum-of-the-parts analysis that points to an intrinsic valuation of $140 to $160 per share versus the Friday, November 13th closing price of $118.97.

Despite broader macroeconomic anxieties, the theme park and cruise divisions are showing surprisingly resilient demand. Domestically, the metrics are highly encouraging: attendance is climbing, and more importantly, per-capita spending continues to rise.

On the digital front, Direct-to-Consumer (DTC) Disney's streaming margins currently sit in the low teens, respectable given the segment's previous lack of profits. But the real upside scenario requires looking ahead: if Disney can scale these DTC margins to 20% or higher, the stock could at least trade at a multiple comparable to streaming king Netflix (which would imply a share price of ~ $139 or so.

With linear TV stabilizing, the parks firing on all cylinders, and streaming marching toward higher profitability, Disney offers a highly asymmetric risk-reward profile. The magic is returning to Disney's financials, and the current share price heavily discounts a business fundamentally engineered for double-digit growth.

Right now, the options market is pricing a ~6% move by the end of the week (the company reports Tuesday, pre-open), which is below the 8 quarter median move of 7.4%. Anticipating a "vol crush" after earnings, I like selling December premium to help finance the purchase of a longer-dated call - a diagonal call/spread risk reversal.

When selecting an option trade consider the key things that affect its value and how those things differ from simply buying (or selling) the underlying asset. For these option trades, let's consider three sensitivities (Greeks): Delta, the rate at which the option position changes in value for changes in the underlying price. Theta, or decay, which measures how much the value of an option declines as time passes, and Vega, the change in an option's price associated with changes in the implied volatility of an option.

In the case of the diagonal risk reversal each of these factors plays into the structure to our benefit. The delta of the structure is less than 100, meaning that the p&l associated with moves up or down will be less for one spread than they would be for 100 shares of stock.

We like this because the stock has already had a decent run recently, and the option structure will lose less than the stock will if it gives that back. Because nearer dated expiration decay more rapidly as a percentage of the options value than longer dated options do (and out of the money options also exhibit accelerated decay), the shorter dated December options we're selling should decay in value more quickly than the longer dated March options we are buying, even though the March option has a higher premium. Vega can work in our favor as well due to "vol crush". When option premiums are elevated due to a catalyst such as earnings we should expect that much of that premium will be sucked out or crushed once the event has passed.

This will impact both the December and March options to a degree, but remember that the March options also capture another earnings event in February, so some measure of event variance (elevated option premium) will remain in the March expiration to account for that.

#153. Minister backs legal right to job support for young people

Watch: Pat McFadden refuses to rule out that some young people might lose benefits

The work and pensions secretary has backed the idea that the government should legally guarantee young people help to get into employment.

Pat McFadden told the BBC that job support should be given a "legislative underpinning", adding the level of assistance on offer was currently "vague".

McFadden also confirmed that some current benefit claimants would see their current entitlement to benefits reduced when they are reassessed.

But his Conservative counterpart Helen Whately said the idea of legally guaranteeing support "doesn't make sense" and ministers should focus instead on reducing taxes paid by employers.

It comes ahead of a long-awaited review by former Labour cabinet minister Alan Milburn, which will recommend on Tuesday that under-25s should be guaranteed job placements or training to help them find permanent work.

His report is also expected to say that under-25s should no longer be able to claim the health element of UK-wide universal credit - or personal independence payment (Pip), the main disability benefit in England and Wales.

Some could be eligible for a new "additional cost allowance" to cover day-to-day costs related to health conditions, under the plans.

Speaking to Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg, Milburn said the government should divert money away from the benefits system to boost work support, adding he wanted to see "billions" extra spent on help over the next decade.

However, the idea of reducing the entitlement of existing benefit claimants could prove controversial among Labour MPs, who scuppered a previous benefits-cutting plan under Sir Keir Starmer last year.

Milburn said the reduced entitlement for some should be accompanied by a new "duty on government" to provide jobs support to young people, such as apprenticeships, college places, job coaching, or a job.

He added that the temporary nature of previous job support schemes, including the Kickstart programme during the Covid pandemic, meant that they had been less effective as reducing long-term joblessness.

How the new guarantee would work in practice is not yet clear, with McFadden adding it would have to be "carefully" drafted.

Although local councils have some specific legal duties, including in the areas of child protection and homelessness, duties on central government currently tend to be much broader, such as operating a "comprehensive" health service.

Watch: Helen Whately says legal guarantee of work or training for under-25s 'doesn't make sense'

Asked on the same programme whether he agreed with the idea of a guarantee, McFadden said: "I do think we need some legislative underpinning to this."

He added that the offer of support "needs to be more firm," adding: "People need more certainty about what they are entitled to."

He confirmed that the government planned to move younger claimants onto a new benefits system when they undergo scheduled reassessment, and some of them would not receive "what they would get today".

Other claimants, such as those with long-term disabilities, would receive an increased amount, he added.

Also speaking to Kuenssberg, shadow work and pensions secretary Helen Whately said her party backed moves to reduce welfare spending.

But she criticised the proposed legal guarantee of work support, arguing it might "sound a nice thing to do," but ministers would be better off reducing taxes and regulations on businesses to help youngsters find "real jobs".

She added it was "absurd" the government had increased taxes for employers in order to "subsidise things that look like jobs". She added: "Just stop doing the taxing.

Elsewhere in his interview, former health secretary Milburn said:

a sustainable reduction in welfare spending cannot be achieved in two or three years, warning that "crude cuts" to the benefits system would create other additional costs for government

GPs should not be responsible for signing sick notes declaring people unable to work

ministers should "look very carefully" at Labour's manifesto commitment to phase out the reduced minimum wage for those aged 18-21, adding that moves towards this had "imposed a lot of cost" on firms

It comes as the government announced it will further widen eligibly for its scheme that offers jobless under-25s subsidised six-month job roles on the minimum wage.

From next April, eligible claimants will be offered a role after 12 months of unsuccessfully looking for work, rather 18 currently.

It is the second time that eligibility for the scheme has been widened, after the maximum age was increased from 21 to 24 earlier this year.

#151. Suspected China spy linked to Nvidia's ex-boss left in 2025, Megaspeed says

The Nvidia scandal has sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley as well as the political establishment

Alice Huang, a suspected China spy who has been linked to Nvidia's former boss Jensen Huang, left her executive role at Megaspeed in August last year, the company says.

Jensen Huang resigned as chief executive of the AI chip-making giant Nvidia after it emerged he had allegedly had affairs with Alice Huang as well as with one of his employees.

Megaspeed is the biggest buyer of Nvidia chips in South East Asia and the scandal at Nvidia has raised questions about the possibility that high-end AI chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

The Singapore-based company said on Sunday it had launched an internal investigation into its business.

Megaspeed said it was aware of reports "containing assertions about our business, supply chain and global operations that are not supported by verifiable evidence".

It added that it "conducts legitimate and lawful business" and has "retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation".

Alice Huang stepped down from her role as director of Megaspeed near the end of 2023, according to Washington-based security analysis group C4ADS but it said she was still "practically in control" of the company.

Alice Huang - who is of no relation to Nvidia's former boss - had been investigated by Singapore authorities in February 2025 as part of the country's probe into Megaspeed and other companies.

Also on Sunday, US Congressman John Moolenaar said that Jensen Huang had agreed to testify before the House Select Committee on China, which he chairs, as well as the Foreign Affairs and the Intelligence committees.

The committees had threatened to issue subpoenas to Jensen Huang if he did not comply with their requests to testify.

Moolenaar told CBS, BBC's US partner, that none were ultimately issued.

Jensen Huang's testimony is expected to take place behind close doors, with the dates still being finalised.

Moolenaar said: "That's going to be a very important meeting to understand to what degree privileged information was accessed, and to what degree this potential alleged Chinese spy influenced Nvidia's philosophy and may have had an influence on some of our policies with respect to AI and export controls."

Separately, a group of tech leaders including Space X's Elon Musk, have been trying to rally support to have Jensen Huang reinstated as Nvidia's chief executive.

A representative forJensen Huang declined to comment on a report in the Financial Times suggesting that he was discussing a comeback to the firm he co-founded.

The BBC understands that the likelihood of him being reinstated is very low while the company's internal investigations are ongoing.

The move would also face scrutiny from Washington, with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent calling for Nvidia to hire its next chief executive from outside the company.

For the moment, Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress is leading the firm and will present the company's results on Tuesday, 17 November.

On Friday, Bessent blamed the US Commerce Department, led by fellow cabinet colleague Howard Lutnick, for failing to properly oversee the export of high-end chips.

Bessent told CNBC that several agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months... but Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process".

The so-called entity list restricts US trade with overseas firms and individuals.

Lutnick has been called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over his department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

Other congressional committees have asked both Lutnick and his department to retain all records relating to Nvidia and Jensen Huang.

Megaspeed is suspected of giving backdoor access to Nvidia chips to Chinese companies via its data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Nvidia has been banned from selling its advanced semiconductors to China.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Singapore has said that while it does not enforce "unilateral export controls imposed by other countries" it expects all companies in its jurisdiction to respect such regulations.

Megaspeed said it "designs and maintains its global operational and supply-chain control frameworks to comply with all applicable laws and regulatory regimes". It said this includes "US export control requirements and local jurisdiction rules in every market where we operate".

China has denied that Alice Huang is a spy and warned the US not to use the scandal as a "pretext for imposing a technological blockade" on AI technology.

#150. Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh stresses importance of AI to economy, but says it could ‘disappoint’

[The stream is slated to start at noon ET. Please refresh the page if you do not see a player above.]

Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh on Monday highlighted the potential economic benefits of artificial intelligence while warning that the emerging technology also could lead to widespread job losses or fail to deliver on its lofty expectations.

Speaking in San Francisco before a blue-ribbon task force he assembled to examine productivity and employment, the central bank leader stressed that AI "will have significant impact on the economy, with important implications for monetary policy down the road."

"I stress that while I'm not here to offer predictions, I'm not exactly impartial about American leadership in a time so filled with both challenge and possibility," he said in prepared remarks before his productivity and jobs task force.

In his remarks, Warsh used the term "super intelligence" instead of artificial intelligence and described it as "an entirely new force in economic life and society" that has already begun to transform economic activity. President Donald Trump recently ordered that the U.S. government now use the term "super intelligence" almost exclusively instead of AI.

Since taking the Fed chair position, Warsh has emphasized the importance of AI and its potential to improve productivity and ultimately to be a disinflationary force on the economy.

In his remarks Monday, he noted that AI could lead to productivity gains that could accelerate real economic growth beyond its current range while keeping employment near full capacity. On the downside, he said "less positive" outcomes include job displacement through automation that could raise unemployment.

At the same time, he outlined a third scenario where AI could simply "disappoint."

"After significant capital investment and stock market gains, expected returns could turn down and equity valuations could fall, leading to an economic slowdown and a more notable rise in unemployment," he said. "In time, the economy would recover, but without the productivity gains."

The potential for AI advancement has driven stock market valuations higher and led to companies raising capital to continue competing.

Monday's conference features discussion panels with business leaders, economists and academics. The final session will be a fireside chat that will include Warsh and task force leader and AI advocate Marc Andreessen, co-founder of Andreessen Horowitz.

Warsh created four other task forces addressing topics such as Fed communication, data reliance, balance sheet policy and inflation frameworks.

The chairman noted that this was the first visit he has made to a regional bank.

#149. Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant here, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12 to 18 months "would be great" and is something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said plant construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an internal combustion engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said extended-range and SUV models are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla.

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start at under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exteriors that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

#148. Beauty advent calendars can cost hundreds but are they value for money?

It's easy to see the appeal of a beauty advent calendar. Like many women at this time of year, Julia says she often finds herself tempted to splurge on one - especially a perfume calendar. But she takes quite an unsentimental approach.

"I always look at what's in them. If I like enough of the fragrances, I'll buy it," says the 39-year old, who works as a senior commercial manager in engineering.

Luxury advent calendars are supposed to offer the thrill of a surprise but, for Julia, it is more important she actually wants the items inside - a thought that is also striking more and more customers in the wake of the scandal over the Skims calendar.

Customers likened the contents of Kim Kardashian's Skims calendar to something you would find down the back of a sofa

This week, Kim Kardashian's beauty brand offered all of its customers refunds for its $148 (Â£110) calendar after being widely criticised on social media over the quality of its contents, with some customers likening the hair clips, scrunchies and keychains inside to things you might find down the back of a sofa.

Julia says she earns a good salary but is keen to get value for money and doesn't want to waste it on items she won't use.

"I spent Â£160 on a Space NK fragrance calendar last year," she says. "I got some fragrances I'd been wanting for a while for a lot less than recommended retail price, and those I didn't like I managed to sell on for a slight mark-up on what I paid."

Julia, 39, says she thinks fragrance calendars are usually good value for money

Beauty calendars, which often cost upwards of Â£100 and sometimes much more, have been around for more than a decade and the marketing has grown ever more sophisticated.

High-profile brands will warn that their calendars risk running out, which may boost early demand, while unboxing videos posted by influencers can encourage spending by creating an infectious sense of surprise and delight.

And it appears to be working. Ipsos data shows, external around 15% of all advent calendars sold are beauty ones, with people aged 18-34 particularly willing to splash out.

But in the face of that tsunami of marketing, customers seem to be getting savvier.

"I love beauty, but I rarely buy beauty advent calendars. They are pricey and often only have a few things that I actually want to try or are trending products," says Priya Raj, 30, a fashion journalist.

She thinks some are good value but, for her, the advantage is being able to try on a small scale without forking out for a full-size product.

"For me, an advent calendar - particularly a beauty one - serves the purpose of allowing me to try out different products, particularly in the lead-up to Christmas, and then I can hopefully snag a bargain in the Boxing Day sales."

Priya Raj, 30, a fashion journalist, says the calendars "serve the purpose of allowing me to try out different products"

Podcaster Rhea Somaiya, 28, also likes the general idea, but has a healthy scepticism around a lot of them in practice.

"I do think some brands take the consumer for granted in that they provide each day with something so menial that people are bombarded with irrelevant and cluttering items.

"And when that's the case, they certainly put me off as the excitement to open each window dwindles into irritation rather than joy."

Podcaster Rhea Somaiya, 28, is less keen on the calendars and says they leave consumers "bombarded with irrelevant and cluttering items"

Jessica Jasper, a marketing consultant, is more positive - she thinks it's worth it. But she also thinks some companies are just jumping on a trend and that "some brands are getting a little carried away with the pricing".

So can beauty calendars offer value for both the brands and the customer - an example of a win-win? Or are they mainly designed to make us spend more at Christmas than we need to?

"Some brands are getting a little carried away with the pricing," says marketing consultant Jessica Jasper

Heather Widdows, a beauty expert and philosophy professor at Warwick University, has pointed out that while you might buy some of the products in an advent calendar individually, it's highly unlikely you'd buy all of them.

"Beauty calendars may offer you lots of products for a really good price. They can also be a bit of fun and feel sensuous.

"But it is exceptionally unlikely that you would use all the products inside them, so they're probably not worth it."

This year, Julia has spent Â£99 on a John Lewis fragrance calendar which she thinks is good value for money. But it doesn't mean she'll keep everything in it.

In fact she's joined a Facebook group where people trade the ones they don't want for ones they do.

"The ones I keep are already a lot cheaper per mililitre than if you bought them at retail," she says.

With additional reporting from Kris Bramwell

#146. Avoid this Black Friday scam - how to check it's a real deal

This month's Black Friday and the tax return deadline at the end of January are both opportunities for fraudsters to scam you.

That's because people are more likely to expect emails, texts and other correspondence at this time of year.

Fraudsters will use the dates to impersonate genuine organisations and retailers by pushing victims towards fake websites to steal personal information.

Here's how to spot a scam and how you can avoid being caught out.

If you are self-employed or have more than one source of income you will need to file a tax return by the end of January.

Fraudsters seize on this deadline to blitz people with messages that look like they are official and from the UK's tax authority, HMRC.

Any such calls or messages should be ignored, but you can also check whether an email, external, text message, external or phone call, external claiming to be from HMRC is genuine.

"Reporting scams helps us shut them down and protect others," adds Lucy Pike, HMRC's chief security officer.

New figures from HMRC, shared with the BBC, show that it received 95,340 reports of suspected scams in the year to the end of October.

There were 900 reports of one specific scam which used emails containing a malicious attachment which directed customers to a fake gov.uk webpage designed to steal personal and financial information.

it never contacts people by email, text or phone to tell them they are due a refund or ask them to claim one

it never asks for personal or financial information by email or text message

it never leaves threatening voicemails about legal action or arrest

In the run-up to Black Friday you may be looking around for discounts, but fraudsters will impersonate genuine retailers to steal personal details or take payment for a product that will never be delivered.

Kirsty Adams, a fraud and scams expert at High Street bank Barclays, says make sure you pause before you buy.

"When you're scrolling on the sofa and a countdown timer starts ticking, or a deal pops up in your feed from an account that looks just like a brand you trust, it's easy to get swept up and pay before you've had a chance to check.

"No deal is worth rushing for. So take a moment before you buy."

The bank also advises doing these four things:

Pause and check, rather than being blinded by a countdown timer

Pay safely using a credit or debit card via a trusted payment service, not a bank transfer to a stranger

Open an official website or app yourself rather than clicking on links in emails, ads or social posts

Talk to a friend, family or your bank if you think something does not feel right, and calling your bank straight away if you think you've been scammed

Doing these things now will help you save money at Christmas

Don't fall for this - how to protect yourself from AI scams
