#143. Intel sued by investors after PC sales slump hits share price

Intel, the US chip giant, is being sued by its shareholders who accuse the firm of hiding the severity of a slump in its personal computer (PC) sales that caused its share price to plunge almost 29% in one day.

Billions of dollars was knocked off the value of the company after it forecast disappointing revenues ahead of its latest earnings report.

Shareholders allege that Intel made "materially false and misleading" statements that failed to disclose the slowdown in PC sales and the impact of its data centre projects, according to a proposed class action lawsuit filed in San Jose, California.

In October, Intel firm announced its computer hardware sales had dropped sharply in the three months that ended in September.

That report undermined earlier suggestions from the chipmaker that demand had been improving, sending Intel shares plunging, according to the lawsuit.

Shareholders claimed the "depressed demand for PC sales" caused "a dramatic slowdown" in quarterly earnings â a drop of 9%, or about $800m - to $8.1bn.

The lawsuit, filed by legal firm Robbins Geller Rudman & Dowd on behalf of owners of Intel common shares between 23 July and 29 October this year, was filed against the chipmaker, its chief executive Tan Lip-Bu and other company leaders.

Intel has poured huge investments into manufacturing to meet the enormous demand for AI computing power.

As more semiconductors are channelled towards data centres, chips for PCs and other electronics have become more expensive.

Intel boss Tan had told analysts that the industry was facing an "unprecedented memory crunch", resulting in a shortage of chip supplies for PCs.

"We need to address these challenges with urgency," Tan said in October.

Intel shares have fallen sharply in the past month by 37.5%, though its share price is still up by 51% overall this year.

#141. Gas starts flowing from Jackdaw after government green light

The Jackdaw platform is located 150 miles east of Aberdeen

Gas has begun flowing from the Jackdaw gas field, with the UK energy secretary saying it will help to heat millions of homes and businesses this winter.

Ministers last month announced the Jackdaw field, which lies 150 miles east of Aberdeen, had been approved after years of uncertainty and a court case.

It has now been announced that it has begun producing gas which will start flowing into Britain, according to its operator Adura.

The UK government approved production from the gas field in the North Sea last month, amid intense debate about the project's environmental and economic impact.

Miliband says he won't resign if government approves Rosebank oil field

Burnham defends Jackdaw decision as Badenoch urges PM to overturn ban on new oil and gas licences

Jackdaw is operated by Adura under a joint venture between Shell and Norwegian state energy firm Equinor.

A decision is yet to be taken on whether production can begin at Rosebank, the UK's largest known untapped oil field, which is located off the coast of Shetland in the North Atlantic.

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh said: "Last month, I gave Jackdaw the green light and today its gas will start flowing into Britain, thanks to the hard work of the skilled North Sea workers.

"It means homegrown gas from this project will help to heat millions of British homes and businesses this winter and beyond, in a boost for our energy security.

"It also will help minimise the UK's use of imported higher emission gas as we continue our transition to renewables and nuclear."

Claire Mueller, Jackdaw project director at Adura, said the gas field was "now helping to power Britain".

She added: "At a time of considerable uncertainty around the world, Jackdaw will provide secure energy from our own waters to heat British homes and power our economy, while reducing our reliance on imports with higher emissions.

"Jackdaw also demonstrates what can be achieved when Britain makes pragmatic, evidence-led decisions about the energy it needs, while properly accounting for our climate responsibilities."

Mueller said the decision was a "positive step towards restoring confidence in the North Sea".

She added that Adura was "proud to be producing the oil and gas our country will continue to need for decades".

Why is the Jackdaw gas field approval so controversial?

Adura has said Jackdaw will provide about 6% of UK gas production, enough to power 1.4 million homes. After imports are included, that amounts to about 2% of total UK demand.

The gas will be pumped to the existing Shearwater platform for processing â known as a tie-back in industry terms â before being piped to the St Fergus gas terminal in Aberdeenshire.

The UK government said that over its expected 11-year lifespan, Jackdaw would support the UK's gas supply, protect hundreds of jobs and add Â£2.9bn to the economy.

The decision to green-light Jackdaw drew criticism from some Labour backbenchers and environmentalists, while Conservatives called on the government to also approve the Rosebank oil field.

Both projects were approved for development by the previous Tory government in 2022 and 2023 respectively but these decisions were struck down by the Scottish courts after a challenge from environmental campaigners.

This followed a Supreme Court ruling that developers had to account for the climate impact caused by burning fossil fuels in their planning applications.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has pledged to stick to the UK's net zero target despite giving the green light to Jackdaw.

He previously said the decision would "significantly strengthen our energy security by increasing domestic gas production by about 6% and support the UK's energy supply for more than a decade".

A decision on the larger Rosebank oil project is yet to be made.

Protesters gathered outside the Department for Energy Security and Net Zero in Westminster on Wednesday morning

The co-leader of the Scottish Greens, Ross Greer, described the Jackdaw approval as "climate vandalism".

He said: "It is a devastating betrayal of our children's future and it won't even lower energy bills in the here and now."

Environmental campaigners argue that this summer's heatwaves demonstrate the need to tackle climate change by rejecting both Jackdaw and Rosebank.

Areeba Hamid, the co-executive director of Greenpeace, said approving Jackdaw was "the wrong decision" in terms of lowering energy bills, providing energy security, and addressing climate change.

Oxfam said the government had "drilled straight through the UK's climate commitments" and accused the prime minister of sending "the wrong signal as the impacts of climate change intensify".

The charity says the government must commit to not approving any new oil and gas licences, including the proposals for Rosebank.

Jackdaw's operators said its approval was vital to avoid domestic supply shortages

Jackdaw sits close to the boundary between the British and Norwegian sectors of the North Sea, in an area where Westminster originally granted licences to explore for oil and gas in 1970 and 1989.

The field was discovered in 2005 but it was expensive and technically difficult to exploit.

Shell did not commit to its development until 2022, shortly after the Conservative government had granted consent for production.

But in January 2025, after a challenge by environmentalists, the Court of Session in Edinburgh ruled that consent for Jackdaw and Rosebank had been granted unlawfully.

The judge, Lord Ericht, said the developers should have provided a more detailed account of the fields' impact on climate change as part of the permitting process.

The judge allowed the companies to continue with preparatory work short of producing oil and gas while they crafted new environmental impact assessments taking into account the eventual effect of actually burning the fossil fuels, not just the emissions which would be created in extracting them.

An inside look at Jackdaw, the UKâs newest and most contested gas platform.

When BBC News visited the platform this summer, Adura CEO Neil McCulloch said its approval was vital to avoid domestic supply shortages this winter.

However, the UK government has previously suggested that Jackdaw will have little impact on energy security and none on reducing domestic gas and electricity bills.

In August, in an interim response to a consultation on the future of the country's gas supplies, Energy Minister Michael Shanks said the UK would inevitably become more reliant on imports as domestic production declined, external.

"With diminishing output from the North Sea over the past decades, any gas produced from the UK Continental Shelf represents a tiny fraction of global demand and so will not lower prices nor materially boost supply," wrote Shanks.

Labour has previously said it is opposed to the issuing of new exploration licences but it will consider new production projects if they are tied back to existing facilities.

McCulloch described the Jackdaw decision as "an important and positive step forward" - but said attention now turned to Rosebank.

He has urged the UK government to approve production from the field, in which Adura owns an 80% stake. The other 20% is held by London-based Ithaca Energy.

Rosebank contains an estimated 300 million to 500 million barrels of oil.

But while most of Jackdaw's gas is likely to be consumed in the UK, Rosebank's oil will be taken off by tanker and sold on international markets.

UK government approves drilling in Jackdaw gas field with production to start this month

Chris Mason: Jackdaw approved but tricky energy trade-offs remain

What is happening with the Jackdaw and Rosebank gas and oil fields?

#139. Disney's 'Star Wars' is coming to Cosm domes ahead of 50th anniversary as part of multiyear film deal

On Thursday, the immersive entertainment technology company announced a new studio partnership with The Walt Disney Co. that will bring the original "Star Wars" to its domes early next year as part of a multiyear, multifilm collaboration.

The financial terms of the deal were not disclosed.

Cosm's massive concave screens will drop film fans into a galaxy far, far away starting in March, just ahead of the Academy Award-winning film's 50th anniversary in May.

"We are thrilled to add the Disney partnership," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "We do look for those opportunities of these like anniversary and commemorative moments. We look for those opportunities to celebrate with the fans, but also find this mutually beneficial partnership between both parties."

This will be Cosm's sixth theatrical collaboration with Hollywood, having already partnered with Warner Bros. to bring "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. The company is set to launch immersive screenings of Universal's "Shrek" in December.

Each Cosm dome has stadium-style seating, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width fills much of the viewers' periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the onscreen environment.

In this case, "Star Wars" will be displayed in a large rectangle in the center of the dome while the background is transformed into the iconic planets of Tatooine and Yavin IV that are featured in the film as well as the infamous Death Star. Audiences will also see elements that will make it appear like they're traveling at lightspeed and getting trapped in an intergalactic trash compactor.

Cosm is working directly with studios Lucasfilm and Industrial Light and Magic as well as creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and the sound mixing for the unique presentation of the film.

"We look for those moments where sound, music ... listening to Darth Vader's breath for the first time ... you feel those moments differently in an 87-foot diameter dome and 12k resolution with the sound system we have," Scalice said. "And it's one of the things that with experiential cinema and our technology that we were so excited and honestly privileged that we can take these defining moments for fans and work closely with our partners that doesn't really change the authenticity of it, it just enhances it in a different manner."

The premium experience will also be paired with a themed menu. Cosm has done this with other titles, such as "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," serving guests throughout the screening with house-made shepherd's pie, pretzel wands with cheese dip and a box of Bertie Bott's Every Flavour Beans.

Cosm is trying to establish itself as a destination for fandom — both for sports and for film. It has a wide variety of live event offerings, which it telecasts using proprietary cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, as well as its expanding library of movies.

"What I love is that we really are kind of building these Cosm communities built on fandom," Scalice said. "It's the No. 1 foundation for our business."

And Star Wars is a powerful fandom. The franchise remains one of the top-ranking pieces of intellectual property across film, television and retail annually, and Disney is gearing up for a massive celebration of its semicentennial including merchandise launches, theatrical re-releases, its biannual Star Wars Celebration convention and collaborations with other brands.

Correction: This story has been updated to correct the spelling of Alexis Scalice's name.

#136. Faisal Islam: What's in the Brexit reset food deal and are there trade-offs?

Some British sausages have faced barriers to being exported to the EU

In Boston, Lincolnshire, a supplier of ready meals to Marks & Spencer (M&S) manufactures thousands of portions of spaghetti bolognese to be sent to the retailer's shops in the Republic of Ireland.

Every plastic pallet is meticulously individually tagged with a green-grey label âEireâ.

Every truck full of food sent to European Union countries requires hundreds of pages of forms, checks by vets, expense and delays.

It has cost M&S millions of pounds and is part of that reason the British business shut down 11 French stores in 2021.

Many smaller exporters stopped sending their goods to the EU entirely. Some produce, including sausages and certain types of seafood, were entirely stopped.

All this was the predictable consequence of former Prime Minister Boris Johnsonâs 2020 deal prioritising regulatory freedom over freeflowing goods trade.

That will end with a Brexit "reset" that Prime Minister Andy Burnham is set to sign with the EU on Friday.

The government claims it could boost the economy to the tune of Â£5bn a year and cut the cost of food for households.

But will the agreement mean a return to frictionless trade for food with the EU or are there trade-offs and quid pro quos the UK will have to make?

EU affairs minister Hamish Falconer, who is a key negotiator with the bloc, acknowledged a âchoiceâ is being made.

In exchange for being able to export produce such as Lincolnshire sausages to the 27 countries, the flipside is the extent to which the UK will be able to influence EU food policies.

The UK will have a "voice in the room", said Falconer, but no vote. Critics have said a reset will make the UK "a rule taker", not a rule maker.

But Falconer said the UK will be involved in a process to shape the rules.

A Brexit reset would not involve any upfront payments, said Falconer, but made it clear there could well be an "exit clause" in case a future UK government reversed the closer alignment.

He said this was "reasonable" to consider what would happen if a future Conservative or Reform government wanted to undo the new arrangements. Both parties have said they would if they were in power.

"The EU is worried about that and you can see why," he said.

The BBC understands that the clause is a result of EU concerns about the possible cost of having to rebuild facilities for border food safety checks which were put in place following Brexit.

The prime minister has said he believes there is a "win-win" deal to be had with the EU.

Speaking at a Marks & Spencer food court in central London, Falconer said reducing the cost of importing food "will mean less cost for supermarkets, and I hope that less cost then turns into cheaper produce on the shelves".

He said companies have to currently deal with "pages and pages" of form-filling.

Some exports are rejected for having the wrong colour of ink on forms, or an inaccurate Latin name for cooked meats.

The reset could also mean "tearing up" checks that were introduced on goods flowing from Great Britain to Northern Ireland, following Brexit, he said, as well as separate "Not for EU" labelling.

The whole deal to be signed on Friday is calculated to add 0.4% to UK GDP.

Minister for EU relations Hamish Falconer

Tom Bradshaw, president of the National Farmers' Union, welcomed reduced border inspections and paperwork but said that for the NFU it was "a moment of opportunity and risk" with farmers waiting to see what carve-outs and transitional arrangements would be included.

Trade volumes in food exports to the EU had "fallen significantly - by over a third" since 2019, he said.

But the UK should take a "pragmatic" approach to implementing any new rules.

He raised pesticides and precision breeding - or gene edited crops - as areas where he hoped some compromise would be found.

UK firms are, for example, developing tomatoes engineered to contain extra vitamin D.

Historically the EU has had much stricter rules on gene-editing, but this year relaxed regulations.

Falconer said people would have to "wait and see the detail" in this area.

Another contentious area has been crisp flavourings, with concerns raised that stricter EU rules on certain ingredients might limit the sale of some popular snack flavours in the UK.

"I want to take this opportunity to absolutely stamp down on misinformation about smoky bacon crisps," Falconer said.

Post-Brexit the UK had stayed "pretty closely aligned" to European food safety standards, he said.

Two leading snack brands had already adapted their recipes to accommodate EU rules, an example of the kind of alignment that would happen whether or not there was a reset of UK-EU relations.

"The truth is, almost every business in this country that wants to export food or drink will be aligning with EU rules because it's our biggest market.

"Lots of people, like the good people at Walkers and Frazzles have made these changes already, so I would want to reassure people, particularly smoky bacon fans, it's okay," he said.

Could smoky bacon crisps really be banned?

Ten years on, Brexit's economic impact is becoming clearer

#135. California sues OpenAI over Hugging Face, other AI model loss of control

California Attorney General Rob Bonta on Monday sued artificial intelligence giant OpenAI for allegedly endangering the public by developing advanced AI models without adequate safeguards and monitoring.

"OpenAI disregarded public safety, knowingly tested potentially dangerous AI models without proper oversight and safety measures, and misled consumers and the public," Bonta said at a press conference.

Bonta cited the "infamous" incident in July, where two OpenAI models broke out of a testing environment, gained unauthorized access to the open internet, and breached the internal systems of open-source developer platform Hugging Face.

OpenAI said the models were trying to find information that they could use to cheat on an evaluation, and succeeded. The break-in at Hugging Face, which Nvidia acquired for roughly $13 billion in September. rattled industry researchers and prompted government officials to call for more stringent oversight of the AI industry.

On Monday, Bonta said that OpenAI later disclosed to his investigators that it was aware of 80 incidents "involving loss of control and access to third-party networks and websites."

"But the company failed to provide the detailed information we requested to evaluate these incidents; this is concerning, and it puts the public at risk," he said.

Bonta's suit, filed in state Superior Court in San Francisco, alleges OpenAI violated California's Unfair Competition Law and accuses the company of being a public nuisance as defined by state law.

The attorney general told reporters he believed in AI's technological power, and the potential of AI models to "achieve scientific breakthroughs, boost economic growth, guard against cyber attacks, and benefit all Californians."

"But AI systems must be developed and designed safely," he said. "The companies that develop these models have a moral and yes, a legal responsibility to keep public safety at the forefront."

Bonta's suit comes as Congress has been presented with, but not passed, a comprehensive AI safety bill. AI companies, including OpenAI and Anthropic, along with critics of AI, have called for such legislation on the heels of the Hugging Face break-in and other incidents that have raised concerns about the safety of AI models.

Other AI companies, including Anthropic, Meta and Google, have since disclosed incidents where their own models went rogue. But OpenAI is the first to be sued in connection with such incidents by California.

OpenAI spokesperson Drew Pusateri, in a statement on the lawsuit, said, "Since the Hugging Face incident, we've undertaken an extensive and transparent effort to understand and address misaligned model activity."

"We've published a detailed technical report, commissioned and published a separate independent report, notified affected organizations, strengthened safeguards across our research systems, and created a framework for additional identification and disclosure of misalignment," Pusateri said.

"This lawsuit appears to single out OpenAI over a challenge that's been identified by every major AI lab. We take misalignment seriously, and we have engaged — and will continue to engage — constructively with the Attorney General's office to share information and advance strong, practical safety standards for the entire industry," Pusateri said.

Bonta's lawsuit adds to OpenAI's growing list of legal woes.

In June, Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier sued OpenAI and CEO Sam Altman, alleging that the company knowingly released an unsafe product that could harm users.

OpenAI is also facing lawsuits alleging that the company's ChatGPT platform led users to experience harmful delusions and, in some cases, to commit suicide.

California's lawsuit comes almost three months after a suit by Bonta and a coalition of other state attorneys general ended with Meta agreeing to pay $16.7 billion after being accused of misrepresenting the extent of child mental health harms caused by its apps, including Facebook and Instagram. California could receive $1.5 billion to $2.1 billion from that agreement.

OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the Securities and Exchange Commission in June, setting the stage for a potentially massive initial public offering in 2027.

It is not clear how, if at all, Bonta's lawsuit will affect the IPO.

Lise Buyer, partner at IPO advisory firm Class V Group, said she had not closely followed news about the suit.

But Buyer, in an email, said, "Potential IPO buyers weigh a laundry list of risk factors, some more heavily than others."

"Investors generally do their best to assess timing and a continuum of potential outcomes," Buyer said. "Often, they will speak with uninvolved law firms in calculating the potential outcomes. Then, the risks, the timing and each investors' preferred holding period factor into the price they are willing to pay - assuming they have the option to buy or pass."

"Overall the aggregate belief about the risks — all risks — factor in to the IPO price," she said.

Earlier Monday, OpenAI announced agreements with four independent evaluators to help support its safety work.

The organizations, Apollo Research, METR, Transluce and Goodfire, will be granted "deep access" to OpenAI's internal systems and conduct distinct pilot assessments around model behavior and safeguards, OpenAI said. The company could decide to expand the agreements in the future.Altman pledged to grant independent evaluators "employee-like access" to the company in September, shortly after Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said his company would do so as part of his call to slow down the pace of advanced model development.Independent evaluators have taken on outsized importance following high-profile safety breaches, including the Hugging Face incident, this year.

President Donald Trump encouraged AI companies to work with an "independent external auditor" as part of a "morally binding" accord he unveiled in September.

#132. Customers report service outages at major US banks

Thousands of customers at major US banks, including JP Morgan Chase, Wells Fargo and Bank of America, have reported trouble accessing services.

Chase Bank - the largest banking institution in the US - said a "software bug" in its hardware systems may have been behind the glitch and noted the impacts may have affected other banks. It added the issue was "temporary" and not "systemic" or "cyber-related".

Problems appeared to be easing for many customers by early Monday evening.

The issue comes after a string of unrelated technical problems, including a massive Microsoft outage, a network failure that grounded all US flights, and United Airlines reporting a "widespread system outage".

Problems started on Monday afternoon. There were almost 5,000 reports of problems with Chase Bank's services, according to the Downdetector website.

Clients reported issues accessing their online accounts, viewing balances and performing transactions, like bank transfers and wire transfers, according to the BBC's US media partner CBS News.

A Chase Bank customer in Texas told the outlet that he could not deposit a check at a Dallas branch on Monday, reporting that he was told his transaction could not be completed. He said a bank teller told the customer in front of him that the "system was down" but did not provide additional details.

Microsoft says services fully restored after day-long global outage

Microsoft outage 'stark reminder' of how much we rely on tech

Chase Bank told its customers that "some transactions are taking longer than usual to complete" but they still had "access to their accounts and funds".

The bank, a subsidiary of JP Morgan Chase, serves more than 86m customers at 5,000 branches across the US, as well as 7.4m small businesses, according to the company's webpage. It is also the top credit card issuer in the US.

The exact nature of the bug was unclear. The BBC has contacted Chase Bank.

Bank of America customers had issues at ATMs and using mobile banking services online, while US Bank customers reported issues with their online accounts and the bank's mobile app, CBS reported.

Customers using another institution, US Bank, also reported issues transferring funds and Wells Fargo customers said they had problems signing into their accounts, getting account balances and making transactions.

US Bank said some its systems were not directly affected but clients using the Zelle, a digital payment platform, received error messages saying their payments could not be sent.

Zelle has said its service is "operating as it should" and issues at other banks could be affecting payments.

Customers also reported issues with Truist, PNC and Capital One but all these companies told the BBC they have not been impacted.

As of 18:00 EST (23:00 GMT), Chase, Bank of America and US Bank had seen a fall in the number of reported outages.

Just as the banking issue affected US customers on Monday, United Airlines confirmed a "widespread system outage", which it described as "a technology issue affecting departing flights".

The Chicago-based airline did not say what is believed to have caused the issue, but a spokesperson said it is not related to cybersecurity.

The issue did not appear to be affecting any other airlines.

The United issue is separate from the one that hit the US Federal Aviation Administration (FAA), but comes as other industries report different outages around the US.

On Friday, the FAA issued a rare nationwide a ground stop, halting all US air travel due to a telecommunication and network upgrade issues.

The halt lasted just over four hours, but the knock-on effect continues to snarl US air travel.

How telecoms upgrade work sparked US flight chaos

Copper wire upgrade behind US flight suspension, transport secretary says

#131. U.S. crude oil declines to $85 a barrel on recovering Middle East exports

Oil prices are falling Monday as shipments through the Strait of Hormuz recover and tensions between Iran and the U.S remain at a stalemate.

Brent crude fell 3.55% to $92.44 per barrel Monday while West Texas Intermediate crude futures settled down 3.58% to $85.39 a barrel.

The International Energy Agency said on Friday it expects global supply to exceed 1.7 million barrels a day in the fourth quarter of 2026, reversing its earlier forecast for a deficit as Middle East exports recover faster than global demand and even as shipping attacks continue.

Meanwhile, Russia's crude oil production rose to 8.73 million barrels a day in October, a 210,000 barrel-a-day surge, according to the IEA.

Goldman Sachs said in a Sunday note it expects Brent to average $88 a barrel in 2027 and fall to $78 by December 2027 as the market returns to a surplus.

The bank believes Middle East supply to rise above its 2025 average as producers use ship-to-ship transfers and add pipeline capacity.

#129. Create more jobs for under-25s by cutting National Insurance, key report urges

Salon owner: 'We have parents offering to pay their kids' wages'

National Insurance (NI) paid by firms on under-25s should be gradually lowered to help create more jobs, a major report will urge.

There are nearly one million 16-24 year-olds not in employment, education or training (Neets) in the UK, official figures show.

Reducing NI costs for firms starting with retail and hospitality firms - which provide almost 40% of jobs for these people - will help improve their opportunities, the report will suggest.

Collette Osborne, a hairdresser who owns two salons in Nottingham, said she feels "suffocated by employment costs" including NI and while she used to employ eight apprentices a year, now she cannot afford to take on any.

Alan Milburn's report will also make recommendations on cutting the benefits bill

Her workforce has more than halved to 21 staff. Osborne told the BBC it costs "around Â£60,000 to train an apprentice before we can actually start allowing them onto customers' heads".

She said she has had parents so desperate for their children to find employment at her salons that some have even offered to pay their wages.

"I'm inundated with applications - and I mean inundated - which is heartbreaking because I can't take them on," she said.

Osborne does not feel she is getting help to offer jobs to youngsters.

"I feel like the government's foot is firmly on my neck," she said. "They promise breathing space. I feel suffocated, suffocated by employment costs."

The independent report by former Labour minister Alan Milburn has been in the works for a year and will be published on Tuesday in full.

He has already unveiled some of his proposals including:

Reducing benefits - under-25s should no longer be able to claim the health element of UK-wide Universal Credit or personal independence payment (Pip), which is the main disability benefit in England and Wales

A legal guarantee of a job, training or employment support with those who refuse to take up potentially losing their benefits.

Doubling the threshold at which businesses start paying NI for 21 to 24-year-olds from Â£5,000 to Â£10,000.

At the moment, businesses pay no employer NI contributions for workers younger than 21 or for apprentices under 25 â unless they earn above a threshold of Â£50,270.

However, for non-apprentices aged 21-24, employers pay 15% on annual earnings above Â£5,000.

This has been the situation since April 2025, after the then Chancellor Rachel Reeves increased the rate from 13.8% and lowered the threshold from Â£9,100.

Under Sir Keir Starmer, the government raised NI for firms but Milburn will say the Neets issue was not started by the increase.

Immediately axing NI for 21 to 24-year-olds would cost Â£5.1bn, according to research by the Resolution Foundation, a left-leaning think tank.

Milburn is set to say that an immediate across-the-board cut in employer NI is not the best use of several billions of pounds of public money.

But shadow work and pensions secretary, Helen Whately, said the government should "just stop doing the taxing".

Milburn told the BBC's Laura Kuenssberg programme he supported the government subsidising wages to encourage businesses to take on young workers.

"Because young people today are walking into the workplace often with a mental health problem, ADHD or anxiety, if you're a small business that's a cost that you've got to absorb," he said.

"The state has a responsibility to help them with that."

On Sunday, Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden said the government would extend its subsidised jobs guarantee scheme for 18 to 24-year-olds who are claiming Universal Credit.

People who have been on benefits for 12 months will be eligible, down from 18 months and the scheme would be available for people in England, Scotland and Wales.

McFadden said the scheme, which guarantees workers a six-month job, "will help more than 90,000 young people into work by March 2029".

Additional reporting by Ollie Smith and Anuk Weerawardana

#127. Jim Cramer says Nvidia’s leadership shake-up won’t derail the AI trade

CNBC's Jim Cramer said Monday that Nvidia's leadership shake-up won't derail the artificial intelligence boom.

Cramer's comments came after another rough day for AI-related stocks in the wake of Jensen Huang's resignation from Nvidia last week. Investors have been worried that Huang's departure as CEO could jeopardize the chipmaker's financial support for the broader AI ecosystem, especially computing providers known as neoclouds. Nvidia is set to report earnings Tuesday night.

Cramer argued that demand for computing power extends far beyond Nvidia and its former CEO.

"The most important thing you need to know is that Nvidia is not going to bring down the western world," the "Mad Money" host said. "It is not going to bring down the data center world."

Under Huang, Nvidia built an extensive investment portfolio, including a $5 billion stake in Intel last year and an additional investment in AI cloud provider CoreWeave earlier this year. Nvidia had helped anchor CoreWeave's IPO in 2025 and participated in a 2023 Series B round for the company. In addition to taking equity stakes in other companies, Nvidia has provided other financial guarantees to customers, such as a $105 billion backstop for a sprawling data center project intended for OpenAI.

JPMorgan strategist Michael Cembalest, who publishes the closely followed Eye on the Market commentary, estimated that Nvidia's broader financial commitments may have reached $530 billion, based on the company's latest quarterly securities filing. Still, Cramer rejected the notion that the AI industry depends entirely on the chipmaker's backing.

"You can't overstate the importance of Jensen Huang to all of the AI investment that we've seen and expect to see," Cramer said. "But the idea that he was somehow all of the AI world is ludicrous."

Cramer expects interim CEO Colette Kress to take a more conservative approach, potentially scaling back some of Nvidia's financial commitments. While that could pressure heavily indebted companies, he said strong demand for computing power will sustain the broader AI build-out.

"Will there be a rein in of guarantees?" Cramer asked. "I hope so. Will Nvidia be more conservative? I am sure of it. Will artificial intelligence be stopped in its tracks? Are you kidding me?"

More generally, Cramer expressed confidence in Kress, praising her financial discipline and expecting a measured outlook when Nvidia reports earnings Tuesday. "I think interim CEO Kress will present a balanced non-hyperbolic view of the situations at Nvidia tomorrow night," he said. "I do not think it will cause a tsunami of selling."

A successful Anthropic IPO later this week could further restore confidence in the AI trade, Cramer argued.

"If the Anthropic deal works later this week, look out – we might see investors scrambling to get back into the same stocks that were sold today," Cramer said.

#125. Nvidia dismisses 'Bring Jensen Back' campaign as Trump backs effort to reinstate ex CEO

Donald Trump greeting Jensen Huang in September this year

Nvidia has dismissed a campaign calling for Jensen Huang to be handed back his job as allies, including US President Donald Trump, backed the effort.

Trump said on Monday that he "would certainly not mind" if Huang was reinstated, adding to a list of supporters who are backing a "Bring Jensen Back" campaign.

Fellow tech bosses, including SpaceX's Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, chief executive of Fortnite-maker Epic Games, have also supported Huang's return.

But the BBC has been told the board of the AI chip giant is not interested in bringing him back after he resigned last week following revelations he had two affairs, including one that involved a suspected Chinese spy.

Meanwhile on Monday, the Trump administration said it will further restrict China's âaccess to AI chips from companies like Nvidia.

The reported affairs created a crisis for one of the world's most valuable firms - a central company in AI and the US battle against China for dominance. Since his departure, supporters have launched an online petition demanding his return.

Musk and Sweeny are among the more than 175,000 who have signed the open letter calling for the board to reinstate Huang while an ongoing internal probe is conducted.

The "Stand with Jensen" campaign claims to have been launched by a make up of current and former Nvidia employees, partners, shareholders and supporters, all demanding the return of the former chief executive.

They expressed concerns the ex-Nvidia boss "was removed without meaningful due process".

President Trump gave his views on Huang's future on Monday, saying he "would certainly not mind" seeing the former boss given his job back.

"I guess maybe one lesson is more important than another, but I would certainly not mind seeing that," he told reporters as he left the White House.

The Trump administration on Monday also issued new restrictions on chip exports to overseas data centres, which allow Chinese companies to access advanced AI chips to train AI models.

The move came ahead of Trump's meeting with Chinese President Xi Jingping this week.

The new rules will require companies in Singapore, Thailand, Malaysia, Indonesia and Brazil to apply for US export licences for the most advanced chips in an effort to cut down on them being illegally diverted to China.

Huang had been expected to weather the storm following the revelation of an affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy. However, he resigned last Thursday after a second extra-marital relationship emerged, allegedly with an executive at Nvidia who reported to him.

The scandal-hit tech boss was discussing a possible return to Nvidia with confidants, according to the Financial Times, external, though Nvidia's board is not considering reappointing him.

A representative for Huang declined to comment on the report.

The so-called war room also wants to get support for Huang from the White House.

President Trump has spoken in glowing terms about Huang since the scandal erupted, and also said he believed Huang when the former Nvidia boss told him he had "no idea she was a spy".

Trump's view is at odds with his Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who has called for Nvidia to hire its next chief executive from outside the company.

The scandal has created tensions between the US Treasury and Commerce departments over shipments of high-end Nvidia chips to Alice Huang's former firm, Megaspeed. China has denied that Alice Huang is a spy.

It has raised questions about the possibility that the chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

On Monday, Trump said he did not know whether the women Huang had an affair with was a spy for China.

"They say she's a spy. I don't know that she was a spy at all. I mean, I really don't, I don't know that," he said.

Huang has agreed to testify in Congress, including before the House Select Committee on China to discuss whether US national security was compromised by his affair.

Trump said he did not think Huang had put national security at risk. "You know, China plays games with us. So we play games with China," he said.

The FBI is also investigating the affair between the former Nvidia boss and Alice Huang, according to Trump.

Musk claimed that "critical deals" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation.

"Jensen drove supply chain brilliantly at Nvidia but many of those deals were a handshake with Jensen, not a written contract," he posted on X., external

"Without Jensen, at least some of those critical deals will be lost."

Fellow tech boss Sam Altman, the chief executive of OpenAI, sidestepped the question of Huang returning to Nvidia at a conference, saying: "I don't think I know enough to answer that question."

But Altman, who himself was reinstated to his job after being fired back in 2023, said Huang was a "hero of mine".

Shareholders in Nvidia also want Huang back at the company's helm.

Hundreds of billions of pounds have been wiped off the firm's stock market value in recent days, but it is still worth some $3.87 trillion. Its share price was down in early Monday trading.

In an open letter to the board, Brad Gerstner, founder of Altimeter Capital and Atreides Management boss Gavin Baker called for management to leave "all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang".

Lord Sarfraz, a member of the UK's House of Lords, has also written to Nvidia's new chair, Harvey Jones, urging the firm's directors to give "serious consideration" to Huang's return following the investigation.

For the moment, Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress is leading the firm and will present the company's results on Tuesday.

#124. Retailers are turning to entertainment to win shoppers’ attention

Legacy retailer Gap Inc. made an unconventional hire earlier this year: a chief entertainment officer.

It was a newly created role as the company tapped former Paramount executive Pam Kaufman to helm its entertainment, content and licensing platform. Gap has since rolled out major campaigns, partnering with bands like Just Your Type and building on the success of last year's ads with girl group Katseye.

"Beloved brands have value beyond the products they sell. Consumers identify with them, trust them and want to be part of their world," Kaufman told CNBC in a statement. "The key is authenticity: Every extension has to reflect what the brand stands for and give its fans a meaningful new way to experience it."

She said it's part of a broader goal to emphasize what Gap calls "fashiontainment," a strategy to blend retail and entertainment to better align with consumers. Gap is part of a growing cohort of retailers investing in entertainment and experiences both in-store and through exclusive online content to drive engagement.

"Brands are stories, and we are the storytellers," CEO Richard Dickson said at the Goldman Sachs Global Consumer and Retail conference in September. "I think you could see that come to life when you look at some of the work that we've done, putting our brands in the center of culture and cultural conversation."

In another blending of retail and media, Mattel made waves a few years ago for its "Barbie" movie, building on the intellectual property of a decades-old fan favorite and drawing in massive box office success. At the time, Dickson was president and chief operating officer of the company, witnessing firsthand the sectors' intersection before departing for Gap.

"I think the post-Covid consumer attitude around shopping certainly evolved a lot," said Bryan Eshelman, who leads the retail practice at consulting firm AlixPartners. "It has gone from shopping as a fulfillment task to shopping, in many instances, more as an entertainment form."

Between experiences in physical stores and media campaigns featuring exclusive content, Eshelman said, retailers are getting more creative about the ways they're driving engagement and marketing.

"Community, at the end of the day, is one of the key ways to drive loyalty, so I think retailers of all types are trying to tap into some sense of community, some sense of lifestyle, some sense of belonging that's beyond the product that they offer," he said.

Branded entertainment has been around for a long time. Procter & Gamble made headlines in the 1930s for helping to coin the term "soap opera," and companies have for decades created media campaigns to draw in customers.

But with the rise of social media, content creation and short-form offerings, experts said audiences are becoming hungrier than ever for something new and original.

The retail and entertainment intersection is happening at a time when intellectual property is becoming more valuable than ever, according to experts.

As consumers encounter more marketing than ever, the companies that will win the battle for attention are the ones that can "cut through all of the noise," said Alex Michael, senior managing director at investment firm LionTree.

"The hardest thing to find and to get is audience these days," Michael told CNBC. "IP, in that sense, is the most valuable thing out there, and only becoming more valuable because it's attached to audience."

At the same time, he said, the way IP is distributed is broadening to include video, audio, short-form, long-form and live event content. Consumers are also redefining their loyalty to brands, which means companies have to "play much earlier in the consumer journey" than they have in years past to secure their trust, he said.

The trend is also likely helped by executives who have worked in both retail and entertainment: Gap's Kaufman came from Paramount, for example, and Dickson and Skydance co-CEO Ynon Kreiz held top roles at Mattel.

"The gray areas have never been more pronounced as to what is classically defined as retail or media or entertainment or live events," Michael said. "People who have cross-sector expertise are increasingly more valuable because you can't be defined by a single vertical, especially as things change so quickly."

Still, there's room for newer players, especially with the studio landscape rapidly changing, said Sean Akaks, the CEO of SonderCo, an agency that partners brands with celebrities and entertainment properties.

"I think it's a really interesting time for nontraditional players to be making content because you're seeing this massive consolidation of these legacy entertainment companies," he said. "I think there are just more open lanes for independent creators and also brands and anyone outside the traditional studio system to create content."

That's where retailers might tap creators, Akaks said, because they can use their platforms to reach consumers directly.

"I do think there's a world where we're going to see creators and brands sort of co-creating IP that maybe they're owning together," he said.

Once those companies get consumers into the store, they're also leaning into experiential retail, making shopping a more immersive experience than it otherwise would be, like Dick's Sporting Goods offering interactive activities and simulators in its stores.

According to data from AlixPartners, companies investing in experiential retail such as Coach and Ralph Lauren saw close to 10% year-over-year increases in consumers' average basket size.

"The idea that retailers would potentially invest in some distributed electronic content to help drive traffic, and then when you get there, it's experiential — it really goes into the kinds of flywheels that entertainment companies are also trying to build and grow," said Jeff Goldstein, co-lead of the media and entertainment practice at AlixPartners.

Yet, the entertainment that retail brands are developing is only one piece in a larger puzzle to retain customers and attract new ones. Translating that into tangible sales growth isn't necessarily a given.

"The basics of having great product in stock at a transparent price that feels like value is not swept away by animatronics," AlixPartners' Eshelman said. "The fundamentals are still there, and if you're going to have a good retail proposition, you need to have that balance. You can't mask bad product at a bad price with no inventory with glitz and glamour."

#122. We couldn't afford a house alone, so we bought one with strangers

The Pullman family, on the left, and the Wilcox family, bought a home together soon after meeting

When Elizabeth and Tom Wilcox realised they could never afford to buy the home they wanted for themselves and their two children, they decided to take a leap of faith.

They'd find another couple with young kids, complete strangers to them, and propose that they go halves on a property and all move in together.

Based in Vancouver, which has long had some of the highest house prices in Canada, the Wilcoxes were desperate to buy a home that had a back garden for their children to play in.

"We wanted a house with a yard that could fit a trampoline, in a walkable neighbourhood, but we couldn't afford it," says Elizabeth, a project manager in the healthcare sector.

With a budget of C$1m ($700,000; Â£500,000) all they could hope for was a small apartment with a balcony at best.

That was when Elizabeth came up with the idea of buying with another family.

They placed an advert in a local estate agent's e-newsletter, but it took two years before they finally met a family whom they clicked with â Ashley Pullman and her husband and two children.

In 2023, the Wilcoxes and the Pullmans co-bought a six-bedroom, detached house, complete with a back garden, for C$1.85m.

The two families split the deposit of C$250,000, and continue to go halves on the monthly mortgage payments of C$3,900. They also divide bills and other expenses equally, such as any necessary home repairs.

Elizabeth says the arrangement has saved both families "tens of thousands of dollars".

Mums Ashley Pullman, left, and Elizabeth Wilcox say the two families get along

While there is no official data available for the number of people buying homes with people they previously didn't know, Parimal Gosal says he has seen a big increase in interest in Toronto as a response to high property prices.

Gosal is the founder and CEO of Husmates, an app and website that connects people in Canada's largest city who are looking to potentially buy a home with a stranger.

It can be seen as "a financial relationship, and that takes emotions out of it", he says.

It's unclear how common this phenomenon is elsewhere in the world. David Hollingworth, from British mortgage broker L&C, says while he hasn't come across it himself, he thinks it also happens in the UK, though is "very niche".

"I remember that before Covid-19 there was a matchmaking event in a London pub, like speed dating, for people looking to buy a house with a stranger," he says. "How successful this was I don' t know."

The Wilcoxes and the Pullmans pooled their resources to buy a detached Vancouver home

Three years on, the Wilcoxes and the Pullmans say their living arrangement is still working out.

While each family has some private living spaces, such as their own kitchens, they share the big basement-level living room, which includes a home gym in one corner, and a shared freezer.

"If someone says they're in the basement to work out, we know not to go there for a bit, and it's never been an issue," says Elizabeth.

Ashley agrees that there are no problems. "Maybe it's arrogance but my husband and I felt like we were the type of people who could live with strangers," she says.

Sabrina Bowman, a freelance business consultant in Toronto, has co-owned a home with two other housemates since 2018. When she signed on the dotted line and moved in she didn't know one of the other two people.

While Sabrina says she isn't comfortable sharing the exact financial details, she says they decided to divide up the deposit and mortgage payments based on each of their levels of income.

She says any household disputes can be addressed early with open discussions. "What is important to us is having structures to communicate about everything that happens in the home."

They have regular house meetings where they discuss chores, financial issues, birthday celebrations, and any disagreements.

"It's great to see people be honest about something they may not be comfortable about and for us to find a solution to that problem," Sabrina says.

Sabrina Bowman says it is important to have open communication

In the case of both Sabrina's house in Toronto and the Wilcox and Pullman property in Vancouver, they have legal agreements in place in case someone wants to sell up and move out.

For both homes, reasonable notice periods are specified, and for Sabrina's, the departing housemate is required to give those remaining the first opportunity to buy their share.

Toronto real estate lawyer Tanya Walker says such legal assurances are vital. "You need something legally binding instead of a bunch of emails and Facebook messages that can be too messy to collect if a dispute comes up," Walker says.

Back in Vancouver, Elizabeth says that neither family has a timeframe for how long they will live in the house. "All of us are really happy with the set-up and location so it would be hard to move.

"I can't put a price on how it felt to watch the kids jump on the trampoline for the first time, or run in circles on our patch of green grass."

#121. Eric Trump-backed robot soldier startup can't pay staff after founder borrowed $4.6 million from company to buy home

Foundation Future Industries, a maker of military humanoid robots advised and backed by Eric Trump, failed to pay employees last month as its cash reserves dwindled, according to people with direct knowledge of the matter.

Workers have yet to receive paychecks after San Francisco-based Foundation missed payroll on Oct. 30, said the people, who asked for anonymity to speak candidly about a private company.

The situation has particularly angered some inside the startup because its co-founder and CEO, Sankaet Pathak, borrowed $4.6 million from the company last year to buy a luxury home, money that could have been used to pay workers, the people said.

Public records show that an LLC that names Pathak as sole principal purchased a $4.6 million Sausalito, California, property with "sweeping, cinematic views" of the San Francisco Bay in October 2025.

A spokesman for Pathak acknowledged receiving a detailed list of the major points from this story on Thursday, but didn't respond to requests for comment. Pathak's previous startup, a fintech firm named Synapse, collapsed in 2024, leaving thousands of Americans locked out of their savings accounts.

Foundation's cash crunch comes at a precarious moment for the company, which Pathak has said is close to unveiling a next-generation, weapons-ready robot that will help the U.S. compete against Chinese rivals. At the same time, Pathak is seeking to raise hundreds of millions of dollars in funding at a $3 billion valuation.

The company has garnered headlines this year for Pathak's claims that his robots could soon take on battlefield tasks in Ukraine or patrol the U.S. border with Mexico. Broadcast interviews and articles about Foundation also include claims that the company has won $24 million in Pentagon contracts.

"What we're about to unveil in the next couple of months [is] going to be the strongest humanoid robot that exists anywhere in the world, including China," Pathak said in an April television interview.

That April interview introduced Eric Trump, the son of the U.S. president, as a Foundation investor and chief strategy advisor. It is unclear how large his stake is.

Eric Trump and his brother Donald Trump Jr. have invested in an array of defense firms, including makers of military drones and autonomous weapons. A July Washington Post investigation found that startups backed by investment vehicles linked to the brothers had secured at least $3.2 billion in government business.

"We have to win robotics in the United States of America," Eric Trump said in April. "When you go up and interact with these robots ... they fist bump you, they high-five you, they follow your commands."

Eric Trump's involvement in Foundation drew the swift ire of Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., who asked whether Foundation won U.S. defense contracts because of nepotism.

"Eric Trump went on FOX to brag about winning ANOTHER multimillion-dollar contract from the Trump administration," Warren said in an April 23 social media post. "This looks like corruption in plain sight."

Eric Trump didn't respond to requests for comment. His spokesperson recently told the Financial Times that Eric Trump had "no involvement in Foundation's management, operations, strategic decisions or commercial activities, including the solicitation, negotiation, procurement or execution of any government contracts."

While the Eric Trump announcement created speculation within Foundation that a Democratic-led Congress may look to investigate the company, Foundation itself didn't actually win the $24 million in contracts, despite that assertion being widely reported across news outlets.

Instead, it acquired Florida-based Boardwalk Robotics in late 2024, and government research contracts won by that firm account for the claim, said a person with knowledge of the matter. Foundation used Boardwalk's robot Alex as the basis for its first robot, the Phantom MK-1, this person said.

Foundation's deal for Boardwalk included performance milestones for the robot that haven't been reached, said a separate person with direct knowledge of the deal.

In 2024, CNBC reported that letters Pathak sent to prospective investors cited an imminent investment and $300 million purchase order from General Motors, claims the automaker rejected.

Foundation co-founder Mike LeBlanc said at the time he was "embarrassed" about the marketing materials.

Insiders fret that Foundation could soon fold if Pathak doesn't secure fresh funding. Pathak has been seeking to raise hundreds of millions of dollars at a $3 billion valuation since at least January.

Pathak is Foundation's sole board member, a structure that has raised concerns among prospective investors about corporate governance, said the people familiar with the matter. He also owns most of the firm's shares.

That setup allowed Pathak to borrow $4.6 million last year, which he called a short-term loan at the time, the people said. Existing investors are aware of the loan through the company's financial disclosures, the people said.

"This is what happens in a company with no governance," said one of the people. "Are you just going to let it ride into the ground, and you ride off into the sunset?"

Foundation also ran out of cash for payroll in August, though employees only had to wait about two days to get paid after the company secured a bridge loan from an existing investor, the people said.

Pathak told employees this week in a memo that he was working on sourcing money to pay back wages.

The company was founded in April 2024 by Pathak, Tribe Capital CEO Arjun Sethi and LeBlanc, co-founder of Cobalt Robotics, a maker of autonomous security guards, according to a fundraising pitch at the time.

Foundation went on to hire dozens of robotics engineers and artificial intelligence experts, managing to lure some from Tesla and Amazon-owned autonomous vehicle company Zoox.

Before Foundation, Pathak was known as the founder of Synapse, a kind of middleman that enabled fintech firms like Mercury, Dave and YieldStreet to offer savings accounts and credit cards with the help of small community banks.

Synapse was a behind-the-scenes player until its collapse left more than 100,000 Americans with a combined $265 million in deposits locked out of their Federal Deposit Insurance Corp.-backed accounts.

While some customers received partial payments during Synapse's bankruptcy proceedings, thousands remain without access to their money after more than two years. A bankruptcy trustee, the former FDIC Chair Jelena McWilliams, said as much as $96 million in customer funds went missing.

The bankruptcy proceedings concluded last year without uncovering where the funds went; Pathak blamed a banking partner named Evolve, and Evolve blamed Synapse for the mess. U.S. regulators didn't intervene until the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau sued Synapse last year, opening up a $55 million fund for customer relief.

Pathak withdrew $320,000 in loans from Synapse as it spiraled toward bankruptcy in 2024 amid disputes over unmatched ledgers, according to the consultant and independent journalist Jason Mikula.

Federal prosecutors convened a grand jury to investigate possible criminal misconduct at Synapse, The Wall Street Journal reported last year. The Journal report didn't mention Pathak specifically.

The controversy didn't dissuade Eric Trump from investing in Foundation and Pathak, who he called a friend in the April broadcast interview.

"I walked into his factory, and I couldn't believe what I saw," Eric Trump said. "I'm telling you, he's doing a phenomenal job."

#119. Rivian, Lucid, Scout CEOs aren’t counting on Democrats to restore EV federal support after midterm wins

Leaders of several U.S. electric vehicle manufacturers said they're not counting on increased federal support for their businesses once Democrats gain control of Congress in January, but they said they'll gladly take it if it does happen.

That was the overall sentiment from CEOs of Rivian Automotive, Lucid Group and EV startup Scout Motors during interviews with CNBC after Democrats' historic midterm election wins.

"We've honestly constructed Scout from day one to be in an environment where we're not going to count on, let's say, legislation or count on things that may or may not be there," Scout CEO Scott Keogh, an automotive veteran, told CNBC on Wednesday.

EV makers have had to contend with flip-flopping support from Washington over the years. The Trump administration has ended or rolled back support for climate change initiatives and EVs that were supported by the Biden administration, including an up to $7,500 customer incentive for purchasing an EV.

Legacy automakers such as General Motors and EV startups alike had counted on such credits to help them boost their sales and assist in electric vehicle profitability. But now they don't have that same level of federal assistance.

Rivian CEO RJ Scaringe said it's his hope that the Democratic-controlled Congress and Republican President Donald Trump can find middle ground to make progress for the EV industry, but Rivian's not counting on that.

"We try not to plan for those sort of things. The reality is it's probably going to have less of an impact than we think," Scaringe said Nov. 6 following the election results. "I think we're going to actually have a lot of challenges getting things done."

Fellow EV manufacturer Lucid, which is backed by Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, is taking a similar approach.

"You know, they say, 'Plan for the worst and … hope for the best.' That's all we can say," Lucid CEO Silvio Napoli told CNBC on Monday. "Anything positive, we'll take it."

The lack of expected support comes as Democrats seem to be more focused on hot topics such as healthcare and data centers rather than climate change and support for alternative energy technologies such as EVs.

CNBC previously reported that House Democrats plan to bring to the floor bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law last year. They also plan to focus on oversight of the Trump administration and affordability.

Rachel McCleery, executive director of the nonprofit policy advisory Coalition for Reimagined Mobility at SAFE, said she believes affordability concerns also could include the cost of vehicles, including EVs.

"I fully expect there will be legislative debates over what to do moving forward, including on EV and EV supply chain incentives," McCleery, a former U.S. Department of Treasury senior advisor and Ford Motor policy director, said. "Bringing down the cost of vehicles for U.S. consumers is top of mind for Democratic policymakers, and electric vehicles are a critical aspect of this agenda because of the multiple challenges greater EV adoption solves for."

Scout's Keogh said if legislators decide to offer any type of support, he would prefer it to be for EV production, which could then have a trickle-down effect on vehicle pricing and help companies amid inflated costs.

"I always prefer it on the production side," Keogh said. "Anything that can drive down production cost is going to, of course, get passed along to the customer."

Affordability has been a concern for EV adoption as the vehicles typically cost more than traditional models with internal combustion engines.

Sales of EVs spiked last year ahead of the Trump administration ending the up to $7,500 incentive after September 2025, but have since come down from that high.

Cox Automotive reports the average price for a new electric vehicle as of September was more than $54,800, according to the most recent available data. That compared to the average overall industry at just over $51,100 last month.

"Ultimately, incentives can help accelerate adoption, but the industry can't build its long-term strategy around them," Stephanie Valdez Streaty, Cox director of industry insight, said Friday. "The goal has to be making EVs affordable and compelling enough that they make sense for more consumers with or without an incentive."

#118. Aged 16-24 and looking for work? Use our look-up to find out which areas have the most jobs

If you're one of the nearly one million people aged 16-24 not in education, employment or training - otherwise known as "Neet" - then you might be wondering where to start.

Under-25s should be guaranteed job placements or training to help them find permanent work, a major review aimed at cutting the number of Neets will say.

But how many jobs are there where you live and in which sectors are you most likely to find an opening?

There is no official measure of how many vacancies are suitable for young people, but across the whole economy there were 691,000 jobs available in the July to September period this year, which is the lowest number since early 2021.

The Covid pandemic led to huge swings in the number of job vacancies available as companies reacted to shutdowns and then reopened as restrictions were lifted.

Stripping out the fluctuations seen during the pandemic, the number of vacancies is now at its lowest level for 12 years.

Apprenticeships have often been a way for people to get their first taste of employment.

In England the number of people starting apprenticeships has risen in recent years, although this has been driven by an increase in those aged 25 and over after schemes for older workers were fully rolled out in 2008.

The number of people aged 24 and under starting apprenticeships in England in 2025 was 172,230, down from a recent peak of 291,300 in 2012.

Where you live will often have a big impact on your ability to find a job.

We have used online job adverts to help identify the number of vacancies suitable for young people in the area they live, relative to the size of the local population.

The Institute for Public Policy Research (IPPR) think tank used AI to help identify the jobs, which were broadly defined as roles requiring relatively low experience or education.

According to its analysis, South Oxfordshire had 387 suitable vacancies per 1,000 18 to 24-year-olds in the area - the most of any local authority. Rotherham had the least, at 53 per 1,000.

Use this tool to check out the situation in your area. The data for London is only available for the region as a whole and is not broken down by individual councils.

Even if there are jobs available in your area, you might not find one in the sector you want to work in.

The sector with the highest average number of entry-level vacancies in the year to October was teaching, with most of those jobs being for teaching assistants, learning support and cover supervisor roles, according to jobs website Adzuna.

The next largest was hospitality and catering, traditionally an area where younger people get their first experience of work.

Adzuna, a major online jobs website, includes adverts for full-time roles marked as entry-level, those asking for up to two years' experience, graduate roles, internships and apprenticeships.

Accounting and finance had the third-highest number of job openings.

Figures on salaries for vacancies can be patchy because many employers do not include them in job adverts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham recently urged businesses to start publishing salaries ahead of plans to make pay details compulsory in adverts.

Only about 40% of Adzuna's jobs data for the year to October stated a salary, although that figure varies widely by sector.

Here are the average salaries for jobs in the sectors with the largest number of entry-level jobs, according to Adzuna. The highest average salary is Â£30,530 in engineering, while social care has the lowest at Â£25,880.

#116. California sues OpenAI over Hugging Face attack

California Attorney General Rob Bonta sued OpenAI on Monday, alleging the ChatGPT-maker misled and endangered the public by developing artificial intelligence (AI) models without proper safeguards.

In the lawsuit, Bonta cited the July 2026 attack on the AI platform Hugging Face by experimental OpenAI agents which accessed the internet to breach that company's internal systems.

Bonta claimed OpenAI created a public nuisance and violated the state's unfair competition laws.

OpenAI spokesperson Drew Pusateri told the BBC in a statement that Bonta's lawsuit "appears to single out OpenAI over a challenge that's been identified by every major AI lab".

Other major AI developers including Anthropic, Meta, and Google have also said their models have staged similar attacks.

But Bonta's lawsuit, filed in San Francisco, where the company is headquartered, appears to be the first such suit filed by a US state.

Warning shot or publicity stunt - how worried should we be about the OpenAI hack?

OpenAI says its rogue AI tried to hack other companies

Hundreds of OpenAI agents coordinated the Hugging Face attack over two days even though they were supposed to be contained in a safe testing environment.

The incident drew added scrutiny because the agents went as far as attempting to cover their tracks.

Bonta announced an investigation into the incident in September, and served an investigative subpoena on OpenAI as part of its probe.

"The company knowingly tested a dangerous product without proper oversight or safety procedures and misled the public about it - this disregard for public safety violated California law," Bonta said in a statement on Monday.

OpenAI said it has been transparent and responsive to the Attorney General's office.

"We take misalignment seriously, and we have engaged - and will continue to engage - constructively with the Attorney General's office to share information and advance strong, practical safety standards for the entire industry," the company said.

The company added that, since the Hugging Face incident, it has worked to understand and address "misaligned model activity" which industry figures often refer to as rogue agents.

Nathan Calvin, chief strategy officer at AI safety campaign group Encode AI, said the lawsuit "recognises something we at Encode have long raised alarms about".

"OpenAI and other companies developing frontier AI systems have safety and security practices that are insufficient to protect the public from risks that could be catastrophic," he said.

Unexpected chat between OpenAI agents led to Hugging Face hack

#115. Big-ticket holiday items may be harder to find as shipping costs rise

This holiday season, big-ticket items are expected to be in short supply with diesel prices near records, trucking rates close to a post-pandemic level and carrying costs hitting multidecade highs. All of that is forcing many retailers to rethink their holiday inventory strategy.

"We are going to have some of the lowest inventory levels on some of these big-ticket items we have seen in history," said Deborah Weinswig, CEO of retail analytics firm Coresight Research.

Weinswig said retailers she is working with are holding less inventory of big-screen TVs, grills, large consumer appliances like washers and dryers, and other items that take either big amounts of space or cost a lot of money to hold.

"It's freight getting it from there to here; it's carrying costs; then it's just the trucking to get it into the stores," Weinswig said. "We're talking three different pieces right here and each is up significantly year on year."

The surge pricing for supply chain services is being caused by domestic and international policy.

According to Evercore ISI, the rates for general trucking have increased more than 47% year over year during the week of Nov. 2.

Industry experts say a large factor has been a crackdown by the federal government on non-citizen truck drivers and non-U.S.-based truck drivers operating in the U.S. That has created a capacity crunch that has raised rates and is expected to continue to create upward pressure.

At the same time, fuel surcharges are spiking.

Most transportation providers charge customers an extra fuel surcharge to offset changes in the cost of diesel fuel. That surcharge has spiked 64% year over year, according to Evercore ISI data, as diesel prices have hit new record highs due in part to the U.S.-Israel war with Iran.

And inventory holding costs — or the total expense of storing unsold goods, which could include the costs for capital, service, storage and other risk factors — are closely tied to the yield on the 10-year Treasury note, which exceeded 5% in October and remains at multiyear highs in November.

Collectively, the higher costs could mean bigger discounts for shoppers willing to wait, according to Bill Catania, CEO of logistics technology company OneRail, which powers same-day delivery for companies such as FedEx and Lowe's.

"You're going to see more discount and price promotions as we get closer to the holiday to liquidate inventory, so they're not having to transfer it or dispose of it or position it some other way," Catania said.

Retailers are also facing a conundrum in their holiday inventory strategy when it comes to shipping costs versus opportunity costs. In other words, is it worth it to stock up now, or wait it out in hopes that shipping rates will come down some but potentially miss out on having the right product mix?

It's leading to a wait-and-see approach for stocking shelves after early holiday sales in October, executives said.

UPS CEO Carol Tomé said on the company's earnings call on Oct. 27 that it's "just starting to get peak season forecasts from our customers."

Similarly, Derek Leathers, CEO of Werner Enterprises, a trucker for big-box stores and major retailers, said retailers are cautious.

"When rates are higher and the cost of capital is more expensive, you are going to have a much more intense focus on not having excess inventory," Leathers said.

Still, 2026 holiday sales are expected to top $1 trillion as inflation drives growth and customer spending is expected to remain healthy, according to Bain & Company's annual forecast. Leathers said even with the surge in trucking and shipping rates, he's seeing that retailers are willing to pay up to stock up.

"The bigger concern right now is wanting to make sure they have their capacity locked in. There is way more dialogue around the assurance of having a truck or whatever mode that needs to be secured," Leathers said. "Not saying they are price insensitive, because they are certainly always concerned about price. But you can't sell it if it's not on the shelf."

ShipMatrix, a data analytics firm for the logistics industry, forecasts 2.4 billion packages will be delivered during the 2026 holiday peak, a 4% increase year over year.

It's an increase that will likely lead retailers to increasingly use gig delivery, especially later in the holiday peak. Gig workers allow retailers and delivery companies to adjust to changes in order flow and accommodate surges of last-minute shopping without spending time and money onboarding new team members.

"Gig economy delivery is great. You know, it fulfills fast, but there's better unit economics out there," Catania said.

Industry experts said they also expect to see more retailers use drones for last-mile delivery for smaller items.

A 2024 PwC study forecast that the cost of drone delivery would "drop by more than 70% over the next 10 years, which will not only match traditional last-mile delivery costs, but surpass them and create an inflection point for the industry."

Amit Regev, CEO of drone company Flytrex, said the company is in active talks with retailers about using drones for holiday deliveries.

"The expectation now is becoming so much more instant," Regev said.

Retailers have been increasing their use of drones. Lowe's in September said it was launching a drone delivery service in partnership with DoorDash and Alphabet's Wing. Amazon earlier this year said it was aiming to offer drone deliveries in nearly 500 U.S. cities and towns by the end of the year.

Flytrex currently delivers for Walmart and a number of restaurants including Little Caesars and Papa John's.

"Customers are looking for that instant gratification," Regev said. "So if it takes too long, they might not want to order with you. And from what we are seeing that's a stronger incentive for them to try and test or deploy deliveries using drones."
