#114. Warren Buffett donates $5.5 billion, says kindness 'compounds' in Thanksgiving letter

Billionaire Warren Buffett donated Berkshire Hathaway shares worth about $5.5 billion to four philanthropic foundations, stepping up his annual charitable giving from the prior year.

Buffett, 96, converted 6,150 Class A Berkshire shares into 9,225,000 Class B shares and gave 3,690,000 of those shares to The Susan Thompson Buffett Foundation, and 1,845,000 shares each to The Sherwood Foundation, The Howard G. Buffett Foundation and NoVo Foundation — the foundations run by his three children, Susie, Howard and Peter Buffett.

Following the donations, the chairman emeritus of Berkshire still owns 182,140 Class A shares — roughly $162 billion.

"Last Thanksgiving I wrote that kindness is costless but also priceless. A year later, I would only add that it compounds, and at a better rate than anything Charlie and I ever bought," wrote Buffett, who was succeeded as chairman in September by his son Howard, in his annual Thanksgiving message.

The letter was much shorter than his message last Thanksgiving. Charlie Munger, former vice chairman of Berkshire, passed away in November 2023.

Berkshire shares have been on a tear recently, up more than 19% since the start of the fourth quarter and ending last week with four consecutive record high closes. That compares with the S&P 500's decline of 1.2% so far for the fourth quarter. Berkshire has been seen as a safe haven as investors have worried about the artificial intelligence buildout, corporate drama and geopolitical risk.

The shares are now outperforming the S&P 500 for the year by about 7.5 percentage points.

In his letter, Buffett reiterated that all of his Berkshire shares eventually will be donated to the four foundations, with the goal being to increase the size of the grants each year.

Buffett wrote that the gifts were a signal of his confidence not only in his three children, but also in Berkshire CEO Greg Abel:

"My children have given thoughtfully to worthy causes for decades, and I trust 100% both their abilities and their fidelity. ... You heard plenty from me in September about my confidence in Berkshire. That confidence is as strong as ever," he said.

Referring to Munger and himself, Buffett added, "Both of us received beyond measurement costless benefits from a few teachers such as Ben Graham, Joe Rosenfield and Tom Murphy. Because of this we not only prospered mightily but were able to pass along many of the benefits we received."

"Warren continues to share his wisdom with us all during this holiday season. His example reflects the kindness he encourages in all of us," said CEO Abel of the letter.

Buffett closes his message with this advice: "This Thanksgiving, thank one of those selfless people in your own life. Better yet, be a benefactor for someone else."

#112. 'Complete system reset' needed to get 440,000 young people into work, says major review

Sweeping reforms are needed to help 440,000 more young people into work, according to former minister Alan Milburn.

Almost one million young people aged 16 to 24 in the UK are not in education, employment or training - or "Neet" - a current rate of 13%, which Milburn wants to fall to around 5% within a decade.

His government-commissioned review, due to be published in full on Tuesday, calls for ministers to intervene from early childhood through to adulthood, funded in part by radical changes to benefits for young adults.

But Helen Whately, Conservative shadow work and pensions secretary, said Milburn's proposals were in parts "too cautious" and too reliant on the "expensive hand of the state".

Many of the review's proposed changes are specific to England, with some welfare and tax measures applying across all home nations.

Milburn, a former Labour health secretary, says extensive change is needed to avoid a "lost generation", and address what he describes as a "moral" crisis.

The review says that, in 2024/25, for every Â£1 spent helping young people into work or education, Â£25 was spent on youth benefits.

By 2036, it says the aim should be for that ratio to be Â£2 on benefits for every Â£1 on work support.

In order to make change of this scale over the next decade, the review will call for:

A new target of 90% of children to be school-ready with basic life skills by five years old (this figure is now 68% in England, with a current government target of reaching 75% by 2028)

More emphasis on work skills in the school curriculum

Instant access to mental health support without diagnosis

A doubling of the number of young apprentices

Incentives for employers, such as lowering the National Insurance rate for young workers

At the heart of the changes recommended by Milburn would be a legal entitlement to support, provided through a new "Youth Opportunity Service" in England.

The service would put a young person in touch with a named adviser to help to secure them an apprenticeship, college place, supported employment or a job within four months.

To pay for these reforms, young adults would no longer be eligible for the health top-up of Universal Credit, which provides extra money for people whose health conditions limit their capacity to work, or Personal Independence Payment (Pip), which helps with the extra living costs of having a long-term health condition or disability.

The review's key welfare measures, some of which have already been announced, suggest replacing these benefits for under-25s with a new Youth Allowance at the current base rate of Universal Credit for young people, and a new Additional Cost Allowance to replace Pip.

The minority of young people who are unable to ever work would be supported through a new Severe and Complex Needs Insurance.

Kat, 21, from Devon, currently gets Universal Credit with the health top-up, so is potentially at risk of receiving a much lower level of benefits under the proposed new system.

She has fluctuating physical and mental health problems, including occasional seizures caused by functional neurological disorder, which recently left her needing crutches.

Kat says her health issues limit her ability to find work

Kat says she "really wants to work", but says she has to rely on welfare support as she has been unable to find a job where she can be employed in a "safe way" because of her health.

Milburn, a Labour MP from 1992 to 2010, says many young people are desperate for opportunity, but that many get knocked back repeatedly.

Josh, 19, has spent three years looking for work while on Universal Credit with the support of youth employment charity Spear.

Although they help him with applications to prospective employers, Josh says "none of them are getting back, and if they do they say you've not got the spot".

Grace Coates, who manages Spear St Helens, says she wants to see "more openness to employing people with limited experience".

Josh has been looking for work for three years

Milburn's review will suggest gradually lowering the rate of National Insurance which employers pay for young adult employees to make hiring and keeping them more attractive.

The existing Youth Jobs Grant, where employers receive Â£3,000 for hiring a young person who has been looking for work for six months, would be retained under the recommendations.

So would the existing Jobs Guarantee, in which the government underwrites the cost of a paid six-month job.

The government has recently announced that young adults will be eligible for the guarantee after 12 months of being on Universal Credit and looking for work, rather than the current 18 months.

The Institute for Fiscal Studies has said reforms will have a greater chance of success if support for young people is balanced with incentives for businesses to hire them.

It said that while cutting benefit entitlements would reduce the cash young people receive immediately, it might take longer for an increase in employment to make up for that for some young adults.

Responding to the Milburn review proposals, the Conservatives' Whately said: "Where Milburn gets it right, we'll back him," but added that the review "fails to bring down the welfare bill, proposes billions more in government spending and won't make the slightest difference for young people stuck on welfare now".

Liberal Democrat deputy leader Daisy Cooper said cutting national insurance for young workers would "only partly undo the damage of Labour's job tax".

She said her party's plans would "shut off the Neets pipeline at source, with 100 hours of real work experience before 18 and a careers coach for every young person".

Reform UK's shadow education secretary Suella Braverman said the 10-year wait to see changes from Milburn's proposals "would be consigning another generation to the scrap heap".

"Reform UK would act now to axe Labour's jobs tax, double the number of apprenticeships and end university scam courses that laden students with debt," she said.

For the Greens, deputy leader Mothin Ali said Milburn's proposals announced so far were "extremely concerning", adding: "There is an alternative to threatening vulnerable people with benefits they need, an alternative to unsuitable short-term work."

#111. Kalshi plans to launch stock trading soon

Leading prediction market platform Kalshi will offer stock trading within the next few months, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC.

The platform's move to offer equity trading is the boldest so far in its ongoing push into traditional financial markets and comes amid an escalating fight by states and lawmakers against its sports trading business. Sports contracts continue to account for a large share of trading on the platform.

In September, the platform introduced perpetual futures tied to precious metals such as gold and silver after it had originally filed in July to offer beyond prediction market event contracts with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, the federal regulator overseeing derivatives contracts. Perpetual futures are futures-style contracts that have no expiration date.

Kalshi also received approval to list perpetual futures tied to cryptocurrencies in late May.

The prediction market's stock trading offering was enabled by its purchase of broker-dealer FB Securities in July, the source said. FB Securities was founded in Delaware in 2024 and became registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission in December 2025. The broker did not respond to a request for comment.

The Wall Street Journal first reported that Kalshi plans to introduce stock trading.

Shares of Robinhood, which offers some prediction markets alongside equity trading, fell on Monday, though the stock was already lower amid a broader market sell-off. Robinhood closed down by 7.8%.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#109. Novo says Wegovy injection lowers BMI and obesity rates in children in trial

Novo on Monday released full results from a late-stage trial showing its blockbuster Wegovy injection helped children ages 6 to under 12 with obesity shed weight, significantly lowering their body mass index.

Wegovy reduced BMI by an estimated 19.7 percentage points on average compared with a placebo at 68 weeks, with BMI falling 17.4% among children receiving the drug and rising 2.2% among those receiving a placebo. Those results assumed patients stayed on treatment during the trial.

When evaluating all children in the study, including those who discontinued treatment, Wegovy lowered BMI by an estimated average of 16.2 percentage points more than the placebo at 68 weeks. All 167 kids in the study received lifestyle modifications throughout the trial, including a ​reduced-calorie diet and increased physical activity.

The detailed results mark an encouraging step forward in treating childhood obesity — a chronic disease that can carry serious cardiovascular, metabolic and psychosocial consequences but has had few treatment options for younger children. When left unaddressed, childhood obesity can extend into adulthood, bringing earlier and more severe health complications.

"This is a very significant disease burden in this very young patient population," said Dr. Andrea Traina, Novo's medical director lead of obesity.

In 2025, the disease affected an estimated roughly 177 million children and teens ages 5 to 19 worldwide, according to the World Obesity Federation. That figure is projected to rise to 228 million by 2040.

While Wegovy is already approved to treat obesity in children ages 12 and older, there are currently no approved medications for those under 12. The latest results offer evidence that Wegovy can potentially fill that treatment gap, safely and effectively reducing the severity of the disease in children as young as 6.

In Novo's trial, 85% of children started with moderate to severe obesity, which corresponds to BMI thresholds of 35 and above and 40 or higher, respectively. Nearly half already had at least one obesity-related complication, such as high blood pressure, high cholesterol or sleep apnea.

But more than 40% of children treated with Wegovy were no longer classified as having obesity after 68 weeks of the trial, assuming patients stayed on treatment. Novo initially shared that result in September.

"We are driving towards control of this chronic disease in that patient population when they are still so young and at such high risk for more advanced and accelerated disease progression because they developed it so early," Traina said.

Novo said Wegovy's overall safety and tolerability among children — how well patients managed the drug — were consistent with those seen in ​trials ⁠with adults. The company added that it observed no safety concerns related to growth or pubertal development in the kids.

Still, the two-year study does not establish whether treating obesity earlier can change its long-term trajectory, or what treatment and maintenance might look like as children grow into adulthood, Traina said.

"That is something that is still an evidence gap," she said, pointing to the potential of future research.

#104. 'We slept here': Flight delays linger as airlines recover from ground stop, but schedule improves Monday

U.S. flights delays eased on Monday as airlines scrambled to get back on track after the Federal Aviation Administration grounded departures nationwide for more than four hours on Friday.

Nearly 300 U.S. flights were canceled and close to 1,000 were delayed Monday, a fraction of Sunday's disruptions, according to FlightAware data.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy over the weekend tied the issue that prompted officials to call for the ground stop to a router in southern Arkansas and work to update copper wires to a fiber-optic system overseen by L3Harris Technologies. FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford said the upgrades were part of "routine" maintenance to switch over to fiber optic.

Officials ordered the ground stop before 4 p.m. ET on Friday and it lasted until after 8 p.m. ET, marking the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Hundreds of thousands of travelers were stranded and about 18,000 U.S. flights were canceled from Friday through Monday morning, according to FlightAware.

Over that period, American Airlines had the most cancellations with 5,367 flights, close to 22% of its schedule, followed by Delta Air Lines with 3,434 cancellations, or 17% of its schedule, according to aviation data firm Cirium. Southwest Airlines and United Airlines each canceled more than 16% of their schedules. Bad weather on Thursday and Friday had also contributed to disruptions before the ground stop.

On Sunday, disruptions lingered but were starting to improve.

"We slept here," said Beth Martin, who was trying to travel home to Birmingham, Alabama, from New York's LaGuardia Airport on Sunday at the end of her daughter's birthday trip. The two were booked for Saturday night, and when delay announcements started rolling in from Delta, they decided to make good use of the extra time: After strolling through Central Park, they went window shopping on Fifth Avenue.

But when the flight was canceled, the standby list for a backup flight looked grim and their original midtown hotel was charging about $700 for an additional night, the two slept at LaGuardia on Saturday. She said they found wheelchairs to sleep in outside security.

Airlines waived change fees and fare differences and offered pilots and flight attendants extra pay to ease the delays.

Customers told CNBC they were booked on several flights before getting on board.

Justine Fritz, who works in sales for a consumer goods company, was in New York for a business event on Friday. She tried five different flights before getting on one to Houston, then flew four hours out of her way to get back to Minneapolis after her Saturday flight was canceled. Fritz said she had so many boarding passes that the Transportation Security Administration wouldn't let her through security at first.

"It's been bananas," she told CNBC at the gate. Fritz said she made the best of it by shopping and getting a haircut, but she's still disappointed.

"I'm missing the first hour of the Vikings game," she said.

Martin said her trip home with her daughter will take even longer. Delta booked them to Memphis, and her husband will drive the four hours from Birmingham to pick them up.

Officials are investigating the root cause that led to what the FAA described as "instability" in data feeds. Duffy said Sunday that two routers, the original one detected as a problem in southern Arkansas, and another in Amarillo, Texas, are in an L3Harris lab in Florida.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," L3Harris said in a statement Saturday afternoon.

President Donald Trump said Monday he didn't "take any responsibility" for the ground stop.

Shortly after the ground stop, Duffy said he was asking Congress for as much as $50 billion for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system. A day later, Democratic lawmakers pushed back, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress' approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

"Our committee, on behalf of the flying public, will hold the administration accountable for what happened to make sure this chaos does not happen again," said Reps. Rick Larsen, D.-Wash., ranking member of the House Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure, and Andre Carson, D.-Ind., ranking member of the aviation subcommittee, in a joint statement.

#103. Berkshire Hathaway raised its stake in Delta in the third quarter. It also trimmed a key bank holding

Berkshire Hathaway made several moves in the stock market during the third quarter, adding to key positions and trimming others.

The Omaha-based conglomerate, now led by CEO Greg Abel, increased its Delta Air Lines stake by 12%, scooping up 7.13 million shares. The position was worth $5.37 billion at the end of the third quarter.

Abel succeeded legendary investor Warren Buffett, 96, at the start of this year. Buffett continues to control roughly 13% of Berkshire common stock, Reuters said Monday, after the former CEO donated $5.5 billion to nonprofit foundations.

Berkshire boosted its stake in Delta as the Atlanta-based carrier's shares tumbled 11% in the third quarter, hurt by rising jet fuel prices due to the U.S.-Iran war that pressured the entire airline industry. That marked the stock's worst quarterly performance since the first quarter of 2025, when it plunged 28%. Delta last month also cut its full-year earnings forecast, citing a surge in fuel costs.

To be sure, buying that dip has been a winning investment thus far. Delta is up 3.8% so far in the fourth quarter, while the S&P 500 has dropped 3.1%. Shares are also up nearly 15% over the past month.

Berkshire also trimmed its holding in Bank of America by nearly 6%, selling more than $1.6 billion worth of stock.

BofA was under pressure in the third quarter, falling more than 4%, as interest rates surged alongside stubbornly high inflation and expectations of tighter Federal Reserve monetary policy. At one point in September the 30-year Treasury bond yield surged above 5.6% to its highest level since 2002.

Despite the share sale, Bank of America remains a top Berkshire Hathaway holding, with the conglomerate's stake valued at $24.8 billion at the end of the third quarter.

Berkshire also initiated a position in Bending Spoons and nearly doubled its stake in homebuilder Lennar in the latest quarter.

#99. Robert Pattinson cast as next James Bond

Robert Pattinson has been cast as the next James Bond in the upcoming film from director Denis Villeneuve. It will be the 26th James Bond film and the first since Amazon MGM took over the iconic franchise.

Pattinson will be the seventh actor to play the role. Monday's announcement — which came in part via an Instagram post — ends a long period of speculation about who would take over as "007" after Daniel Craig.

"James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honour of a lifetime," Pattinson said in a statement. "I couldn't be more excited about this partnership and the work ahead with this fantastic team."

Pattinson has appeared in several big-ticket films this year, including Christopher Nolan's "The Odyssey" and psychological thriller "Primetime," and he'll soon appear in "Dune: Part Three," also directed by Villeneuve. Pattinson, who rose to fame with the Twilight movie series, is also in production for Matt Reeves' "The Batman Part II," a follow-up to the 2022 film based on the DC comic.

The upcoming Bond film will have a script by Steven Knight and will be produced by Amy Pascal, known for her work on the Spider-Man franchise, and David Heyman, who produced all eight Harry Potter films.

"We were surrounded by extraordinary talent, but there can only be one 007," Villeneuve said. "I am overjoyed to welcome Robert Pattinson as our new James Bond. He has the timeless soul of 007 and brings a spirit that is completely new. I cannot wait to begin."

In 2022, Amazon bought MGM Studios, which had long produced the Bond movies, for roughly $8.45 billion. Amazon MGM then acquired creative control of the James Bond intellectual property rights in 2025, when it closed a deal with longtime producers Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson.

The film is still untitled, and Amazon MGM hasn't announced a projected release date.

#98. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: As Nike and Lululemon struggle in China, Xiaohongshu is helping local brands take off

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

While the world watched the Nvidia saga around Jensen Huang unfold, Chinese retailers quietly wrapped up the local version of Black Friday, known as Singles Day. What can foreign brands learn?

As Lululemon, On Running and other Western consumer brands have sought to expand in China, so have a crop of local brands with English names.

Monday Sleeping Club, Crying Center, AW Project, an action a day, Liegee. The list of trendy new Chinese brands has grown rapidly, despite flatlining retail sales nationwide.

"Their success rests on one shared formula," said Cynthia Zhang, analyst, Euromonitor International.

"Build a non-product asset first — credible fabric quality, an emotion, an aesthetic reputation, or proprietary fit data — then monetize it through founder-led content, Xiaohongshu-driven discovery, tight supply chains, and the quiet-luxury and Guochao [national pride] mood that prizes craftsmanship over conspicuous logos," she said.

For Liegee, one of its three founders, Cocoa Lin, told me its sales surged by roughly 160% during the latest Singles Day period to the tens of millions of yuan, equivalent to a few million U.S. dollars.

The brand launched in 2022 in Hangzhou to meet a perceived gap in clothes for early career women looking to express their personality, Lin said. She said the brand, which has over 100,000 fans for its store on Alibaba's Taobao e-commerce site, largely uses internet influencers and celebrity product seeding to generate awareness.

The brand uses Xiaohongshu, on which it has over 22,000 followers, for catalog-style photos and marketing videos. It opened a "Liegee Motel" pop-up in Shenzhen last month.

"We believe online and offline will have a closer connection in the future," Lin said in Mandarin via translation, adding that "as lifestyles become more experience-oriented, this energy can be transmitted to online."

It's a dynamic that Western brands have struggled to crack.

Nike reported a 22% sales drop in Greater China in the quarter ended Aug. 31, while Lululemon reported a 2% drop in mainland China comparable sales in the quarter ended Aug. 2.

Adidas runs at least six official Xiaohongshu accounts, with two notching well over 200,000 followers each. Nike and Lululemon each operate one official account, with over 300,000 followers each.

For a country of around 1.4 billion people, Xiaohongshu has captured a key consumer audience.

The social media app, whose layout is a mix of Instagram and Pinterest, has focused on women and young people in China's largest cities, and recent expanded into less developed parts of the country, according to Questmobile. Xiaohongshu recorded average daily users of 120 million in October, a fraction of the 422 million on Taobao and 717 million on Douyin, the report showed.

The app has also attempted to build up its own e-commerce business in order to monetize.

"RedNote [or Xiaohongshu] has been a key platform for product discovery and research, especially for female consumers shopping in the beauty, skincare, lifestyle, fashion, and health categories. Now, we're seeing more transactions within RedNote itself," said Jacob Cooke, co-founder and CEO of WPIC Marketing + Technologies. The company helps foreign brands sell in China and other parts of Asia.

Alibaba, JD.com and ByteDance's Douyin still dominated this year's Singles Day sales with a combined 1.68 trillion yuan in sales over the Oct. 7 to Nov. 11 period, according to Syntun.

What's changed more fundamentally is the mood of the Chinese consumer, including more affluent ones.

In the era of double-digit annual growth in real estate prices, consumers pursued conspicuous consumption to meet "aspirational" desires, Christine Peng, head of Greater China consumer research, UBS, told reporters last week.

"Now those needs no longer exist," she said in Mandarin, translated by CNBC.

"The rise of these trendy brands reflects how consumers now seek to define their own identities and pursue personal enjoyment, while still remaining mindful of price and quality," Peng said.

She said wealthy consumers are also embracing domestic brands, and no longer just buying traditional luxury brands.

Alice Huang tied to Nvidia's biggest Chinese competitor

Alice Huang has owned stakes in companies that have benefited Huawei, private intelligence firm Tesari shared exclusively with CNBC. Some in Beijing worry the Jensen Huang affair will negatively impact U.S.-China relations.

Exports in October jumped by 35.2%, while imports soared 41.6%, both beating Reuters' estimates. But industrial output, retail sales and investment figures for October all missed estimates. China and the U.S. discussed tariffs and agricultural trade ahead of Xi-Trump meeting, the Chinese Commerce Ministry said Saturday.

Alibaba announces plans to list T-Head, its Nvidia chip rival

The Chinese tech company disclosed the Hong Kong Stock Exchange will allow it to spin off its semiconductor subsidiary T-Head.

Nov. 18 - 19: APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting in Shenzhen

#97. JPMorgan Chase says temporary software bug is affecting consumer banking

JPMorgan Chase reported a "technical issue" that was affecting some consumer banking services Monday.

The bank said a "temporary issue related to a software bug in on-premise hardware systems" was causing some customer transactions to take longer than usual, though it said customers can still access their accounts and funds.

"There is no evidence it is cyber-related," a JPMorgan spokesperson said in a statement to CNBC.

"This is not a systemic issue — other banks may have been impacted by slowness in transactions only to the extent that they were a party to a transaction with us," the spokesperson said. "We're working diligently to resolve this by the end of the day today."

CNBC's Eamon Javers reported there was no indication of bad actors involved.

Still, other major banks were listed in outage reports as transactions between Chase and other institutions appeared to get held up. Users reported errors as early as 11:30 a.m. ET.

A representative for U.S. Bank said that company was "not directly impacted. However, clients attempting to send or receive Zelle payments with another financial institution may experience transaction delays."

Zelle is the peer-to-peer payments system backed by many of the largest U.S. banks, including JPMorgan.

The outage reports come days after separate, widespread issues with Microsoft Outlook and communication systems used by air traffic control centers.

#94. Aschenbrenner’s Situational Awareness made AI bets again before October rout

Leopold Aschenbrenner's artificial intelligence-themed hedge fund Situational Awareness, which nearly collapsed in July, loaded back up on AI stocks as it returned to the financial markets at the end of the third quarter. But all of its holdings are down big the last month and a half.

According to a new regulatory filing, as the fund got back on its feet Aschenbrenner took an equity position in Australian neocloud company SharonAI valued at more than $440 million, along with equity stakes in AI-centric plays Cleanspark, IREN, Bitdeer Technologies and Fermi. The Cleanspark stake is worth almost $39 million, while Iren and Bitdeer stakes are more than $21 million and more than $20 million, respectively. Fermi, meanwhile, has a value of more than $11 million.

The fund also owned call options at the end of September in neocloud Nebius, memory chip name Sandisk, the Roundhill Memory ETF (DRAM) and data center power play Bloom Energy. Sandisk and Nebius were key holdings that almost took out his fund over the summer.

All of these Situational Awareness holdings have been routed since Sept. 30. SharonAI is at the top of that list, seeing a 58% loss in October. It's also fallen 11% so far this month. IREN was down 41% in October, while Nebius declined 35%. Last week, Nebius plummeted more than 16% in a single day after the company issued a weak full-year forecast.

It's not known whether the hedge fund manager got out of those positions during the October AI selloff. The 13F filing is just a snapshot of what the hedge fund held at the end of the quarter.

Aschenbrenner — who launched the fund in 2024 after rising to fame that same year with his AI manifesto also entitled Situational Awareness — re-entered financial markets a bit in September, sources told CNBC at the time.

The Monday filing shows he picked up right where he left off.

The near-collapse of his hedge fund over the summer forced him to sell all of its public equity positions to Ken Griffin's Citadel. The fund had $45 billion in assets at its peak, but that tumbled to around $10 billion, consisting mostly of private stakes.

Stocked tethered to the AI trade took a hit in the most recent quarter as investors grew concerned about a slowdown in development of the technology. Those fears were spurred by comments from industry leaders such as Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman back in September, with the latter saying that the company going public this year would be "ill-advised."

Since then, pressure on the trade has only continued.

In recent weeks, investors have worried about how Democratic wins in the midterm elections could hinder data center development, whether some technology companies are using too much debt to finance the AI buildout and what role Nvidia will play in that financing with Jensen Huang now out as CEO at the company.

Losses in Nvidia as well as other AI-related stocks dragged down the three leading U.S. indexes during Monday's session as investors continued to assess the fallout of Huang's departure.

#91. United Airlines resolves system outage that affected departing flights

United Airlines on Monday said it had resolved a "widespread system outage" that briefly affected departing flights.

"We have resolved a technology issue that delayed flight departures," the airline said in a statement. "While some flights were delayed, there was no impact on flights in progress, or on United Express flights.

A United spokesperson said there was no indication that the outage was a cyberattack.

The Air Line Pilots Association, which represents pilots for United and other carriers, said in an earlier message to members seen by CNBC that the operational outage was "affecting multiple pilot facing applications" and that United could not "provide outbound flights with final weights."

United's outage came three days after a nationwide ground stop halted departures across the country for more than four hours, snarling travel and leading to widespread delays and cancellations over the weekend.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy blamed the issues that led to Friday's ground stop on a router in Arkansas that overwhelmed systems that feed radar and other data for air traffic control. Federal Aviation Administration Administrator Bryan Bedford said the upgrades were part of "routine" maintenance to switch over from copper wires to fiber optic.

There were significantly fewer flight delays and cancellations Monday than had been reported Sunday, according to FlightAware data.

There was no indication that United's outage Monday was related to the issues that caused Friday's ground stop.

— CNBC's Leslie Josephs and Phil LeBeau contributed to this report.

#90. Analysis: The Fed's chairman sees an AI revolution coming. Now he has to figure out what it means

Artificial intelligence is about to sweep through the economy. The Federal Reserve is bracing for impact.

"We are at a hinge point in the U.S. economy," Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh said at a conference he convened in San Francisco on Monday. But exactly when and how the economy could turn, and what that means for interest rates and inflation, isn't yet clear.

The meeting of Warsh's AI task force saw the new Fed chairman publicly asking the questions Americans will ultimately expect him to answer. He hinted at a few of his private views on whether white-collar workers could soon face massive job losses, but he largely left the speculation to an array of experts he has assembled to advise the Fed.

With the technology racing ahead, Warsh won't be able to put off giving his own answers much longer. AI lab Anthropic plans to price the shares for its initial public offering on Wednesday and could raise as much as $64 billion at a $1.4 trillion valuation. Vast spending from AI firms is prompting the Fed to contemplate raising interest rates in December to slow down the rate of price increases as the tech sector heats up the economy.

Meanwhile Americans are souring on some aspects of the technology. The Nov. 3 midterms saw voters enact an array of state-level measures to slow down construction of the data centers that are essential to powering AI.

Warsh moderated three out of four sessions at Monday's event and was involved in selecting the speakers. Among them was one of the few prominent Democrats who was willing to speak out against that anti-data center trend.

It is "a very troubling sign" that so many anti-data center measures passed in the midterms, Gina Raimondo said. She is a former secretary of commerce under President Joe Biden and a former governor of Rhode Island.

Stopping AI "would be very bad for America" and for the people who voted for the measures, Raimondo said.

Warsh didn't say whether he shares that view, and how it would translate into monetary policy isn't exactly clear.

The event was an unusual one for the Fed. The central bank employs a small army of Ph.D. economists who can rapidly weigh in on anything affecting the U.S. and global economies. But in keeping with his longstanding critiques of the Fed as insular and outdated, Warsh has chosen to draw expertise from outside the institution to help it figure out how to think about what AI will do to productivity and jobs.

Warsh has assembled a task force on that issue, alongside four others devoted to aspects of Fed policymaking.

The public heard Monday from one of the three official members of the task force: venture capitalist Marc Andreessen. Warsh went to Stanford University with Andreessen's wife, and the two have been longtime friends. Another task force member, Stanford economist Chad Jones, is on leave and working at Anthropic and couldn't speak during the company's blackout period ahead of the IPO. The third, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma, didn't attend the event.

Andreessen is hugely optimistic about the promise of AI as a technology, but believes the government won't let Americans experience its full effects. Heavy regulation in areas such as healthcare and education mean technology can't replace people's jobs in those sectors, he said.

"To the extent our economy is a free-market economy, then maybe you could see the, let's say, extremely rapid displacement scenarios people are talking about," Andreessen said. "But we've mostly outlawed the kind of job transition that … both the utopians the doomers talk about."

Warsh returned repeatedly to one scenario for the economy that draws on past experience. The so-called China shock unfolded after that country joined the World Trade Organization in 2001 and moved heavily into low-cost manufacturing. Americans saw prices on clothing and furniture drop rapidly, for instance, but at the cost of severe job losses. One widely cited estimate found the U.S. lost a million manufacturing jobs and 2.4 million jobs overall in the years that followed.

Warsh suggested to one of the panelists that the widespread adoption of AI might produce its own version of the China shock, this time focused on service workers.

"I don't want to hold this too tightly," Warsh said. "But if in some ways over the last generation or generation and a half, the China shock, for lack of a better word, lowered prices of goods in the United States, the way that I heard you talking about the philosophy of this new technology is it might do something for services."

In other words, AI could do, say, legal or administrative work more cheaply than a human. That could lower prices but put many people out of a job.

Warsh asked panelists whether "we should expect disinflation over the medium term, from what AI could do to services."

They largely suggested it was possible, which could mean the Fed might eventually want to lower interest rates over the long term.

But at the end of the day, it is Warsh and his fellow Fed members who will need to have conviction on the answer.

There are still many steps between the task force discussion and any kind of change in Fed policy. First, the three task force members will have to assemble their points of view into a report for Warsh sometime in the next few months. Then the Fed itself will need to debate those results and decide whether or how to use them to shape monetary policy.

But with AI growing bigger every day, the Fed may need to make up its mind sooner rather than later.

#85. Trump’s accounts traded up to $97.5 million in Nvidia as Huang gained White House access

President Donald Trump has had plenty of reason to cheer the rise of Nvidia.

The chipmaker helped propel the stock market that Trump is fond of touting. Its billionaire co-founder, Jensen Huang, became one of the president's favorite businessmen. And by the end of last year, Nvidia had become one of Trump's three largest individual stock holdings, worth between $12.5 million and $58 million, up from between $615,000 and $1.3 million a year earlier.

Since returning to the White House, Trump's investment accounts have bought or sold Nvidia shares at least 103 times — transactions worth a combined $27 million to $97.5 million, according to a new CNBC analysis of his annual and periodic financial disclosures, which were most recently updated through August.

By midday Monday, Trump's Nvidia stake, based on the value he reported holding in August, would be worth $4.5 million to $57.7 million with stock priced around $157. But the filings give broad dollar ranges for holdings rather than the number of shares, leaving his current stake unclear.

Now, Huang's resignation as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, following revelations about his relationship with a woman suspected of spying for China and reports of affairs with company employees, has put those trades back in the spotlight.

Among them was a sale of $500,001 to $1 million on May 18, the first Monday after Trump returned from a state visit to China with Huang. Five days earlier, before Huang boarded Air Force One during a refueling stop in Alaska, Secretary of State Marco Rubio had briefed Trump on intelligence concerning Huang's relationship with the suspected spy, The New York Times reported.

Trump on Monday denied being briefed. Asked about the reported briefing from Rubio, Trump said, "I haven't heard that," adding that he did not know whether the woman, Alice Huang, was a Chinese spy.

The president's financial accounts also bought smaller amounts of Nvidia shares in the days immediately before and after the May 18 sale, part of a broader trading blitz. Such flurries have defined his second term: Trump disclosed more than 21,000 trades in 2025 alone, compared with roughly 500 during his entire first term, according to CNBC's analysis.

A White House spokesperson told CNBC independent advisors manage Trump's portfolio without his input. Trump Organization spokesperson Kimberly Benza said the arrangement is designed to "avoid even the appearance of a conflict of interest." The disclosures offer no evidence Trump directed the trades or that his advisors had access to classified intelligence.

Trump has long used the stock market as a scorecard for the economy, touting its record highs — sometimes when highs have not actually been reached — despite the turbulence caused by his tariffs and the Iran war.

"Look, we have the highest stock market we've ever had," Trump told ESPN commentator and political talk show host Stephen A. Smith on Thursday when pressed about Republicans' midterm losses.

Nvidia alone accounted for about 13.5% of the S&P 500's 2026 gains through September, according to an analysis by Choate Wealth that looked at how chip stocks have driven the year's performance.

Huang's rise in Trump's Washington has been nearly as striking as Nvidia's on Wall Street. The two have met or appeared together at least 15 times since Trump returned to office, according to a CNBC review of public records and news reports. Huang in June told The Associated Press that Trump even "calls me in the middle of the night."

At Trump's Jan. 20, 2025, inauguration, several Silicon Valley titans occupied prime seats, but Huang wasn't there. The two men had never met.

That changed on Jan. 31, 2025, when Huang visited the White House, capping a bruising week for Nvidia: Chinese AI startup DeepSeek had wiped $593 billion off the company's market value in a single day, according to LSEG data cited by Reuters.

Two days before the meeting, Trump's money managers bought between $1 million and $5 million in Nvidia stock.

For Huang, much of the relationship hinged on access to China. In April 2025, the administration effectively halted sales of Nvidia's H20 chips to China, prompting a $4.5 billion charge, according to an SEC filing. Huang argued that withholding U.S. chips would only accelerate China's development of its own.

The administration eventually relented. After another meeting with Trump in July 2025, Huang announced from Beijing that Nvidia expected to resume H20 sales. A week after Trump confirmed an August 2025 deal calling for 15% of the revenue from the company's chip sales to China to go to the U.S. government, his accounts also bought between $5 million and $25 million in Nvidia stock, one of several large tech investments that day.

Huang was also becoming a fixture of Trump's international diplomacy, joining him in Saudi Arabia and the U.K. Nvidia, already a $1 million donor to Trump's inaugural fund, would pledge another $25 million to his planned White House ballroom on condition of anonymity, The Washington Post first reported.

But behind the scenes, intelligence officials first briefed Trump last fall on Huang's relationship with the woman suspected of spying for China, The New York Times reported.

Trump nevertheless continued to ease restrictions on Nvidia's business in China, announcing Dec. 8 that the company could sell its more powerful H200 chips. His accounts had sold as much as $1 million in Nvidia stock four days earlier and bought more the day after.

In the first eight months of 2026, Trump's accounts made another 38 Nvidia trades worth a combined $9.4 million to $28.3 million, according to CNBC's analysis. A disclosure released Oct. 8 — the day Trump awarded Huang the National Medal of Science — showed purchases of up to $1.6 million and sales of up to $1 million in August alone.

Trump stood by Huang as Nvidia's board weighed his future last week, urging him to fight to keep his job, the Times reported.

With Huang out and Nvidia's shares tumbling, Trump described him Thursday as a "tremendous talent" who was "going through hell" — in the same interview in which he boasted of the market's record highs.

Trump is already floating a comeback for Huang, telling reporters Monday that reinstating him "certainly isn't a bad idea." But Nvidia's board is not considering his return, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC. The person asked not to be named because they were discussing internal matters."
